Near-Optimal Space Lower Bounds for Streaming CSPs
Yumou Fei, Dor Minzer, Shuo Wang

Abstract
In a streaming constraint satisfaction problem (streaming CSP), a p p -pass algorithm receives the constraints of an instance sequentially, making p p passes over the input in a fixed order, with the goal of approximating the maximum fraction of satisfiable constraints. We show near optimal space lower bounds for streaming CSPs, improving upon prior works: 1. Fei, Minzer and Wang ( STOC 2026 ) showed that for any CSP, the basic linear program defines a threshold α LP ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}\in[0,1] such that, for any ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 , an ( α LP − ε ) (\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}-\varepsilon) -approximation can be achieved using constant passes and polylogarithmic space, whereas achieving ( α LP + ε ) (\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}+\varepsilon) -approximation requires Ω ⁡ ( n 1 / 3 / p ) \Omega(n^{1/3}/p) space. We improve this lower bound to Ω ⁡ ( n / p ) \Omega(\sqrt{n}/p) , which is nearly tight for a gap version of the problem. 2. For p = o ⁡ ( log ⁡ n ) p=o(\log n) , we further strengthen the lower bound to Ω ⁡ ( n ⋅ 2 − O ε ​ ( p ) ) \Omega(n\cdot 2^{-O_{\varepsilon}(p)}) . Combined with existing algorithmic results, this shows that α LP \alpha_{\mathrm{LP}} is not only the limit of multi-pass polylogarithmic-space algorithms, but also the limit of single-pass sublinear-space algorithms on bounded-degree instances. 3. For certain CSPs, we show that there exists α < 1 \alpha<1 such that achieving an α \alpha -approximation requires Ω ⁡ ( n / p ) \Omega(n/p) space. Our proofs are Fourier analytic, building on the techniques of Fei, Minzer and Wang ( STOC 2026 ) and the Fourier- ℓ 1 \ell_{1} -based lower bound method of Kapralov and Krachun ( STOC 2019 ).



1
Introduction

Constraint satisfaction problems (CSPs) are among the most well studied problems in theoretical computer science. Traditionally, they have been studied in the context of polynomial time computation, and by now the tractability of their decision version
[
7
,
34
]
, as well as of their approximation version
[
25
]
, are (for the most part) well understood. Over the last decade CSPs have gained significant interest in the streaming community, with the goal of understanding what approximation ratios efficient streaming algorithms can achieve. This is the subject of the current paper, and our main result is a quantitatively stronger (and nearly optimal in some sense) space lower bound for the classes of single pass and multi-pass streaming algorithms for CSPs.

1.1
Constraint Satisfaction Problems in the Streaming Model

A single-pass, space
S
streaming algorithm receives its input as a stream, and upon viewing each element it is allowed to modify its memory arbitrarily. At the end of the stream, the algorithm is supposed to output an answer.
The study of CSPs in the streaming setting started in
[
21
]
, who considered the max-cut problem. In this problem, the stream consists of the edges of some graph
G
, and the algorithm is supposed to estimate the size of the largest cut in
G
. There is a trivial
O(\log n)
space algorithm achieving approximation ratio
1/2
, and the work
[
21
]
shows that for all
\varepsilon>0
, achieving approximation ratio
1/2+\varepsilon
requires
\Omega_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{n})
memory.

The work of
[
21
]
inspired a flourish of activity in the study of the performance of streaming algorithms on optimization problems; see
[
19
,
22
,
10
,
1
,
2
,
8
,
27
,
20
,
9
,
28
,
13
,
14
,
29
,
32
,
4
]
and
[
31
,
3
,
30
]
for surveys. All of these works consider constraint satisfaction problems, which is a rich class of problems extending the max-cut problem, defined as follows.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be a parameter,
\Sigma
be a finite alphabet and
{\cal{F}}\subseteq\{f:\Sigma^{k}\rightarrow\{0,1\}\}
be a family of predicates. An instance
\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{C})
of
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
(we write
\mathcal{I}\in\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
) consists of a set of variables
\mathcal{V}
and a collection of constraints
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},\dots,C_{m})
. Each constraint
C_{i}
is specified as
((\mathsf{v}_{i,1},\dots,\mathsf{v}_{i,k}),f_{i})
, where
\mathsf{v}_{i,j}\in\mathcal{V}
is a variable for
j=1,\ldots,k
, and
f_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
is a predicate.

Given an instance
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
, the goal is to find an assignment
\tau\colon\mathcal{V}\to\Sigma
satisfying as many of the constraints as possible, i.e., maximizing

\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}(\tau)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}(\tau(\mathsf{v}_{i,1}),\ldots,\tau(\mathsf{v}_{i,k})).

The value of the instance
\mathcal{I}
is defined as
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}=\max_{\tau}\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}(\tau)
. We will sometimes discuss the degree of an instance, which is the maximum number of constraints a variable appears in.

All prior works on streaming CSPs either consider a specific problem (such as max-cut or max-directed-cut) or a class of constraint satisfaction problems. They then study the performance of a class of streaming algorithms on them (such as sketching algorithms, single-pass algorithms, mult-pass algorithms), both on the algorithmic front and the hardness front. For example, the work of
[
9
]
gave an exact characterization of the power of the class of sketching algorithms, which is a subclass of single-pass streaming algorithms.
Another example, which is most relevant to our paper, is the work of
[
22
]
, who improved the result of
[
21
]
and showed that any single-pass,
(1/2+\varepsilon)
-approximation streaming algorithm for max-cut requires
\Omega_{\varepsilon}(n)
memory. In that work
the authors show how to replace the
L_{2}
-based lower bound approach of
[
21
]
by a Fourier-
\ell_{1}
-based lower bound approach, which gives better quantitative bounds.

Multi-pass algorithms:

the main topic of this paper is multi-pass streaming algorithms. Given an instance
\mathcal{I}
of a CSP, a
p
-pass streaming algorithm gets to view the constraints of
\mathcal{I}
in the same predetermined order for
p
times, and it then must output an approximation of
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}
. A multi-pass streaming algorithm is considered efficient if both the pass complexity
p
and the space complexity are small (by which one typically means poly-logarithmic). Building on
[
33
,
28
]
, the work
[
14
]
proposed a general approximation algorithm based on a linear-programming relaxation of
\mathcal{I}
due to
[
33
]
, called
{\textsc{BasicLP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
, defined as follows. The program has variables
(x_{\mathsf{v},\sigma})_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V},\,\sigma\in\Sigma}
, thought of as specifying a probability distribution over the possible assignments to each variable
v\in\mathcal{V}
, and variables
(z_{i,b})_{i\in[m],\,b\in\Sigma^{k}}
, thought of as specifying a probability distribution over the possible assignments to each clause.

{\textsc{BasicLP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
for
\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{C})

maximize

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{b\in\Sigma^{k}}f_{i}(b)\,z_{i,b}

subject to

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}x_{\mathsf{v},\sigma}

\displaystyle=1\quad

\displaystyle\forall\,\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle\sum_{b\in\Sigma^{k}}\mathbbm{1}\{b_{j}=\sigma\}\cdot z_{i,b}

\displaystyle=x_{\mathsf{v}_{i,j},\,\sigma}\quad

\displaystyle\forall\,i\in[m],\,j\in[k],\,\sigma\in\Sigma

\displaystyle x_{\mathsf{v},\sigma}

\displaystyle\geqslant 0\quad

\displaystyle\forall\,\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V},\,\sigma\in\Sigma

\displaystyle z_{i,b}

\displaystyle\geqslant 0\quad

\displaystyle\forall\,i\in[m],\,b\in\Sigma^{k}

The objective value counts the total mass put on assignment that satisfies the constraints of
\mathcal{I}
, and the constraints enforce local consistency between the distributions described by the variables of the program. We denote by
\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
the value of
{\textsc{BasicLP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
.

The work
[
14
]
argued that for

\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathcal{F}):=\inf_{\mathcal{I}\in\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})}\frac{\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}}}

(1.1)

and any
\varepsilon>0
, a streaming algorithm with
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
many passes and
O_{\varepsilon}(\log n)
memory can
\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}
-approximate the values of
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instances.
1
1

1

Here
n
stands for the number of variables in the input instance. We additionally assume that the number of constraints is at most polynomial in
n
.
To get a matching hardness result, they showed that for any
\varepsilon>0
, a
p
-pass, space-
S
streaming algorithm achieving approximation ratio
\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathcal{F})+\varepsilon
must satisfy
pS=\Omega_{\varepsilon}(n^{1/3})
. Thus, a sharp transition in the complexity of approximation
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
occurs at
\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathcal{F})
, roughly from constant-pass/logarithmic-space, to polynomial. While this gap is very large, it is not optimal.

1.2
Main Results

The main result of this paper is a near-optimal, quantitative improvement of the result of
[
14
]
. We state it in the form of gap problems: given parameters
1\geqslant c>s\geqslant 0
,
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[c,s]
is the promise problem wherein the input instance
\mathcal{I}\in\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
is promised to either have
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}\geqslant c
or
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}\leqslant s
, and the goal is to distinguish between these two cases.

Theorem 1.2
.

Fix a nonempty instance
\mathcal{I}\in\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
. Let
s:=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}
and
c:=\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
. Then the following statements hold:

(1)

If
c<1
, then for any fixed error parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, the memory size of any
p
-pass streaming algorithm for
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[c-\varepsilon,s+\varepsilon]
is at least
\max\big\{\Omega_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{n}/p),\;n\cdot\exp(-O_{\varepsilon}(p))\big\}
.

(2)

If
c=1
, then for any fixed error parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, the memory size of any
p
-pass streaming algorithm for
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[1,s+\varepsilon]
is at least
\max\big\{\Omega_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{n}/p),\;n\cdot\exp(-O_{\varepsilon}(p))\big\}
.

Theorem

1.2
immediately implies that for general pass complexity
p
, the lower bound of
[
14
]
can be improved to
pS=\Omega_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{n})
:

Corollary 1.3
.

For any predicate family
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\Sigma^{k}\rightarrow\{0,1\}\}
and any
\varepsilon>0
, any
p
-pass streaming algorithm with
S
bits of memory achieving approximation ratio
\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathcal{F})+\varepsilon
(the threshold
\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathcal{F})
is defined in (
1.1
)) for
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
must satisfy
pS=\Omega_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{n})
.

Remark 1.4
.

The threshold
\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathcal{F})
in Corollary
1.3
is optimal due to the algorithm of
[
14
]
mentioned earlier. We note that for the specific problems of max-cut and max-directed-cut, the threshold
\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}
equals
1/2
(see
[
14
, Theorem 1.5]
). It is not clear whether the space-pass tradeoff
pS=\Omega_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{n})
in Corollary
1.3
is optimal. However, we do know that the
\Omega(\sqrt{n}/p)
lower bound in Theorem
1.2
(2) cannot be improved by more than
\mathrm{polylog}(n)
factors (see Section
1.2.2
).

For
p=o(\log n)
, the space lower bound in Theorem
1.2
becomes near-linear in
n
. In particular, for single-pass streaming algorithms, we have the following corollary:

Corollary 1.5
.

For any predicate family
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\Sigma^{k}\rightarrow\{0,1\}\}
and any
\varepsilon>0
, any single-pass streaming algorithm achieving approximation ratio
\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathcal{F})+\varepsilon
(the threshold
\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathcal{F})
is defined in (
1.1
)) for
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
must use at least
\Omega_{\varepsilon}(n)
bits of memory.

Interestingly, the threshold
\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathcal{F})
in Corollary
1.5
is also optimal for bounded-degree instances. Indeed, as observed by
[
29
]
, combining the algorithm of
[
28
, Section 5]
with
[
33
]
yields the following result:

Theorem 1.6

(
[
33
,
28
]
)
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
and any degree bound
\Delta\geqslant 1
, there is a single-pass
n^{1-\Omega_{\varepsilon,\Delta}(1)}
-space streaming algorithm that achieves
(\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathcal{F})-\varepsilon)
-approximation on
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instances with maximum degree at most
\Delta
.

1.2.1
Discussion: Approximation Resistance

A specific question that has guided prior research in streaming approximability of CSPs is
approximation resistance
. To explain this notion, we define for any predicate family
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\Sigma^{k}\rightarrow\{0,1\}\}
the threshold

\rho(\mathcal{F})=\inf_{\mathcal{I}\in\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})}\mathrm{val}_{\cal I}.

(1.2)

Clearly, the algorithm that simply outputs
\rho(\mathcal{F})
(regardless of the input) is an
\rho(\mathcal{F})
-approximation of the values of all
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instances. We say
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
is approximation-resistant against a certain class of algorithms if for any
\varepsilon>0
, no algorithm in that class can solve the gap problem
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[1-\varepsilon,\rho(\mathcal{F})+\varepsilon]
.

The following approximation-resistance result was proved by
[
9
]
.

Theorem 1.7

(
[
9
, Theorem 1.2]
)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\Sigma^{k}\rightarrow\{0,1\}\}
is a predicate family such that every predicate
f\in\mathcal{F}
supports one-wise independence (as formally defined in Section
2.1
). Then for any
\varepsilon>0
, any single-pass streaming algorithm solving
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[1,\rho(\mathcal{F})+\varepsilon]
must use
\Omega_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{n})
bits of memory.

Before this work, it was not clear whether the lower bound in Theorem
1.7
can be improved. For the specific problem of max-cut,
2
2

2

Max-cut corresponds to the predicate family
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\{0,1\}^{2}\rightarrow\{0,1\}\}
that consists of the single predicate
f
defined by
f(x_{1},x_{2})=1
if and only if
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
(which clearly supports one-wise independence). For this family
\mathcal{F}
, we have
\rho(\mathcal{F})=\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathcal{F})=1/2
.

[
22
]
proved that any single-pass streaming algorithm solving
\mathsf{MaxCut}[1,1/2+\varepsilon]
must use
\Omega_{\varepsilon}(n)
bits of memeory. Later,
[
8
]
generalized
[
22
]
’s result to predicate families that satisfy a certain condition (stronger than supporting one-wise independence). However, they were not able to generalize their lower bound to all predicate families that support one-wise independence, due to technical complications (as explained in
[
8
, Section 1.4]
).

One of the technical components (see the discussion at the end of Section
1.3.4
) in the proof of Theorem
1.2
is to address these complications and generalize
[
22
]
’s result to all predicate families supporting one-wise independence. Indeed, Theorem
1.2
implies the following strengthening of Theorem
1.7
:

Theorem 1.8
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\Sigma^{k}\rightarrow\{0,1\}\}
is a predicate family such that every predicate
f\in\mathcal{F}
supports one-wise independence (as formally defined in Section
2.1
). Then for any
\varepsilon>0
, any single-pass streaming algorithm solving
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[1,\rho(\mathcal{F})+\varepsilon]
must use
\Omega_{\varepsilon}(n)
bits of memory.

Remark 1.9
.

Theorem
1.2
actually implies that this approximation resistance also holds against, say,
o(\log n)
-pass
n^{0.99}
-space, or
n^{o(1)}
-pass
n^{0.49}
-space streaming algorithms.

1.2.2
Discussion: Query-to-Communication Lifting

The dichotomy results of
[
33
,
14
]
revealed a phenomenological connection between the multi-pass streaming model and the ‘‘bounded-degree query model’’ from the property testing literature. We do not attempt to fully elaborate on this connection here.
3
3

3

We refer the interested reader to
[
14
, Section 1.2.2]
for a more detailed discussion.
Instead, we provide an informal account of how it relates to the present work.

A standard approach to proving multi-pass streaming lower bounds for
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[c,s]
is to consider a corresponding communication problem. In this formulation, a constant number of players each hold a sequence of constraints over a common set of variables, and their goal is to distinguish whether the value of the combined CSP instance is at least
c
or at most
s
. If this communication problem requires
f(n)
bits of communication, then a standard reduction implies that any
p
-pass streaming algorithm for
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[c,s]
must use
\Omega(f(n)/p)
bits of space.

In communication complexity, a successful paradigm (e.g.,
[
26
,
17
,
18
]
) is to first establish a
query
lower bound for a suitably defined ‘‘query version’’ of the problem,
4
4

4

Typically, the query model is strictly weaker than the corresponding communication model.
and then “lift” this bound to the communication setting.

Interestingly, the bounded-degree query model studied in
[
33
]
serves as an appropriate query analogue of the communication problem for
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[c,s]
. In this model,
[
33
]
shows that for any
\varepsilon>0
, achieving an
(\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}+\varepsilon)
-approximation for
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
requires
\Omega_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{n})
queries. The work of
[
14
]
lifts this query lower bound to a communication lower bound of
\Omega_{\varepsilon}(n^{1/3})
, thereby yielding an
\Omega(n^{1/3}/p)
space lower bound for
p
-pass streaming algorithms. However, their lifting incurs a loss in the exponent, reducing it from
1/2
to
1/3
. Our Theorem
1.2
can be viewed as a refinement of their query-to-communication lifting that avoids this polynomial loss.

Conversely, algorithms in the query model can usually be simulated by multi-pass streaming algorithms. For example, the bipartiteness tester of
[
16
,
23
]
implies the following result:
5
5

5

The algorithm of
[
16
]
works not only for bounded-degree graphs, but also for all regular graphs. To extend the algorithm to general graphs, one can use either the reduction in
[
23
, Section 4]
or (a straightforward adaptation of) the one in
[
14
, Section 4]
.

Theorem 1.10

(
[
16
,
23
]
)
.

For any fixed constant
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there is a
\mathrm{polylog}(n)
-pass,
\sqrt{n}\cdot\mathrm{polylog}(n)
-space streaming algorithm for
\mathsf{MaxCut}[1,1-\varepsilon]
.

Theorem
1.10
implies that the
\Omega(\sqrt{n}/p)
lower bound in Theorem
1.2
(2) cannot be improved by more than
\mathrm{polylog}(n)
factors; for the other lower bound
n\cdot 2^{-O(p)}
featured in Theorem
1.2
(2), the exponent
O(p)
cannot be improved to sub-polynomial in
p
.

1.2.3
Beyond
\sqrt{n}
Queries

There are also some MaxCSP problems for which the query complexity in the bounded-degree query model is known to be
\Omega(n)
. For example, for the predicate family
\mathsf{E3Lin}
6
6

6

The family
\mathsf{E3Lin}
consists of two predicates
f_{0},f_{1}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{3}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
defined by
f_{b}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=1
if and only if
x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}=b
, for each
b\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
.
,
[
5
]
showed that
\mathsf{MaxE3Lin}[1,1/2+\varepsilon]
requires
\Omega_{\varepsilon}(n)
queries. By slightly extending our techniques for proving Theorem
1.2
, we are also able to lift
[
5
]
’s query lower bound to a communication lower bound, yielding the following result:

Theorem 1.11
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\Sigma^{k}\rightarrow\{0,1\}\}
is a predicate family such that every predicate
f\in\mathcal{F}
supports two-wise independence (as formally defined in Section
2.1
). Then for any
\varepsilon>0
, any
p
-pass streaming algorithm solving
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[1,\rho(\mathcal{F})+\varepsilon]
must use
\Omega_{\varepsilon}(n/p)
bits of memory.

A recent work
[
15
]
showed that for any predicate family that has
unbounded width
,
7
7

7

The definition of unbounded width is slightly complicated (see
[
12
]
or
[
15
, Appendix A]
); a nice point of reference is that if deciding satisfiability of
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instances is (known to be)
NP
-hard, then
\mathcal{F}
has unbounded width.
there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[1,1-\varepsilon]
requires
\Omega_{\varepsilon}(n)
queries in the bounded-degree query model. Using the same techniques, it is not hard to lift this lower bound also to the multi-pass streaming model (answering
[
15
, Question 1.11]
):

Theorem 1.12
.

For any predicate family
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\Sigma^{k}\rightarrow\{0,1\}\}
that has unbounded width, there exists a constant
\varepsilon>0
such that any
p
-pass streaming algorithm for
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[1,1-\varepsilon]
must use at least
\Omega(n/p)
bits of memory.

1.3
Technical Overview

In this section we discuss our techniques, which heavily build on
[
22
]
and
[
14
]
. The heart of the proof of
Theorem

1.2
is a communication lower bound for the distributional hidden partition problem (DIHP) from
[
14
]
, which we describe next.

1.3.1
The DIHP Problem

Given an instance
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
and a solution
\{x_{\mathsf{v},\sigma}\}_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V},\sigma\in\Sigma},\{z_{i,b}\}_{i\in[m],b\in\Sigma^{k}}
to
{\textsc{BasicLP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
, we first consider an intermediate object called a distribution-labeled graph. It is convenient to turn the LP solution into distributions over the Abelian groups
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
. To do that, one picks a large enough
N
so that
x_{\mathsf{v},\sigma}N
are all integers, and for each
\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{I}
picks a partition
\{I_{\mathsf{v},\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in\Sigma}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
where
I_{\mathsf{v},\sigma}
has size
x_{\mathsf{v},\sigma}N
. Now for each constraint
C_{i}
, the distribution
\mu_{C_{i}}
is defined as sampling
b=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k})\in\Sigma^{k}
according to
z_{i,b}
, and then for each variable
\{\mathsf{v}_{i,j}\}_{j=1,\ldots,k}
in
C_{i}
, replace
b_{j}
with a random element from
I_{\mathsf{v}_{i,j},b_{j}}
. We denote the underlying
k
-uniform hypergraph by
G
, and the distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
on each edge
{\sf e}
by
\mu_{\sf e}
.

With this setting in mind, the DIHP problem is defined as follows. The number of players is
|E(G)|K
, where
K
is a large constant.
Let
n
be thought of tending to infinity, and consider the blow-up
H
of
G
resulting from replacing each vertex in it by
n
new vertices. For each hyperedge
{\sf e}\in E(G)
we have
K
corresponding players, each one of them receiving a randomly chosen matching of size
\alpha n
between the clouds in
H
corresponding to the vertices of
{\sf e}
. The edges in
H
are additionally labeled, in a way that depends on whether we generate a yes or a no instance:

1.

In
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}
, we first sample
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
uniformly. Then, for each edge
e
sent to a player, we consider
{\sf e}
the edge of
G
that generated it, sample
w\sim\mu_{\sf e}
, and label
e
by
x_{|e}-w
.

2.

In
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}
, the label of each edge
e
of
H
is chosen uniformly from
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
.

An instance of DIHP is generated either from
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}

or
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}
, and the goal of the players is to distinguish between the two cases. As proved in
[
14
]
, a communication lower bound of
f(n)
on this problem implies a memory lower bound of
\Omega_{\varepsilon}(f(n)/p
) for any
p
-pass algorithm solving

\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[c-\varepsilon,s+\varepsilon]
where
c=\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
and
s=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}
, and the rest of the discussion is focused on the DIHP problem.

1.3.2
Previous Approaches and Challenges

Consider any protocol
\Pi
for DIHP. A key idea in
[
13
,
14
]
is that, while a naive application of the discrepancy method fails to give decent lower bounds for
\Pi
, it is successful assuming the protocol
\Pi
is
global
, a notion we introduce next.

Note that if we choose a random
k
-uniform matching of size
\alpha n
on
kn
vertices, then the probability a particular edge will appear in it is roughly
\frac{\alpha}{n}
. More generally, the probability
i
specific edges will appear in it is roughly
\left(\frac{\alpha}{n}\right)^{i}
.
Informally, a protocol
\Pi
is called global if each one of its induced rectangles
R=A^{(1)}\times\ldots\times A^{(|E(G)|K)}
has a similar behavior. Also, we call this type of rectangles
global
rectangles. Namely, if we sample a labeled matching from any one of the
A^{(j)}
’s, each subset of
i
specific edges will appear there with probability
O((\alpha/n)^{i})
. One might expect the following two hopes to be true:

•

general communication protocols can be reduced to global protocols;

•

the discrepancy of global protocols can be bounded.

First, notice that there are a lot examples of non-global communication protocols. To see this, we consider the following protocol: after receiving its input, the first player writes one of the labeled edges in its matching on the blackboard. Then, for every induced rectangle
R=A^{(1)}\times\dots\times A^{(|E(G)|K)}
, there exists one certain edge that appears in every labeled matching in
A^{(1)}
. So, it seems impossible to directly reduce general protocols to global ones. To eliminate this issue,
[
14
]
proves that every general
\Pi
can actually be transformed into a protocol of a certain type, and every induced rectangle
R=A^{(1)}\times\dots\times A^{(|E(G)|K)}
of the transformed protocol is in the following form: there exists tuples of labeled partial matchings
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{z}^{(|E(G)|K)}\right)
, such that (1) for every
i\in[|E(G)|K]
, every labeled matching in
A^{(i)}
subsumes
\mathbf{z}^{(i)}
; and (2) the rest parts of
A^{(i)}
look global. In this case, we simply refer to
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
as a structured rectangle.

The main technical result in
[
14
]
is that given a communication protocol
\Pi
with
O(n^{1/3})
bits of communication:

•

one can transform
\Pi
into a protocol such that most of its induced rectangle are (1) structured, and thus can be written as
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
; (2) large, which means
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}\geqslant 2^{-O(n^{1/3})}
; (3) the total number of labeled edges in
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is small (say, at most
O(n^{1/3})
); (4) edges in
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
do not form a cycle, i.e., there is no collection of
r
edges that cover at most
r(k-1)
vertices.

•

every rectangle with the above four conditions has
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\approx\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)
.

Indeed, the first part can be extended to protocols with
O(\sqrt{n})
communication with corresponding weaker guarantees. The barrier to an
\Omega(\sqrt{n})
lower bound lies in the second part, namely, proving a structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
, which satisfies (1)
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\geqslant 2^{-O(\sqrt{n})}
; (2) the total number of labeled edges in
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is at most
O(\sqrt{n})
; (3) edges in
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
do not form a cycle, has small discrepancy under the two measures.

Furthermore, when one tries to prove a near-linear lower bound against protocols with a bounded number of rounds of communication, the first part also breaks. Briefly speaking, our previous argument is round-insensitive, and there actually exists a communication protocol using
\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{n})
bits to find a cycle in the union of
|E|K
labeled matchings. This means the previous argument cannot show that most structured rectangles
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
do not contain any cycle when the communication cost exceeds
\sqrt{n}
.

With this description in mind, the argument in this paper has to overcome the following two challenges:

1.

Better discrepancy bound for structured rectangles: as mentioned before, the discrepancy bound for structured rectangles in
[
14
]
is the bottleneck in the above argument, and improving it (up to
\sqrt{n}
) would immediately translate to better lower bounds for multi-pass algorithms. Having said that, the Fourier analytic arguments there seem tight and no specific component could be improved in an obvious way.

2.

Better characterization of structured rectangles with low discrepancy: the above argument is incapable of getting lower bounds better than
\sqrt{n}
, even for a small number of passes
p
. The issue is that if we only consider the number of exposed edges in the structured part, we are unable to tell the difference between a collection of
t
disjoint edges being exposed, and a collection of connected
t
edges (in which further addition of edges is more likely to create cycles)
8
8

8

Please see Section
4.2
for a more detailed explanation.
. Thus, it seems necessary to find another characterization of structured rectangles with small discrepancy instead of considering the number of edges exposed.

1.3.3
The Weight of a Restriction

To solve the second issue we use an idea from
[
22
]
. Denoting the graph of exposed edges by
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
and letting its connected components be
V_{1},\ldots,V_{t}
, the weight of
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
is defined as

\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t}|V_{i}|^{2}
. The work
[
22
]
analyzed the behavior of
\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|
under edge exposure when each player only speaks once, and showed that it is a good progress measure towards cyclicity for up to linearly many bits of communication.

The parameter
\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|
plays an important role in our analysis as well: we show that it is a good progress measure toward cyclicity for general protocols, as a replacement of the number of edges exposed by
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
. In particular, we give an analog of the decomposition lemma of
[
14
]
with respect to it (instead the progress measure
|\boldsymbol{\zeta}|=\sum_{i}|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(i)})|
used previously).
For our purposes, we have to consider protocols consisting of several rounds of communication, and we show that
\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|
typically grows by at most a constant factor in each round of communication. Thus, in the end it is typically at most as large as
2^{O(p)}|\Pi|
.
9
9

9

This is the source of the exponential dependence on
p
in the
n\cdot 2^{-O(p)}
space lower bound featured in Theorem
1.2
.

1.3.4
Improving the Discrepancy Bound

To address the first issue raised at the end of Section
1.3.2
, we again draw inspiration from
[
22
]
. The central argument of
[
22
]
analyzes how the distribution of the hidden partition
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
,
10
10

10

In
[
22
]
, the hidden partition lies in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
.
conditioned on the transcript of the communication so far, evolves over the course of the protocol. By controlling the Fourier-
\ell_{1}
mass of the probability density function of
x
,
[
22
]
improved the single-pass space lower bound for
\mathsf{MaxCut}[1,1/2+\varepsilon]
from
\Omega(\sqrt{n})
(obtained in
[
21
]
via Fourier-
\ell_{2}
methods) to
\Omega(n)
.

Our situation appears analogous to the state of affairs prior to the breakthrough of
[
22
]
. In particular, we seek to strengthen the
multi-pass
space lower bound for
\mathsf{MaxCut}[1,1/2+\varepsilon]
from
\Omega(n^{1/3}/p)
, as established in
[
13
]
using Fourier-
\ell_{2}
techniques, to the near-optimal
\Omega(\sqrt{n}/p)
. It is thus natural to suspect the Fourier-
\ell_{1}
-based techniques of
[
22
]
could be used to overcome the first issue mentioned in Section
1.3.2
, and yield a near-optimal multi-pass space lower bound.

However, it is not immediately clear how the techniques of
[
22
]
can be extended to establish multi-pass streaming lower bounds. At a high level, two main obstacles arise:

1.

To control the evolution of the Fourier-
\ell_{1}
mass of the density function of
x
,
[
22
]
applies an induction on the number of rounds in the protocol. At low Fourier levels, the
\ell_{1}
-mass bound deteriorates by a constant factor with each additional message. While this degradation is tolerable in the single-pass setting — where there are only a constant number of players, each speaking at most once — it becomes prohibitive in the multi-pass setting, as it leads to an exponential dependence on the number of rounds (and hence on the number of passes). Consequently, this approach cannot yield an
\Omega(\sqrt{n}/p)
-type lower bound.

2.

The induction argument in
[
22
]
also critically relies on the fact that each player’s message is generated from a fresh random labeled matching that is independent of the prior communication transcript. This independence assumption breaks down in the multi-pass setting, where players may speak multiple times.

The strength of the structure-vs.-randomness framework of
[
13
,
14
]
lies precisely in its ability to handle such obstacles. From the perspective of this framework, however, the argument of
[
22
]
has an intriguing feature: it does not rely on any explicit decomposition of the players’ messages into “structured” and “pseudorandom” components. As noted in Section
1.3.3
,
[
22
]
does carry out a combinatorial analysis of the (highly structured) edge-exposure protocol using the notion of weight
\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|
. Importantly, this analysis is
not
invoked in their treatment of general single-pass protocols. Instead, the Fourier-
\ell_{1}
method developed in
[
22
]
can be viewed as a
generalization
of this combinatorial analysis: it provides a unified approach that simultaneously captures both structured information and pseudorandom noise, which may coexist in general protocols.

The key idea enabling us to incorporate the techniques of
[
22
]
into the framework of
[
14
]
is to apply the Fourier-
\ell_{1}
-based induction argument of
[
22
]
only to the pseudorandom component of each player’s message. We begin by using the decomposition technique from
[
14
, Section 7]
to cleanly separate the structured information in the messages from the pseudorandom noise. The structured components of all players’ messages are then aggregated and analyzed via the combinatorial method described in Section
1.3.3
, while the pseudorandom components are handled using the Fourier-
\ell_{1}
-based induction argument.

This approach overcomes the two high-level obstacles discussed above. Nevertheless, several technical issues must still be addressed:

1.

In the induction argument of
[
22
]
, each player’s message is derived from a labeled matching that is not only independent of prior communication, but also
uniformly random
. While the independence lacked in the multi-pass setting can be restored by the idea described above, the resulting labeled matchings are no longer perfectly uniform, but only “pseudorandom.” Fortunately, such pseudorandom matchings can be shown to be sufficiently similar to uniform for the argument to carry through.

2.

While the above discussion suffices to obtain the desired
\Omega(\sqrt{n}/p)
space lower bound for
\mathsf{MaxCut}[1,1/2+\varepsilon]
, extending the result to general CSPs introduces additional challenges that are largely orthogonal to the issues discussed so far. In particular, we must first establish the
single-pass
result (Theorem
1.8
), which was previously unknown.

1.4
Open Problems

We finish this introductory section with a few open directions for future research.

1.

While the multi-pass lower bound provided in
Theorem

1.2
is tight in general, it can be improved for some problems as in
Theorems

1.11
and
1.12
. It would be interesting to know if there are a natural criteria dictating whether the space-pass tradeoff is
\widetilde{\Theta}(\sqrt{n})
or
\widetilde{\Theta}(n)
, and even more interesting if there are intermediate behaviors. Perhaps the “query version” of this question has to be answered first; see
[
15
, Question 1.13]
.

2.

The algorithm (Theorem
1.6
) of
[
28
]
that complements Corollary
1.5
only works for bounded degree instances, because we cannot afford the degree-reduction step in
[
14
]
as it requires more than a single pass. The recent work
[
4
]
shows that the bounded-degree assumption in Theorem
1.6
can be removed for the specific problem of max-directed-cut. For general CSPs, it remains open whether there is a single-pass
n^{1-\Omega_{\varepsilon}(1)}
-space algorithm for
(\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathcal{F})-\varepsilon)
-approximation of
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
.

3.

As noted in the first item above, it is unknown whether the space-pass tradeoff grows from
\widetilde{\Theta}(\sqrt{n})
to
\widetilde{\Theta}(n)
continuously or discretely as a function of the approximation ratio
\alpha\in[\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathcal{F}),1]
. In the single-pass setting, the mystery lies
below
the LP threshold
\alpha_{\mathrm{LP}}(\mathcal{F})
: for the specific problem of max-directed-cut (whose LP threshold equals
1/2
),
[
10
]
shows that
(4/9-\varepsilon)
-approximation can be achieved by single-pass
O_{\varepsilon}(\log n)
-space algorithms, while
(4/9+\varepsilon)
-approximation requires
\Omega_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{n})
-space. As shown by
[
22
]
, achieving
(1/2+\varepsilon)
-approximation of max-directed-cut in the single-pass setting requires
\Omega_{\varepsilon}(n)
space. It is unknown whether the space complexity grows continuously as a function of the approximation ratio
\alpha\in[4/9,1/2]
. For general CSPs, it is also unknown whether there is a critical approximation ratio at which the single-pass space complexity jumps from
\mathrm{polylog}(n)
to
n^{\Omega(1)}
.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
General Notations

In this subsection we summarize general notational conventions used throughout the paper. Additional notation will be introduced as needed, typically within dedicated “Notation” environments.

Arithmetic.

We use the convention
0^{0}=1
. For a real number
x
, we denote
x^{+}=\max\{x,0\}
.

Probability.

For a finite set
\Lambda
, we write
\mathbb{E}_{x\in\Lambda}\left[\cdot\right]
and
\mathbb{P}_{x\in\Lambda}\left[\cdot\right]
to denote expectation and probability, respectively, when
x
is drawn uniformly at random from
\Lambda
.
If
x
is sampled according to a specific distribution
\mathcal{D}
over
\Lambda
, we write
x\sim\mathcal{D}
in place of
x\in\Lambda
. A
probability mass function
on
\Lambda
is a function
p:\Lambda\rightarrow[0,\infty)
such that
\sum_{x\in\Lambda}p(x)=1
, while a
probability density function
is a function
f:\Lambda\rightarrow[0,\infty)
such that
\mathbb{E}_{x\in\Lambda}\left[f(x)\right]=1
. A
right stochastic matrix
, or a
Markov kernel
, is a matrix in which each row is a probability mass function on the set of columns.

Hilbert space.

For a finite set
\Lambda
, we denote by
L^{2}(\Lambda)
the (finite-dimensional) Hilbert space of complex-valued functions on
\Lambda
, equipped with the inner product

\langle f,g\rangle:=\underset{x\in\Lambda}{\mathbb{E}}\left[f(x)\,\overline{g(x)}\right].

Fourier analysis.

We denote the finite cyclic group
\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
by
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
, where
N\geqslant 2
is an integer. Throughout the paper, the capital letter
N
is reserved exclusively for this notation. For any finite index set
\Lambda
, the collection of Fourier characters on the product group
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
is indexed by
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
itself. More precisely, for
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
, the associated character function
\chi_{b}:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}\to\mathbb{C}
is defined by

\chi_{b}(x):=\exp\left(\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{N}\sum_{v\in\Lambda}b_{v}x_{v}\right),

where
\mathrm{i}
denotes the imaginary unit. For a function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda})
, we write
\widehat{f}(b):=\langle f,\chi_{b}\rangle
, and the Fourier
\ell^{1}
-norm of
f
is denoted by

\left\|f\right\|_{\mathsf{W}}:=\sum_{b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}}\left|\widehat{f}(b)\right|.

Vectors and maps.

For a vector
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
or
x\in[0,1]^{\Lambda}
, we denote its coordinates by subscripts:
x_{v}
for each
v\in\Lambda
.
A related notion is that of a
map

\mathbf{y}:\Lambda\to\Gamma
. We use boldface symbols for maps, especially when their images are themselves vectors, to distinguish them from ordinary vectors.
For
v\in\Lambda
, the value of the map at
v
is denoted by
\mathbf{y}(v)
.
The collection of all such maps is denoted by
\mathrm{Map}\left(\Lambda,\Gamma\right)
.

Support sets.

Let
\Gamma
be a domain containing a distinguished
nullity element
. For either a vector
x\in\Gamma^{\Lambda}
or a map
\mathbf{y}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(\Lambda,\Gamma\right)
, the
support
of
x
or
\mathbf{y}
— denoted
\mathrm{supp}(x)
or
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})
— is the set of elements
v\in\Lambda
such that
x_{v}
or
\mathbf{y}(v)
is not equal to the nullity element. For example, when
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}_{N}
, the nullity element is the additive identity
0
. In some cases, the domain
\Gamma
is taken to be a disjoint union of an Abelian group and a special symbol — such as
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\cup\{\textup{{nil}}\}
— in which case the nullity element is the special symbol
nil
, rather than the identity of the group. The
Hamming weight
of a vector
x\in\Gamma^{\Lambda}
or a map
\mathbf{y}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(\Lambda,\Gamma\right)
, denoted by
\|x\|_{\mathsf{H}}
or
\|\mathbf{y}\|_{\mathsf{H}}
, respectively, is the cardinality of its support set.

Degree decomposition.

Let
\Lambda
and
\Gamma
be finite sets. A function
f:\Gamma^{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is said to be a
d
-junta, where
d
is a nonnegative integer no larger than
|\Lambda|
, if
f(x)
depends on at most
d
coordinates of
x
. A function
f
is said to have
degree
at most
d
if
f
is a linear combination of
d
-juntas. For any function
f\in L^{2}(\Gamma^{\Lambda})
, we let
f^{\leqslant d}
be the orthogonal projection of
f
to the linear subspace of
L^{2}(\Gamma^{\Lambda})
consisting of functions of degree at most
d
. In the case
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}_{N}
, there is a convenient explicit formula for
f^{\leqslant d}
: if
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda})
we will write the degree-
d
part of
f
as

f^{=d}:=\sum_{b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda},\,|\mathrm{supp}(b)|=d}\left\langle f,\chi_{b}\right\rangle\cdot\chi_{b},

and we then have
f^{\leqslant d}=\sum_{i=0}^{d}f^{=i}
.

CSPs and hypergraphs.

Throughout the paper,
\Sigma
denotes the CSP alphabet, and the lowercase letter
k
always refers to the arity of predicates. The calligraphic letter
\mathcal{F}
always denotes a nonempty finite set of predicates mapping from
\Sigma^{k}
to
\{0,1\}
. Correspondingly, all hypergraphs in this paper are assumed to be
k
-uniform. When the context is clear, hyperedges are sometimes simply referred to as edges. A set of hyperedges in a
k
-uniform hypergraph is said to contain a cycle if there exist
\ell
hyperedges in the set that cover at most
\ell(k-1)
vertices, for some
\ell\geqslant 1
. A connected component of a hypergraph is said to be nontrivial if it contains at least two vertices.

Blow-up of hypergraphs.

Sometimes a hypergraph undergoes a
blow-up
, in which each original vertex is replaced by
n
copies. We adopt the following notational convention: pre-blowup vertices and hyperedges are denoted using sans-serif font (e.g.,
\mathsf{v}
and
\mathsf{e}
), while post-blowup vertices and hyperedges are written in standard math font (e.g.,
v
and
e
).

One-wise/two-wise independence.

Let
\Gamma
be a finite set. A probability distribution over
\Gamma^{k}
is called
one-wise independent
if its marginal on each of the
k
coordinates is the uniform distribution on
\Gamma
. It is called
two-wise independent
if its marginal on any two distinct coordinates is the uniform distribution over
\Gamma^{2}
. For any one-wise independent distribution
\mu
, we denote by
\mu(\cdot)
the probability mass function of
\mu
. A predicate
f:\Sigma^{k}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
is said to support one-wise (respectively, two-wise) independence if there exists a one-wise (respectively, two-wise) independent distribution supported on
f^{-1}(1)
.

2.2
Hypercontractivity

Hypercontractive inequalities on product spaces have been crucial tools in establishing streaming lower bounds for approximating CSPs. We need the following version in this paper:

Proposition 2.1

(
[
24
, Theorem 10.21]
)
.

Let
\Lambda
and
\Gamma
be finite sets. For any function
f:\Gamma^{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with degree at most
d
and any real number
q\geqslant 2
, we have

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert_{q}\leqslant\left(\sqrt{(q-1)|\Gamma|}\right)^{d}\lVert f\rVert_{2}

As is standard in many applications, the above hypercontractivity result is used to obtain the following level-
d
inequality.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
\Lambda
and
\Gamma
be finite sets. For any function
f:\Gamma^{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and any positive integer
d\leqslant 2\log_{2}(\|f\|_{2}/\|f\|_{1})
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|f^{\leqslant d}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\|f\|_{1}^{2}\cdot\left(\frac{8|\Gamma|}{d}\log_{2}\left(\frac{\|f\|_{2}}{\|f\|_{1}}\right)\right)^{d}.

Proof.

For any
q\geqslant 2
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|f^{\leqslant d}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\left\langle f,f^{\leqslant d}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\|f^{\leqslant d}\right\|_{q}\cdot\lVert f\rVert_{q/(q-1)}\leqslant\left\|f^{\leqslant d}\right\|_{q}\cdot\lVert f\rVert_{1}^{(q-2)/q}\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2/q}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\sqrt{(q-1)|\Gamma|}\right)^{d}\left\|f^{\leqslant d}\right\|_{2}\cdot\lVert f\rVert_{1}^{(q-2)/q}\lVert f\rVert_{2}^{2/q},

where the second and third transitions are by Hölder’s inequality, and the fourth transition is by
Proposition

2.1
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\left\|f^{\leqslant d}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\|f\|_{1}^{2}\cdot\left((q-1)|\Gamma|\right)^{d}\left(\frac{\|f\|_{2}}{\|f\|_{1}}\right)^{4/q}.

(2.1)

Taking
q=4d^{-1}\log_{2}\left(\lVert f\rVert_{2}/\|f\|_{1}\right)
yields the conclusion.
∎

2.3
The Basic Linear Program

We show that Theorem
1.8
follows from Theorem
1.2
using the following fact about
{\textsc{BasicLP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\Sigma^{k}\rightarrow\{0,1\}\}
is a predicate family such that every predicate
f\in\mathcal{F}
supports one-wise independence. Then for any instance
\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{C})\in\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
, we have
\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}}=1
.

Proof.

Notice that the solution where

(1)

\{x_{\mathsf{v},\sigma}\}_{\sigma\in\Sigma}
represents the uniform distribution over
\Sigma
for any variable
\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V}
, and

(2)

\{z_{i,b}\}_{b\in\Sigma^{k}}
represents a one-wise independent distribution supported on
f_{i}^{-1}(1)
for any constraint
C_{i}=((\mathsf{v}_{i,1},\dots,\mathsf{v}_{i,k}),f_{i})
in
\mathcal{C}
.

achieves objective value 1 in
{\textsc{BasicLP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.8
assuming Theorem
1.2
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, we can pick an instance
\mathcal{I}\in\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
with
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}\leqslant\rho(\mathcal{F})+\varepsilon/2
(by the definition (
1.2
)). By Proposition
2.3
we know that
\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}}=1
. We can then apply Theorem
1.2
(2) to the instance
\mathcal{I}
to yield the hardness of
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[1,\rho(\mathcal{F})+\varepsilon]
.
∎

Similarly to the proof of Proposition
2.3
, it is easy to derive the following proposition, which will be used in Section
8.3
to prove Theorems
1.11
and
1.12
.

Proposition 2.4
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\Sigma^{k}\rightarrow\{0,1\}\}
is a predicate family such that every predicate
f\in\mathcal{F}
supports
two-wise
independence. Then for any instance
\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{V},(C_{1},\dots,C_{m}))\in\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
, there exists a solution
\left((x_{\mathsf{v},\sigma})_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V},\,\sigma\in\Sigma},(z_{i,b})_{i\in[m],\,b\in\Sigma^{k}}\right)
to
{\textsc{BasicLP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
achieving objective value 1 such that
\{z_{i,b}\}_{b\in\Sigma^{k}}
represents a two-wise independent distribution over
\Sigma^{k}
for any
i\in[m]
.

3
Streaming Lower Bound from Communication Complexity

The purpose of this section is to introduce (as a black box) a key lemma in
[
14
, Section 5]
showing that communication lower bounds for a certain communication game imply space lower bounds for streaming approximation of CSPs. In Sections
3.1
to
3.4
, we present the necessary definitions for formalizing the communication game, and we refer the reader to
[
14
]
for more detailed discussion of their motivations.

3.1
Labeled Matchings

The first ingredient in the communication game is labeled matchings, which are combinatorial objects that have been widely used in establishing streaming lower bounds for approximating CSPs. While several different sets of notation have been used in the literature to represent labeled matchings, in this paper we adopt the formalism introduced in
[
14
, Section 5]
. We list the relevant definitions below.

Definition 3.1
.

For finite sets
U_{1},\dots,U_{k}
of equal cardinality, we call the tuple
\mathcal{U}=(U_{1},\dots,U_{k})
a
k
-universe
. The
cardinality
of
\mathcal{U}
, denoted by
|\mathcal{U}|
, is defined to be the common cardinality of the sets
U_{i}
. For convenience, we use the shorthand
\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U}
for the union
\bigcup_{i\in[k]}U_{i}
and
\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U}
for the Cartesian product
\prod_{i=1}^{k}U_{i}
.

Definition 3.2
.

For a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
and a nonnegative integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
, we let
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
denote the collection of all matchings (without labels) in the complete
k
-partite hypergraph
(\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U},\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U})
(the hypergraph with vertex set
\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U}
and edge set
\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U}
) with
m
edges. We also write
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}:=\bigcup_{d=0}^{m}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},d}
.

Definition 3.3
.

For a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
and a nonnegative integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
, we define the following space of labeled matchings:

\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}:=\left\{\mathbf{y}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U},\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\cup\{\textup{{nil}}\}\right):\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})\text{ is a matching with }m\text{ edges}\right\}.

Here,
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})
denotes the support of
\mathbf{y}
, i.e., the edges in
\prod\mathcal{U}
mapped to
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
(see
Section

2.1
).

3.2
The Markov Kernel

The notion of Markov kernels provides significant convenience in both the formalization and the analysis of the communication game. The following notation will be helpful in defining the relevant Markov transitions.

Notation 3.4
.

Suppose
\Lambda
is a ground set and
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
-vector indexed by
\Lambda
. If
e=(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})
is a tuple of elements with each
v_{i}\in\Lambda
for
i\in[k]
, we denote by
x_{|e}
the vector
(x_{v_{1}},\dots,x_{v_{k}})\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
.

Definition 3.5
.

Fix a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
, a positive integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
, and a one-wise independent distribution
\mu
over
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
. We define a right stochastic matrix
\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}\times\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
as follows. For each
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
and
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, the entry
\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}(x,\mathbf{y})
is the probability that the output of the following process equals
\mathbf{y}
:

1.

sample a matching
M
uniformly at random from
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
;

2.

let
\mathbf{z}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
have support
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})=M
, and

3.

for each edge
e\in M
, draw
w_{e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
independently from
\mu
and set
\mathbf{z}(e)=x_{|e}-w_{e}
, where the subtraction is performed in the Abelian group
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
;

4.

output
\mathbf{z}
.

3.3
Distribution-Labeled
k
-Graphs

The communication game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
(to be defined in
Section

3.4
) is based on an abstract structure
G
called a distribution-labeled
k
-graph, which we now define as follows.

Definition 3.6
.

A
distribution-labeled
k
-graph

G
consists of the following data: a vertex set
\mathcal{V}
; a multi-set
\mathcal{E}
of hyperedges, each an ordered
k
-tuple of distinct vertices in
\mathcal{V}
; a positive integer
N
; and a collection of probability distributions
(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}}
, where each
\mu_{\mathsf{e}}
is a one-wise independent probability distribution on the Abelian group
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
.

The second parameter
n
in
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
is the blow-up factor of the distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G
. The set-theoretic structure of the blow-up is captured by the following definition.

Definition 3.7
.

Given a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
and a positive integer
n
, we define the following associated combinatorial objects.

1.

The set
\mathcal{V}\times[n]
, i.e. the
n
-blow-up of the vertex set
\mathcal{V}
, will be referred to as the ground set.

2.

For each
\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V}
, let
U_{\mathsf{v}}:=\{\mathsf{v}\}\times[n]
be the subset of
\mathcal{V}\times[n]
consisting of the
n
copies of
\mathsf{v}
.

3.

We associate with each hyperedge
\mathsf{e}=(\mathsf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{v}_{k})\in\mathcal{E}
the
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}}:=(U_{\mathsf{v}_{1}},\dots,U_{\mathsf{v}_{k}})
.

Note that in the above definition, for each
\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}
, the
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}}
is
embedded
in the set
\mathcal{V}\times[n]
, in the sense that
\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}}
is a subset of
\mathcal{V}\times[n]
. In general, we record the following notational convention.

Notation 3.8
.

A
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
is said to be
embedded
in a finite set
\Lambda
if
\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U}
is a subset of
\Lambda
.

3.4
The Communication Game

The following notation will be helpful in defining the communication game, as well as in later parts of the paper.

Definition 3.9
.

Fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
. The Abelian group
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
will play a central role throughout the paper. For each edge
\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}
, recall from
Definition

3.7
that
\bigcup\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}}\subseteq\mathcal{V}\times[n]
. We denote by
\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}}
the canonical projection from
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
onto
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}}}
.

We are now ready to define the communication game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
.

Definition 3.10
.

Given a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
, parameters
n,K\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we define the communication game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
as follows:

1.

There are
|\mathcal{E}|\cdot K
players, each indexed by a pair
(\mathsf{e},j)
, where
\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}
and
j\in[K]
.

2.

Each player
(\mathsf{e},j)
receives as input a labeled matching in
\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
.

3.

The no distribution:
define
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}
to be the uniform distribution on the Cartesian product
\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
, i.e. each player gets an independent uniformly random input.

4.

The yes distribution:
define
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}
to be the joint distribution of
(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
obtained by the following procedure:

•

Sample a uniformly random vector
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
.

•

For each player
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
, independently draw a labeled matching
\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
according to the distribution given by the probability mass function
\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}\big(\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}}(x),\cdot\big)
.

The goal of the players is to decide whether their inputs
(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
comes from
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}
or
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}
.

Remark 3.11
.

Throughout this paper, whenever we refer to the communication game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
, we treat
G,\alpha
and
K
as fixed parameters, and consider the asymptotic regime
n\rightarrow\infty
.

As is standard in distributional communication complexity, we measure the performance of a communication protocol by its “advantage”, defined as follows.

Definition 3.12
.

A deterministic communication protocol
\Pi
for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
computes a function
\Pi:\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
. We define its
advantage
in the communication game as

\displaystyle\mathrm{adv}(\Pi):=\left|\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\Pi(\mathbf{Y})=1\right]-\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\Pi(\mathbf{Y})=1\right]\right|,

where
\mathbf{Y}
denotes a joint input
\mathbf{Y}=(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
.

The communication complexity of
\mathsf{DIHP}
is then defined as follows.

Definition 3.13
.

The
communication cost
of a protocol
\Pi
, denoted by
|\Pi|
, is the total number of bits broadcasted by all players across all rounds during its execution.
The
communication complexity
of the game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
, denoted by
\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)
, is the minimum communication cost over all protocols
\Pi
that satisfy
\mathrm{adv}(\Pi)\geqslant 0.1
.

The first result of our paper is the following improved communication lower bound of the
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
game.

Theorem 3.14
.

Fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
, an integer
K>0
and a parameter
\alpha\in\big(0,2^{-20}N^{-10k}|\mathcal{V}|^{-2}\big]
. There exists a constant
\gamma=\gamma(G,\alpha,K)>0
such that
\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)\geqslant\gamma\sqrt{n}
.

Remark 3.15
.

Previously,
[
14
, Theorem 5.13]
proved the lower bound
\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)\geqslant\Omega(n^{1/3})
. The
\Omega(\sqrt{n})
lower bound in theorem
3.14
cannot be improved by more than
\mathrm{polylog}(n)
factors, due to Theorem
1.10
. In fact, the result of Theorem
1.10
implies that for some distribution-labeled
2
-graph
G
, there is a
\mathrm{polylog}(n)
-round protocol for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
with cost
\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{n})
as long as
\alpha K
is a sufficiently large constant.

Every communication protocol can be viewed as having a certain number of
communication rounds
, and in each round only one player broadcasts a message of certain length. Motivated by studying the space lower bounds of streaming algorithms with bounded number of passes, it is also of interest to consider how efficient protocols can be if we impose a limit on the number of communication rounds.

Definition 3.16
.

For any positive integer
r
, we define
\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K,r)
to be the minimum communication cost over all protocols
\Pi
that use at most
r
communication rounds and satisfy
\mathrm{adv}(\Pi)\geqslant 0.1
.

Recall from Remark
3.15
that the lower bound in Theorem
3.14
cannot be improved by much as long as we allow
\mathrm{polylog}(n)
rounds of communication. It is not clear, however, whether we can prove stronger lower bound for communication protocols with fewer rounds of communication. In this direction, we prove the following communication lower bound against protocols with bounded rounds of communication.

Theorem 3.17
.

Fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
, an integer
K>0
and a parameter
\alpha\in\big(0,2^{-20}N^{-10k}|\mathcal{V}|^{-2}\big]
. There exists a constant
\beta=\beta(G,\alpha,K)>0
such that
\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K,r)\geqslant\beta^{r}n
for any positive integer
r
.

Remark 3.18
.

Note that our two main results for the
\mathsf{DIHP}
games are incomparable. Theorem
3.14
gives an
\Omega(\sqrt{n})
lower bound regardless of rounds. Theorem
3.17
gives an almost linear lower bound when
r=o(\log n)
but only yields a trivial bound when
r\geqslant\log_{1/\beta}n
.

The proofs of the two theorems will occupy Sections
4
to
7
.

3.5
Streaming Lower Bounds

We are now ready to state the following lemma from
[
14
]
that transfers communication lower bounds to the streaming setting.
11
11

11

In
[
14
, Lemma 5.14]
, the stated memory lower bound for streaming is
(pmK)^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)
, without round limits in the communication complexity. However, the proof of the lemma given in
[
14
]
actually produces the lower bound
(pmK)^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K,pmK)
, because it transforms any
p
-pass streaming algorithm into a
pmK
round communication protocol.

Lemma 3.19

(
[
14
, Lemma 5.14]
)
.

Fix a nonempty
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instance
\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{V},(C_{1},\dots,C_{m}))
, and let
s:=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}
and
c:=\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
.
Then there exists a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
such that for any fixed error parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and constants

0<\alpha\leqslant(100k)^{-1}\varepsilon\qquad\text{and}\qquad K\geqslant 100\alpha^{-1}\varepsilon^{-2}N^{2k}\cdot|\mathcal{V}|\log|\Sigma|,

(3.1)

the following holds for sufficiently large
n
:

(1)

If
c<1
, then any
p
-pass algorithm for
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[c-\varepsilon,s+\varepsilon]
requires at least
(pmK)^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K,pmK)
bits of memory on input instances with
|\mathcal{V}|\cdot n
variables and at most
mK\cdot n
constraints.

(2)

If
c=1
, then any
p
-pass algorithm for
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[1,s+\varepsilon]
requires at least
(pmK)^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K,pmK)
bits of memory on input instances with
|\mathcal{V}|\cdot n
variables and at most
mK\cdot n
constraints.

It is now clear that Theorems
3.14
and
3.17
together imply Theorem
1.2
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
assuming Theorems
3.14
and
3.17
.

We pick constants
\alpha
and
K
such that

0<\alpha<\min\left\{(100k)^{-1}\varepsilon,\,2^{-20}N^{-10k}|\mathcal{V}|^{-2}\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad K\geqslant 100\alpha^{-1}\varepsilon^{-2}N^{2k}\cdot|\mathcal{V}|\log|\Sigma|.

By Theorem
3.14
we have

\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K,pmK)\geqslant\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)=\Omega_{\alpha,K}(\sqrt{n}).

Similarly, by Theorem
3.17
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K,pmK)\geqslant\beta(G,\alpha,K)^{pmK}\cdot n,

where
\beta(G,\alpha,K)
is some constant depends on
G,\alpha,K
. It then follows from Lemma
3.19
(1) that any
p
-pass algorithm for
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[c-\varepsilon,s+\varepsilon]
requires

\max\left\{\Omega_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{n}/p),\,n\cdot\exp(-O_{\varepsilon}(p))\right\}

bits of memory. The second statement in Theorem
1.2
similarly follows from Lemma
3.19
(2).
∎

4
Communication Lower Bound for DIHP

This section is devoted to the proofs of Theorems
3.14
and
3.17
. As in
[
13
,
14
]
, the argument follows the standard structure-vs.-randomness framework in communication complexity, and consists of the two main steps:

1.

Given a protocol
\Pi
with low communication cost, the first step is to decompose each rectangle induced by
\Pi
into smaller subrectangles.
Following
[
13
,
14
]
, we show that after this decomposition, most rectangles are “good”, in the sense that each encodes a well-structured piece of information together with a controlled form of pseudorandom noise.

A key difference from
[
13
,
14
]
is that in our setting the goodness of these rectangles must be established under two distinct sets of assumptions.
For the proof of Theorem
3.14
, we assume
|\Pi|=o(\sqrt{n})
.
In contrast, for the proof of Theorem
3.17
, we assume
|\Pi|=o(n)
and additionally require that
\Pi
uses few communication rounds.

The “decomposition lemma” needed under the first assumption was already established in
[
14
, Lemma 6.11]
.
Under the second set of assumptions, however, a new decomposition lemma is required; this is provided by Lemma
4.14
.

2.

The second step is to show for each “good” rectangle
R
a discrepancy bound of the form
12
12

12

Note that our discrepancy bound is
one-sided
, unlike the two-sided bound
|\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)-\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)|\leqslant 10^{-3}\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)
proved in
[
13
,
14
]
. We are content with one-sided bounds because they are sufficient for proving communication lower bounds.

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)\geqslant(1-10^{-3})\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R).

This is provided by our “discrepancy lemma,” Lemma
4.15
. The “goodness” assumption in our discrepancy lemma is weaker than its counterparts in
[
13
,
14
]
, which is the key reason we are able to obtain improved communication lower bounds.

These two steps are then combined to complete the proof of the communication lower bounds.

The new decomposition lemma (Lemma
4.14
) will be proved in Section
5
; its proof combines ideas in
[
22
, Section 5.2]
and
[
14
, Appendix A]
. The proof of the discrepancy lemma (Lemma
4.15
) will take up Sections
6
and
7
, where we combine ideas from
[
22
, Section 7]
and
[
14
, Section 7]
. This
section is devoted to laying out the overarching framework that connects these
components. In particular, we formalize the notion of “good” rectangles in Sections
4.1
and
4.2
.
Then, in Section
4.3
, we lay out the main lemmas, from which we derive the desired communication
lower bound in Section
4.4
.

4.1
Pseudorandomness Notions

A “good” rectangle is one in which structural information and pseudorandom part are
cleanly separated, with the information contained in the structural part being of bounded size. In this subsection, we formalize the notions of pseudorandomness for sets of
labeled matchings. This will allow us, in Section
4.2
, to control the pseudorandomness in rectangles.

Throughout this subsection, we fix a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}=(U_{1},\dots,U_{k})
and a positive integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
. We will consider pseudorandomness notions for the space of labeled matchings
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
.
Our notion is based on the following type of restriction on the space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
.

Definition 4.1
.

We define the set of
restrictions
to be
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}:=\bigcup_{0\leqslant d\leqslant m}\Omega^{\mathcal{U},d}
, i.e., the subset of
\mathrm{Map}\left(\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U},\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\cup\{\textup{{nil}}\}\right)
that consists of all labeled matchings with at most
m
edges. For each such labeled matching
\mathbf{z}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
, we let
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
be the restricted domain defined by

\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}:=\left\{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}:\mathbf{y}(e)=\mathbf{z}(e)\text{ for all }e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})\right\}.

For notational convenience, we use the following notion.

Notation 4.2
.

Fix a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
, a nonnegative integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
, and a restriction
\mathbf{z}
on the space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
. We denote by
\mathcal{U}_{\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})}
the
k
-universe
(U_{1}^{\prime},U_{2}^{\prime},\dots,U_{k}^{\prime})
defined by setting for each
i\in[k]

U_{i}^{\prime}:=U_{i}\setminus\left\{u:\text{some edge in $\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})$ has $u$ as its $i$-th vertex}\right\}.

We use
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m,\mathbf{z}}
as a shorthand for
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})},\,m-|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})|}
.

Before formalizing the main notion of pseudorandomness, we define the following convenient concept of subsumption of restrictions.

Definition 4.3
.

For two restrictions
\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
, we say
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}

subsumes

\mathbf{z}
if
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})
and for all
e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})
we have
\mathbf{z}(e)=\mathbf{z}^{\prime}(e)
.

We are now ready to define pseudorandomness for sets of labeled matchings:

Definition 4.4
.

A subset
A\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
is said to be
\mathbf{z}
-global
if
A\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}
, and for all restrictions
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
that subsume
\mathbf{z}
we have

\frac{\left|A\cap\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}\right|}{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}\right|}\leqslant 2^{|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})|-|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})|}\cdot\frac{\left|A\cap\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}\right|}{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}\right|}.

When
\mathbf{z}=\boldsymbol{0}
is the trivial restriction, we simply say
that
A
is global (omitting the
\mathbf{z}
).

In words, for a set
A
and a restriction
\mathbf{z}
, we say that
A
is

\mathbf{z}
-global if any further restrictions
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
that subsumes
\mathbf{z}
increases
the relative density of
A
by factor at most
2^{|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})|-|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})|}
.

Given the notion of globalness for labeled matchings defined in Definition
4.4
, it is natural to consider the following analogous pseudorandomness notion for
unlabeled
matchings:

Definition 4.5
.

A distribution
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
is said to be
pseudo-uniform
if for any nonnegative integer
d\leqslant m
and any partial matching
S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},d}
, we have

\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[S\subseteq M\right]\leqslant 2^{d}\cdot\underset{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[S\subseteq M\right].

Definitions
4.4
and
4.5
are related by the following observation.

Proposition 4.6
.

If
\mathbf{y}
is a uniformly random element of a global set
A\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, then the distribution of
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})
is pseudo-uniform.

Proof.

This follows from direct calculation:

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{y}\in A}{\mathbb{P}}\left[M\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{z}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},|M|}\\
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})=M\end{subarray}}\frac{\left|A\cap\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}\right|}{\left|A\right|}\leqslant 2^{|M|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{z}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},|M|}\\
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})=M\end{subarray}}\frac{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}\right|}{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|}

\displaystyle=2^{|M|}\cdot\underset{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[M\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})\right]\qed

The following corollary will be useful in Section
5.2
.

Corollary 4.7
.

Given a restriction
\mathbf{z}
on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
such that
|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})|\leqslant\min\{m,|\mathcal{U}|/2\}
and a
\mathbf{z}
-global set
A\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}
. For any matching
S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
containing
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})
, we have

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{y}\in A}{\mathbb{P}}\left[S\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})\right]\leqslant\left(\frac{6^{k}m}{|\mathcal{U}|^{k}}\right)^{|S|-|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})|}.

Proof.

When
\mathbf{y}
is a uniformly random element of
A
, the induced distribution of
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})
is a pseudo-uniform distribution over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m,\mathbf{z}}
since
A
is
\mathbf{z}
-global. Then, by Definition
4.5
, we have

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{y}\in A}{\mathbb{P}}\left[S\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\mathbf{y}\in A}{\mathbb{P}}\left[S\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{|S|-|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})|}\underset{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m,\mathbf{z}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[S\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})\subseteq M\right].

So, it suffices to prove

\displaystyle\underset{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m,\mathbf{z}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[S\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})\subseteq M\right]\leqslant\left(\frac{6^{k-1}m}{|\mathcal{U}|^{k}}\right)^{|S|-|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})|}.

The left-hand side of the above display is

\prod_{d=|\mathrm{supp}(z)|}^{|S|-1}\frac{m-d}{(|\mathcal{U}|-d)^{k}}\leqslant\left(\frac{m}{|\mathcal{U}|}\right)^{|S|-|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})|}\prod_{d=|\mathrm{supp}(z)|}^{|S|-1}\frac{1}{(|\mathcal{U}|-d)^{k-1}}\leqslant\left(\frac{6^{k-1}m}{|\mathcal{U}|^{k}}\right)^{|S|-|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})|}.\qed

4.2
“Good” Rectangles

Now, we turn to pseudorandomness notions for
rectangles
. In this subsection, we fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}]})
and a communication game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
.

Recall from
Definition

3.10
that in the communication game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
, the joint input to the
|\mathcal{E}|\cdot K
players is an element
\mathbf{Y}
in the product space
\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
. As is standard in communication complexity, a subset of this product space that is a Cartesian product is referred to as a
rectangle
, formally defined below.

Definition 4.8
.

A subset
R\subseteq\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
is called a
rectangle
if it is a Cartesian product of sets
A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
, one for each
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
; that is,

R=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}.

Then, it is natural to extend our definitions of global sets to rectangles, which requires each component
A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
to be a global set.

Definition 4.9
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
be a sequence where each
\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
is a restriction on the space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
. A rectangle
R=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
is called
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
-global
if each set
A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
is
\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
-global. When a rectangle
R
is
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
-global, we also say that the pair
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
is a
structured rectangle
.

In order for a structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
to be “good,” we need the restriction sequence
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
to satisfy additional properties. We would like to consider the following notion of
weight
of a restriction sequence, inspired by
[
22
, Definition 5.1]
:

Definition 4.10
.

Given a sequence of restriction
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
, consider the hypergraph
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
with vertex set
\mathcal{V}\times[n]
and edge set

\bigcup_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}).

Let
V_{1},\dots,V_{t}\subseteq\mathcal{V}\times[n]
be the list of nontrivial connected components of
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
. We define the
weight
of the restriction sequence
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
to be

\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|:=\sum_{i=1}^{t}|V_{i}|^{2}.

We will also need the definition of “cyclic” restriction sequences stated as follows.

Definition 4.11
.

A restriction sequence
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
is said to be
cyclic
if either the edge sets
\left(\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
are not pairwise disjoint, or the hypergraph
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
contains a cycle (for the definition of cycle-freeness in hypergraphs, see Section
2.1
).

The notion of weight defined above is crucial in the proof of the linear lower bound against protocols with bounded rounds of communication (Theorem
3.17
). Intuitively, the quantity
\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|
controls the “tendency” of
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
to become cyclic, as will be formalized in Lemma
5.4
. To get some intuition, let
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
be an acyclic restriction, and consider the probability that
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
forms a cycle when we add a uniformly random
k
-edge
e
to the graph
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
. It is not hard to see that
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
forms a cycle with
e
if and only if
e
intersects some connected component
V
of
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
on at least two vertices, which happens with probability
\approx\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|/n^{2}
.
13
13

13

In our application we care about the case of adding a (pseudo-)uniformly random matching of size
\Theta(n)
instead of adding a single edge. This is addressed formally in Lemma
5.4
by careful calculations.
The work
[
14
]
use the following cruder potential function to measure the tendency of
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
to become cyclic:

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{\zeta}|:=\sum_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\right|.

The probability that
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
forms a cycle with a random edge is approximately at most
|\boldsymbol{\zeta}|^{2}/n^{2}
, as
\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|\lesssim|\boldsymbol{\zeta}|^{2}
. To compare the two potential functions, consider the following two cases:

•

H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
is a graph of
t
connected edges, forming a connected component of size
\approx kt
; in this case,
|\boldsymbol{\zeta}|^{2}\approx\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|
, so the two bounds
|\boldsymbol{\zeta}|^{2}/n^{2}
and
\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|/n^{2}
are roughly the same.

•

H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
is a graph of
t
disjoint edges, forming
t
connected components each of size
k
; in this case,
|\boldsymbol{\zeta}|\approx\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|
, so the bound
\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|/n^{2}
is much tighter than
|\boldsymbol{\zeta}|^{2}/n^{2}
.

Provided that one expects
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
to have relatively small connected components, the above two examples suggest that
\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|
may be a more useful measure, and this is indeed the case.

We are now ready to give the formal definition of “good” rectangles. The rationale behind the three technical requirements in the following definition will become clear in
Section

6
.

Definition 4.12
.

Let
W_{1},W_{2}
be positive real numbers. We say a structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
, where
R=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
, is
(W_{1},W_{2})
-good if the following conditions hold:

(1)

The restriction sequence
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is not cyclic.

(2)

\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right|/\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\right|\geqslant 2^{-W_{1}}
for all
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
.

(3)

The weight of
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is at most
W_{2}
, i.e.
\lVert\boldsymbol{\zeta}\rVert\leqslant W_{2}
.

4.3
Three Main Lemmas

We now present the two decomposition lemmas and the discrepancy lemma promised in the introductory text of Section
4
. The following decomposition lemma needed for proving Theorem
3.14
is already proved in
[
14
, Appendix A]
.

Lemma 4.13

(First decomposition lemma,
[
14
, Lemma 6.11]
)
.

Fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
, an integer
K>0
and a parameter
\alpha\in(0,1)
. There exists a constant
\eta>0
such that given any communication protocol
\Pi
for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
with
|\Pi|\leqslant\eta\sqrt{n}
