, there exists a collection
\mathcal{R}
of pairwise-disjoint structured rectangles
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
in the space
\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
such that the following conditions hold:

(1)

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}\left(\bigcup_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}}R\right)\geqslant 0.99
.

(2)

Each
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}
is
\left(10^{5}|\Pi|,10^{10}k^{2}|\Pi|^{2}\right)
-good.
14
14

14

Note that the goodness condition here contains the requirement
\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|\leqslant 10^{10}K^{2}|\Pi|^{2}
. In the original statement of
[
14
, Lemma 6.11]
, this particular requirement is replaced by the stronger assumption
\sum_{(\mathsf{e},j)}|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})|\leqslant 10^{5}|\Pi|
(while all other components of the goodness condition are unchanged). Since the bound
\sum_{(\mathsf{e},j)}|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})|\leqslant 10^{5}|\Pi|
clearly implies
\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|\leqslant 10^{10}K^{2}|\Pi|^{2}
, the original result of
[
14
, Lemma 6.11]
directly implies our Lemma
4.13
.

(3)

For each
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}
, there exists
a_{R}\in\{0,1\}
such that
\Pi(\mathbf{Y})=a_{R}
for every
\mathbf{Y}\in R
.

The next decomposition lemma, which is needed for Theorem
3.17
, will be proved in Section
5
.

Lemma 4.14

(Second decomposition lemma)
.

Fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
, an integer
K>0
and a parameter
\alpha\in(0,1)
. There exist constants
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in(0,1)
(depending only on
G,\alpha
and
K
) such that given any
r
-round communication protocol
\Pi
for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
with
|\Pi|\leqslant\eta_{1}^{r}n
, there exists a collection
\mathcal{R}
of pairwise-disjoint structured rectangles
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
in the space
\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
such that the following conditions hold:

(1)

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}\left(\bigcup_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}}R\right)\geqslant 0.99
.

(2)

Each
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}
is
\left(\eta_{2}^{-r}|\Pi|,\eta_{2}^{-r}|\Pi|\right)
-good.

(3)

For each
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}
, there exists
a_{R}\in\{0,1\}
such that
\Pi(\mathbf{Y})=a_{R}
for every
\mathbf{Y}\in R
.

The following discrepancy lemma is needed for proving both Theorem
3.14
and Theorem
3.17
.

Lemma 4.15

(Discrepancy lemma)
.

Fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
, an integer
K>0
and a parameter
\alpha\in\left(0,2^{-20}N^{-10k}|\mathcal{V}|^{-2}\right]
. There exists a constant
\gamma\in(0,1)
such that for any
(\gamma n,\gamma n)
-good structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)\geqslant(1-10^{-3})\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R).

The proof of Lemma
4.15
will take up Sections
6
and
7
.

4.4
Communication Lower Bounds

Assuming the lemmas in Section
4.3
, we can now prove Theorems
3.14
and
3.17
.

Proof of Theorem
3.14
.

We apply Lemmas
4.13
and
4.15
to obtain constants
\eta
and
\gamma
, respectively. We will show that any protocol
\Pi
for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
with

|\Pi|\leqslant\min\left\{\eta,10^{-10}k^{-2}\gamma\right\}\cdot\sqrt{n}

(4.1)

must have
\mathrm{adv}(\Pi)<0.1
.

We apply Lemma
4.13
to
\Pi
, and let
\mathcal{R}
be the collection of structured rectangles obtained. By (
4.1
) and conclusion (2) of Lemma
4.13
, we know that each structured rectangle in
\mathcal{R}
is
(\gamma n,\gamma n)
-good. It then follows from Lemma
4.15
that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)\geqslant(1-10^{-3})\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)
for each
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}
.

By conclusion (3) of Lemma
4.13
, the function

\Pi:\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}\longrightarrow\{0,1\}

is constant on
R
for each
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}
. Let
\mathcal{R}_{1}
be the collection of all structured rectangles in
\mathcal{R}
on which
\Pi
evaluates to 1. Let
p_{R}:=\min\{\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R),\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\}
for each
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}
, and define

p_{(1)}:=\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}_{1}}p_{R},\qquad p_{(0)}:=\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{1}}p_{R},\qquad\text{and }p_{(*)}:=p_{(0)}+p_{(1)}.

We thus have

p_{(*)}=\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}}p_{R}\geqslant\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}}(1-10^{-3})\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\geqslant(1-10^{-3})\cdot 0.99>0.9,

and hence

p_{(1)}\leqslant\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\Pi(\mathbf{Y})=1\right]\leqslant 1-p_{(0)}<p_{(*)}+0.1-p_{(0)}=p_{(1)}+0.1.

Similarly,
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}}\left[\Pi(\mathbf{Y})=1\right]
also lies in the interval
\big[p_{(1)},p_{(1)}+0.1\big)
, and therefore by Definition
3.12
we have
\mathrm{adv}(\Pi)<0.1
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.17
.

We apply Lemmas
4.14
and
4.15
to obtain constants
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
and
\gamma
, respectively. We show that any
r
-round protocol
\Pi
for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
with
|\Pi|\leqslant\min\{\eta_{1}^{r},\eta_{2}^{r}\gamma\}\cdot n
must have
\mathrm{adv}(\Pi)<0.1
.

We apply Lemma
4.14
to
\Pi
, and let
\mathcal{R}
be the collection of structured rectangles obtained. By the upper bound on
|\Pi|
and conclusion (2) of Lemma
4.14
, we know that each structured rectangle in
\mathcal{R}
is
(\gamma n,\gamma n)
-good. The rest of the proof is identical to the proof of Theorem
3.14
above.
∎

5
The Decomposition Lemma

In this section, we prove the second decomposition lemma, Lemma
4.14
. The proof is similar to that of the first decomposition lemma in
[
14
, Appendix A]
, consisting of two steps:

1.

We first transform an arbitrary protocol into a
global protocol
— a protocol where roughly speaking, every message broadcasted by a player corresponds to structured rectangles. In the proof of Lemma
4.14
, this first step is essentially the same as its counterpart in
[
14
, Sections A.1 and A.2]
, which we will elaborate on in Section
5.1
.

2.

The second step (carried out in Sections
5.2
and
5.3
) is to show that in the division of the joint input space resulting from a global protocol, most structured rectangles are good (in the sense of Definition
4.12
). This step deviates from
[
14
, Appendix A]
because here we need to keep track of the
weight
of the restriction sequences of structured rectangles (as defined in Definition
4.10
), due to the condition (3) in Definition
4.12
. Our argument here is inspired by the analysis of the “component growing protocol” in
[
22
, Section 5.2]
.

Throughout this section, we fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
along with an integer
K>0
and a parameter
\alpha>0
.

5.1
Transforming Protocols into Global Protocols

Before formalizing the notion of “global protocol” in Definition
5.2
, we introduce the following potential function to quantify the amount of information during the communication process.

Definition 5.1
.

For restrictions
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
and a rectangle
R=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
such that
A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},m}_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}
, we define the potential of
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
as:

\displaystyle\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R):=\sum_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\right|+\log_{2}\left(\frac{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}^{(i)}}\right|}{|A^{(i)}|}\right).

Definition 5.2
.

A communication protocol
\Pi
for
\textsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
is called an
(r,c)
-round global communication protocol if it specifies the following procedure of communications:

•

the
K|\mathcal{E}|
players take turns to send messages according to
\Pi
;

•

there are at most
r
rounds of communication, and there is only one player sending a message in a single round;

•

the length of message in each round of communication is not bounded; instead, from the perspective of rectangles, after each round of communications, a
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
-global rectangle
R
is further partitioned into a disjoint union of rectangles
R_{(1)},\dots,R_{(\ell)}
such that: (1)
R_{(i)}
is
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}
-global; (2)
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}
subsumes
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
; (3) the following inequality holds:

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{|R_{(i)}|}{|R|}\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)},R_{(i)})\leqslant\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)+3c.

(5.1)

Note that in global protocols, the “communication cost” is measured by the increase of (average) potential instead of total number of bits broadcasted.

The following lemma is slightly different from
[
14
, Lemma A.4]
, but its proof is the same as the one provided in
[
14
, Section A.2]
and is thus omitted here.

Lemma 5.3
.

Given an
r
-round communication protocol
\Pi
for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
, we can construct an
(r,|\Pi|)
-round global protocol
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
such that for any leaf rectangle
R
of
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
, the output of
\Pi
is constant on
R
.

5.2
A Global Protocol Grows Components Slowly

Kapralov and Krachun
[
22
]
observed that, informally, a single-pass fully-structured
15
15

15

That is, a one-way global protocol in which every communicated message consists solely of structured information, with no pseudorandom noise.
protocol for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
can be viewed as follows: each player reveals a subset of edges from their labeled matching, with the collective goal of forming a cycle using the revealed edges.

To show that such fully structured protocols are unlikely to succeed, Kapralov and Krachun demonstrated in
[
22
, Section 5.2]
that the connected components in the (hyper)graph induced by the revealed edges cannot grow rapidly. In this subsection, we develop an analogous argument for
general global protocols
, rather than single-pass fully-structured ones.

Lemma 5.4
.

Suppose that in one round of communication of an
(r,c)
-round global protocol for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
, a structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
is partitioned (according to the speaking player’s message) into the disjoint union of structured rectangles

(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(1)},R_{(1)}),(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(2)},R_{(2)})\dots,(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(\ell)},R_{(\ell)}).

We assume that
\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|\leqslant 6^{-k-1}n
. Then we have

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{|R_{(i)}|}{|R|}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}\|\leqslant k^{2}\big(2\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|+\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)+3c\big),

(5.2)

and if (in addition)
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is not cyclic (as per Definition
4.11
), we also have

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{|R_{(i)}|}{|R|}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}\text{ is cyclic}\right\}\leqslant\frac{6^{k+1}\alpha\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|}{n}.

(5.3)

Proof.

We divide the proof into the following three steps.

Step 1: understanding the weight increment.

We write

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)},\qquad

\displaystyle R_{(i)}

\displaystyle=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}_{(i)}\text{ for each }i\in[\ell],

(5.4)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\zeta}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]},\qquad

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}_{(i)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\text{ for each }i\in[\ell].

(5.5)

Suppose
(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
is the player who speaks at the current communication round. Then we have
A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}=A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}_{(i)}
and
\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}=\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}_{(i)}
for all
(\mathsf{e},j)\neq(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})
and all
i\in[K]
.

Recalling the notation in Definition
4.10
, let
V_{1},\dots,V_{t}\subseteq\mathcal{V}\times[n]
denote the nontrivial connected components of the hypergraph
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
. For each
i\in[\ell]
, the hypergraph
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}}
is obtained from
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
by adding the edges in the set

E_{(i)}:=\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}_{(i)}\right)\setminus\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}\right).

(5.6)

Note that
E_{(i)}
is a matching in the family
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}^{*}},\leqslant\alpha n}
. Therefore, if
V^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}\times[n]
is a nontrivial connected component of
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}}
, then either
V^{\prime}
spans exactly one edge in
E_{(i)}
, in which case
|V^{\prime}|=k
, or
V^{\prime}
contains
V_{q}
for some
q\in[t]
, in which case we have
16
16

16

To see this, note that there are at most
\sum_{q\in[t],\,V_{q}\subseteq V^{\prime}}|V_{q}|
edges of
E_{(i)}
lying within
V^{\prime}
, each accounting for at most
(k-1)
vertices in
V^{\prime}\setminus(\bigcup_{q\in[t],\,V_{q}\subseteq V^{\prime}}V_{q})
.

|V^{\prime}|\leqslant k\cdot\sum_{q\in[t],\;V_{q}\subseteq V^{\prime}}|V_{q}|.

For any
q,s\in[t]
and any
i\in[\ell]
, we define

X_{q,s,i}:=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }V_{q}\text{ and }V_{s}\text{ belong to the same connected component in }H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}},\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(5.7)

From the discussion in the last paragraph, it is easy to deduce that for each
i\in[\ell]
, we have

\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}\|\leqslant k^{2}\left|E_{(i)}\right|+k^{2}\sum_{q,s\in[t]}X_{q,s,i}|V_{q}||V_{s}|.

(5.8)

Step 2: using the globalness condition.

For each positive integer
d
and each pair of indices
q,s\in[t]
, let
S(q,s,d)
be the collection of vertex sequences
(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{2d})\in(\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}^{*}})^{2d}
that satisfy the following conditions:

(1)

We have
v_{1}\in V_{q}
and
v_{2d}\in V_{s}
.

(2)

For any
j\in[d]
, the vertices
v_{2j-1}
and
v_{2j}
are distinct.

(3)

For any
j\in[d-1]
, there exists
i\in[t]
such that
v_{2j},v_{2j+1}\in V_{i}
.

Let
S(q,s)=\bigcup_{d=1}^{+\infty}S(q,s,d)
. Note that the cardinality of the sets
S(q,s,d)
can be bounded by

|S(q,s,d)|\leqslant|V_{q}||V_{s}|\cdot\left(\sum_{i=1}^{t}|V_{i}|^{2}\right)^{d-1}=|V_{q}||V_{s}|\cdot\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|^{d-1}.

(5.9)

A matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}^{*}},d}
is called an
exact cover
of a sequence
(v_{1},\dots,v_{2d})\in S(q,s,d)
if the edges of
M
can be listed as
e_{1},\dots,e_{d}
such that
e_{j}
contains
v_{2j-1}
and
v_{2j}
for all
j\in[d]
. The number of exact covers of a given sequence in
S(q,s,d)
is clearly at most
n^{(k-2)d}
. By definitions (
5.6
) and (
5.7
), for any distinct
q,s\in[t]
and any
i\in[\ell]
, if
X_{q,s,i}=1
then
E_{(i)}
contains an exact cover of a sequence in
S(q,s)
. Furthermore, if
E_{(i)}
contains an exact cover of a sequence in
S(q,s)
, then for all
\mathbf{y}\in A^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}_{(i)}
, the matching
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})\setminus\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}\right)
contains an exact cover of a sequence in
S(q,s)
. Therefore, for any distinct
q,s\in[t]
we have

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{|R_{(i)}|}{|R|}\cdot X_{q,s,i}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}_{(i)}\right|}{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}\right|}\cdot X_{q,s,i}

\displaystyle\leqslant\underset{\mathbf{y}\in A^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})\setminus\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}\right)\text{ contains an exact cover of a sequence in }S(q,s)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{d=1}^{+\infty}|S(q,s,d)|\cdot n^{(k-2)d}\cdot\left(\frac{6^{k}\alpha n}{n^{k}}\right)^{d}

(using Corollary
4.7
and
|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})})|\leqslant\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|\leqslant n/2
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{6^{k}\alpha}{n}\cdot|V_{q}||V_{s}|\cdot\sum_{d=1}^{+\infty}\left(\frac{6^{k}\alpha\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|}{n}\right)^{d-1}\leqslant\frac{6^{k+1}\alpha}{n}\cdot|V_{q}||V_{s}|.

(using (
5.9
) and
\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|\leqslant 6^{-k-1}n
)

Plugging this into (
5.8
) yields

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{|R_{(i)}|}{|R|}\cdot\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant k^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q,s\in[t]\\
q=s\end{subarray}}|V_{q}||V_{s}|+\frac{6^{k+1}k^{2}\alpha}{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q,s\in[t]\\
q\neq s\end{subarray}}|V_{q}|^{2}|V_{s}|^{2}+k^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{|R_{(i)}|}{|R|}\cdot\left|E_{(i)}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant k^{2}\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|+\frac{6^{k+1}k^{2}\alpha}{n}\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|^{2}+k^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{|R_{(i)}|}{|R|}\cdot\left|\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}_{(i)}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant 2k^{2}\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|+k^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{|R_{(i)}|}{|R|}\cdot\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)},R_{(i)})\leqslant k^{2}\big(2\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|+\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)+3c\big),

where we used (
5.1
) in the last transition. This proves the inequality (
5.2
).

Step 3: understanding cyclicity.

In the rest of the proof, we assume
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is not cyclic as in part (2) of the Lemma. For any fixed
i\in[\ell]
, we claim that assuming
E_{(i)}
does not contain an exact cover of any sequence in
\bigcup_{q\in[t]}S(q,q)
, the restriction sequence
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}
cannot be cyclic. Since
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is not cyclic, it suffices to prove that
E_{(i)}
does not contain any edge in the hypergraph
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
, and
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}}
does not have cycles. The former statement follows easily from the assumption, since any edge in the hypergraph
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
is an exact cover of a sequence in
\bigcup_{q\in[t]}S(q,q,1)
. We next focus on the latter statement.

Let
E=\bigcup_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})
be the edge set of the hypergraph
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
. We run a breadth-first search (BFS) on the hypergraph
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}}
(whose edge set is
E\cup E_{(i)}
) and rank all edges in
E\cup E_{(i)}
by the time they are discovered in the BFS. This yields a total order on
E\cup E_{(i)}
such that each hyperedge
e\in E\cup E_{(i)}
is incident to at most one vertex that is covered by some hyperedge preceding
e
in the order. Indeed, if some hyperedge
e
violates this condition, then by the time the BFS discovers
e
, it has also found a cycle of distinct hyperedges
e=e_{0},e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{d-1}\in E\cup E_{(i)}
and distinct vertices
v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{d-1}\in\mathcal{V}\times[n]
such that
e_{i}
is incident to both
v_{i}
and
v_{i+1\pmod{d}}
, for each
i\in\{0,1,\dots,d-1\}
. Then the collection of edges in
\{e_{0},e_{1}\dots,e_{d-1}\}
that are from
E_{(i)}
is an exact cover of a sequence in
\bigcup_{q\in[t]}S(q,q)
, contradicting the assumption. Therefore, we have a total order on the edge set of
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}}
such that each edge is incident to at most one vertex that is covered by edges preceding it. It is then easy to see that
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}}
is cycle-free (see
Section

2.1
for the definition of cycle-freeness in hypergraphs).

We can now calculate

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{|R_{(i)}|}{|R|}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}\text{ is cyclic}\right\}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}_{(i)}\right|}{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}\right|}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}\text{ is cyclic}\right\}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}_{(i)}\right|}{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}\right|}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{E_{(i)}\text{ contains an exact over of a sequence in }\bigcup\nolimits_{q\in[t]}S(q,q)\right\}

\displaystyle\leqslant\underset{\mathbf{y}\in A^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})\setminus\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}\right)\text{ contains an exact cover of a sequence in }\bigcup\nolimits_{q\in[t]}S(q,q)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{q=1}^{t}\sum_{d=1}^{+\infty}|S(q,q,d)|\cdot n^{(k-2)d}\cdot\left(\frac{6^{k}\alpha n}{n^{k}}\right)^{d}

(using Corollary
4.7
and
|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})})|\leqslant\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|\leqslant n/2
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{d=1}^{+\infty}\left(\frac{6^{k}\alpha\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|}{n}\right)^{d}\leqslant\frac{6^{k+1}\alpha\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|}{n}.

(using (
5.9
) and
\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|\leqslant 6^{-k-1}n
)

This proves the inequality (
5.3
) (under the assumption that
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is not cyclic).
∎

5.3
Most Rectangles in a Global Protocol are “Good”

As mentioned in the introductory text to Section
5
, our next goal is to show that most structured rectangles produced by a global protocol are good. This is achieved in Lemma
5.7
below, for which the following two definitions provide some convenient notations.

Definition 5.5
.

Let
\Pi
be an
r
-round global protocol.
For each
0\leqslant d\leqslant r
, we let
\mathcal{R}^{d}(\Pi)
denote the collection of structured rectangles obtained after
d
rounds of communication in
\Pi
.
In particular, we write
\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{leaf}}(\Pi)=\mathcal{R}^{r}(\Pi)
for the set of structured rectangles at the leaves of the protocol tree.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
\Pi
be a global protocol for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
and let
W
be a positive real number. We define

\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{bad}}(\Pi,W)
as the following set of structured rectangles:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{bad}}(\Pi,W):=\left\{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{leaf}}(\Pi):\text{$(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)$ is not $(W,W)$-good as per \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:good_rec_2}}\right\}.

Lemma 5.7
.

For any fixed distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G
, integer
K>0
and parameter
\alpha\in(0,1)
, there exist constants
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}>0
such that for any
(r,c)
-round global protocol
\Pi
for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
such that
c\leqslant\eta_{1}^{-r}n
, we have

\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{bad}}(\Pi,\eta_{2}^{-r}c)}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\leqslant 0.01.

(5.10)

We observe that the desired
Lemma

4.14
(restated below) follows immediately from
Lemma

5.7
.

See
4.14

Proof of Lemma
4.14
assuming Lemma
5.7
.

Let
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
be the constants obtained from Lemma
5.7
. We apply Lemma
5.3
to transform
\Pi
into an
(r,|\Pi|)
-round global protocol
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
. We claim that

\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{leaf}}(\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}})\setminus\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{bad}}(\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}},\eta_{2}^{-r}c)

satisfies the three conditions stated in the lemma.

The second condition follows directly from Definition
5.6
, and the third condition follows from the guarantee of Lemma
5.3
.
The first condition follows from Lemma
5.7
together with the obvious identity

\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{leaf}}(\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}})}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)=1.\qed

We are now ready to prove Lemma
5.7
.

Proof of Lemma
5.7
.

We let
\eta_{2}=10^{-5}k^{-2}
; the value of
\eta_{1}
will be determined later. For each joint input
\mathbf{Y}\in\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
and each nonnegative integer
d\leqslant r
, we let
\big(\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},d),R(\mathbf{Y},d)\big)
be the unique structured rectangle in
\mathcal{R}^{d}(\Pi)
that contains
\mathbf{Y}
. Note that the support of each component of
\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},d)
is non-decreasing in
d
.

In the rest of the proof, we use
\mathbf{Y}
to denote a uniformly random element of the joint input space
\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
, i.e. we have
\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}
. Note that the desired conclusion (
5.10
) is equivalent to

\underset{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\big(\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},r),R(\mathbf{Y},r)\big)\text{ is not }(\eta_{2}^{-r}c,\eta_{2}^{-r}c)\text{-good}\right]\leqslant 0.01.

(5.11)

Recall from Definition
4.12
that a structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
with

R=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
is not
(\eta_{2}^{-r}c,\eta_{2}^{-r}c)
-good only if one of the following holds:

(1)

For some
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
we have
\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right|\leqslant 2^{-\eta_{2}^{-r}c}\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\right|
. In this case, by Definition
5.1
we also have
\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\geqslant\eta_{2}^{-r}c
.

(2)

The weight of
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is at least
\eta_{2}^{-r}c
, i.e.
\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|\geqslant\eta_{2}^{-r}c
.

(3)

The restriction sequence
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is cyclic.

Bounding potential.

By Definition
5.2
, for any
d\in[r]
we have (with probability 1)

\underset{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\phi\big(\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},d),R(\mathbf{Y},d)\big)\,\Big|\,\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},d-1),R(\mathbf{Y},d-1)\right]\leqslant\phi\big(\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},d-1),R(\mathbf{Y},d-1)\big)+3c.

Taking expectation over
\mathbf{Y}
and taking sum over
d
yields

\underset{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\phi\big(\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},d),R(\mathbf{Y},d)\big)\Big.\right]\leqslant 3dc\qquad\text{for any }d\in[r],

(5.12)

and by Markov’s inequality we have

\underset{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\phi\big(\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},r),R(\mathbf{Y},r)\big)\geqslant\eta_{2}^{-r}c\Big.\right]\leqslant 3r\cdot\eta_{2}^{r}\leqslant 10^{-3}.

(5.13)

Bounding weight.

By Lemma
5.4
, for any
d\in[r]
we have (with probability 1)

\displaystyle\quad\underset{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\min\left\{\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},d)\|,6^{-k-1}n\right\}\,\Big|\,\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},d-1),R(\mathbf{Y},d-1)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant k^{2}\left(2\min\left\{\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},d-1)\|,6^{-k-1}n\right\}+\phi\big(\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},d-1),R(\mathbf{Y},d-1)\big)+3c\right).

Taking expectation over
\mathbf{Y}
, applying (
5.12
), and inducting over
d
, we get

\underset{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\min\left\{\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},s)\|,6^{-k-1}n\right\}\right]\leqslant\sum_{d=1}^{s}3dc\cdot(2k^{2})^{s+1-d}\leqslant(10k^{2})^{s}c\qquad\text{for any }s\in[r].

(5.14)

By Markov’s inequality we have

\underset{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},r)\|\geqslant\eta_{2}^{-r}c\right]\leqslant(10\eta_{2}k^{2})^{r}\leqslant 10^{-3}

(5.15)

as long as
\eta_{2}^{-r}c\leqslant 6^{-k-1}n
, which we can easily guarantee since
c\leqslant\eta_{1}^{r}n
and we can choose
\eta_{1}
to be sufficiently small.

Probability of cyclicity.

By Lemma
5.4
, for any
d\in[r]
we have (with probability 1)

\displaystyle\quad\underset{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},d)\text{ is cyclic but }\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},d-1)\text{ is not cyclic}\,\Big|\,\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},d-1),R(\mathbf{Y},d-1)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{6^{k+1}}{n}\min\left\{\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},d-1)\|,6^{-k-1}n\right\}.

Taking expectation over
\mathbf{Y}
, applying (
5.14
) and taking union bound over
d
, we get

\underset{\mathbf{Y}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\mathbf{Y},r)\text{ is cyclic}\right]\leqslant\frac{6^{k+1}}{n}\cdot(10k^{2})^{r}c\leqslant 10^{-3}

(5.16)

as long as
c\leqslant 6^{-k-5}(10k^{2})^{-r}n
, which we can easily guarantee since
c\leqslant\eta_{1}^{r}n
and we can choose
\eta_{1}
to be sufficiently small.

Combining (
5.13
), (
5.15
) and (
5.16
), we conclude that (
5.11
) holds if
\eta_{1}
is a small enough constant.
∎

6
One-Sided Discrepancy of Good Rectangles

The goal of this section is to prove the discrepancy lemma (Lemma
4.15
), modulo a Fourier analytic lemma that we prove in Section
7
. We begin with a high-level overview of the proof strategy.

Recall that in
Lemma

4.15
, we are given a restriction sequence
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
and a
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
-global rectangle
R=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
. We will define a probability density function
f^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
on
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
, induced by the set
A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
. As is shown in
[
14
, Section 7.1]
, the quantities
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)
can be related by the identity (see Lemma
6.5
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)=\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\cdot\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}f^{(\mathsf{e},j)}(x)\right].

(6.1)

Thus, it suffices to show that the expectation of the product
\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)}f^{(\mathsf{e},j)}(x)
is not much smaller than 1.

To analyze each function
f^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
(associated to a single player), it is shown in
[
14
, Section 7.2]
that
f^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
can be decomposed as the product of two functions, which represent the “structure part” and “pseudorandom part” of
f^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
, respectively. In Section
6.2
, we will present the same decomposition as in
[
14
]
but using a slightly different formalism.

It is after this decomposition that our strategy diverges from
[
14
]
.

In
[
14
]
, the analytical tool of
global hypercontractivity
is used to obtain
L^{2}
-control over (the Fourier-level weights of) the “pseudorandom part” functions. Although this
L^{2}
-control is, in a sense, nearly optimal on its own, the analysis of
[
14
]
17
17

17

For the specific case of Max-Cut, the paper
[
13
]
combines the pseudorandom-part and structure-part analyses in a slightly different manner, which, however, suffers the same loss as in
[
14
]
.
incurs a significant loss when integrating the pseudorandom-part control with the structure-part information.
18
18

18

Consequently, both
[
14
]
and
[
13
]
obtain only
\Omega(n^{1/3})
communication lower bounds.

It turns out that the “structure” inherent in the structure-part functions is more optimally captured through the lens of
Fourier
\ell^{1}
-norm
than via
L^{2}
-based controls. However, maintaining Fourier
\ell^{1}
-norm controls for
products
of multiple functions is a much more intricate task than in the
L^{2}
world. Notably, this challenge was successfully addressed in
[
22
]
in the context of Max-Cut. In Section
6.5
, we demonstrate how to preserve Fourier
\ell^{1}
-norm control through an induction lemma (Lemma
6.22
, the proof of which is deferred to Section
7
), resembling the approach of
[
22
]
. Before that, we lay out some Fourier-analytic framework in Section
6.3
, and in Section
6.4
we use the framework to analyze products of structure-part functions.

6.1
Relating YES and NO Distributions

In this subsection, we introduce a formula from
[
14
]
that expresses the ratio
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)/\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)
as the expectation of a product of density functions.

Since the YES distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}
is defined from the Markov kernel in Definition
3.5
, the main task is to analyze that Markov kernel. As demonstrated in
[
14
]
, it is convenient to view Markov kernels as
pull-back
linear operators that map functions on the target space to functions on the source space.

Notation 6.1
.

Fix a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
, a nonnegative integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
, and a one-wise independent distribution
\mu
over
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
. The (right) stochastic matrix
\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}\times\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\to\mathbb{R}
, defined in
Definition

3.5
, can be viewed as a linear operator

\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}:L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m})\to L^{2}\big(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}\big)

given by

\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[f](x)=\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}}\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}(x,\mathbf{y})f(\mathbf{y}),

for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
and
f\in L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m})
.

We denote this pull-back operator by the italic bold symbol
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[\cdot]
, distinguishing it from the matrix expression
\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}(\cdot,\cdot)
to reflect that, while formally distinct, the two represent the same underlying Markov transition.

The operator
\boldsymbol{P}_{\mu}^{\mathcal{U},m}
satisfies the following two basic properties (see
[
14
, Section 7.1]
for their proofs).

Proposition 6.2
.

For any
f\in L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m})
, we always have
\left\|\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[f]\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant\|f\|_{\infty}
.

Proposition 6.3
.

The operator
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}
maps a density function on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
to a density function on
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
.

The following notation will be useful throughout this section.

Notation 6.4
.

Given a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
, a nonnegative integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
, and a nonempty set
A\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, the density function of the uniform distribution on
A
is denoted by
\phi_{A}:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
, specifically defined as

\phi_{A}(\mathbf{y}):=\begin{cases}\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|/|A|,&\text{if }\mathbf{y}\in A,\\
0,&\text{if }\mathbf{y}\not\in A.\end{cases}

We remark that the density function
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]
corresponds to taking the joint distribution of
(x,\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}\times\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
in the Markov transition (described in Definition
3.5
), conditioning on the event
\{\mathbf{y}\in A\}
, and then taking the marginal distribution of
x
.

We are now ready to present the formula for
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)/\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)
from
[
14
]
.

Lemma 6.5

(
[
14
]
, Lemma 7.5)
.

Fix a
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
communication game, where
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
. Given a rectangle
R=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
, where
A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
, we have

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)=\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\cdot\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}\Big[\phi_{A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\Big]\circ\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}}(x)\right].

6.2
Separating Structured and Pseudorandom Parts

As mentioned earlier, the goal of this subsection is to express each function

\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}\Big[\phi_{A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\Big]\circ\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}},\qquad\text{for }(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]

(6.2)

as the product of two functions: the structured part and the pseudorandom part. We start with the definition of the structured part.

Definition 6.6
.

Fix a
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
communication game, where
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
. Let
\mathbf{z}
be a restriction on the space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, and an edge
\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}
. We define a density function
g_{\mathsf{e},\mathbf{z}}:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
by

g_{\mathsf{e},\mathbf{z}}(x):=\prod_{e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})}N^{k}\mu_{\mathsf{e}}\big(x_{|e}-\mathbf{z}(e)\big).

Next, we define the pseudorandom part. For that purpose, we introduce another Markov kernel closely related to the one in Definition
3.5
. While the Markov transition in Definition
3.5
samples a labeled matching
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
based on an input
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
, it is also natural to fix a matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
and sample a labeling of
M
based on an input
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
(or an
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
, if
\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U}\subseteq\Lambda
), as captured by the next definition.

Definition 6.7
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
k
-universe embedded in a finite set
\Lambda
(recall Notation
3.8
). Fix a one-wise independent distribution
\mu
over
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
and a matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
of size
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
. We define a right stochastic matrix
\mathbf{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}\times\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
by

\mathbf{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}(x,\boldsymbol{\xi}):=\prod_{e\in M}\mu\big(x_{|e}-\boldsymbol{\xi}(e)\big).

We may then define a linear operator
\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}:L^{2}\big(\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)\big)\rightarrow L^{2}\big(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}\big)
as in Notation
6.1
.

Analogously to Propositions
6.2
and
6.9
, the operator
\boldsymbol{R}_{\mu}^{\Lambda,M}
satisfies the following two basic properties.

Proposition 6.8
.

For any
f\in L^{2}\big(\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)\big)
, we always have
\left\|\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[f]\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant\|f\|_{\infty}
.

Proposition 6.9
.

The operator
\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}
maps a density function on the space
\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
to a density function on
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
.

In order to obtain a formal relation between the Markov operator
\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}
and
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}
, we define a canonical embedding map in Definition
6.10
. The following notations prepare for the definition.

Definition 6.10
.

Fix a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
and a nonnegative integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
. For any restriction
\mathbf{z}
on the space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
and any matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m,\mathbf{z}}
, there is a canonical embedding

\mathfrak{i}_{M,\mathbf{z}}:\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)\xhookrightarrow{\phantom{mmm}}\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}.

This embedding proceeds by mapping any
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
to a labeled matching
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}
defined by

1.

\mathbf{y}(e)=\mathbf{z}(e)
for
e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})
,

2.

\mathbf{y}(e)=\boldsymbol{\xi}(e)
for
e\in M
,

3.

\mathbf{y}(e)=\textup{{nil}}
for all other
e\in\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U}
.

Note that, as
M
ranges over all matchings in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m,\mathbf{z}}
, the images of the maps
\mathfrak{i}_{M,\mathbf{z}}
form a partition of the space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
.

We are now ready to present the separation of the function (
6.2
) into structured and pseudorandom parts.

Lemma 6.11
.

Fix a
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
communication game, where
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
, and fix an edge
\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}
. Given a restriction
\mathbf{z}
on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
and a
\mathbf{z}
-global set
A\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mathbf{z}}
, for any
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
we have

\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}[\phi_{A}]\big(\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}}(x)\big)=g_{\mathsf{e},\mathbf{z}}(x)\sum_{M}\frac{\left|\mathfrak{i}_{M,\mathbf{z}}^{-1}(A)\right|}{|A|}\boldsymbol{R}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n],\,M}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}\left[\phi_{\mathfrak{i}_{M,\mathbf{z}}^{-1}(A)}\right](x),

(6.3)

where the sum is over all matchings
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n,\mathbf{z}}
.

Proof.

For elements
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
and
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},m}_{\mathbf{z}}
, it is easy to deduce from Definition
3.5
that

\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}(\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}}(x),\mathbf{y})=\frac{(N^{k})^{\alpha n}}{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}\right|}\prod_{e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})}\mu_{\mathsf{e}}\big(x_{|e}-\mathbf{y}(e)\big).

Therefore, for any
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
we have

\displaystyle\quad\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}[\phi_{A}]\big(\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}}(x)\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in A}\left(\frac{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}\right|}{|A|}\cdot\frac{(N^{k})^{\alpha n}}{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}\right|}\prod_{e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})}\mu_{\mathsf{e}}\big(x_{|e}-\mathbf{y}(e)\big)\right)

\displaystyle=\underset{\mathbf{y}\in A}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})}\left(N^{k}\mu_{\mathsf{e}}\big(x_{|e}-\mathbf{z}(e)\big)\right)\cdot\prod_{e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})}\left(N^{k}\mu_{\mathsf{e}}\big(x_{|e}-\mathbf{y}(e)\big)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=N^{k(\alpha n-|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})|)}\cdot g_{\mathsf{e},\mathbf{z}}(x)\sum_{M}\underset{\mathbf{y}\in A}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})=M\right\}\cdot\mathbf{R}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n],\,M}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}\left(x,\mathfrak{i}_{M,\mathbf{z}}^{-1}(\mathbf{y})\right)\right],

(6.4)

where the sum on the last line is over all matchings
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n,\mathbf{z}}
.

On the other hand, for any
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
and any
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n,\mathbf{z}}
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n],\,M}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}\left[\phi_{\mathfrak{i}_{M,\mathbf{z}}^{-1}(A)}\right](x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathfrak{i}_{M,\mathbf{z}}^{-1}(A)}\left(\frac{(N^{k})^{|M|}}{\left|\mathfrak{i}_{M,\mathbf{z}}^{-1}(A)\right|}\mathbf{R}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n],\,M}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}(x,\boldsymbol{\xi})\right)

\displaystyle=N^{k(\alpha n-|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})|)}\cdot\underset{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathfrak{i}_{M,\mathbf{z}}^{-1}(A)}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathbf{R}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n],\,M}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}(x,\boldsymbol{\xi})\right].

(6.5)

Comparing the right-hand sides of (
6.4
) and (
6.5
) immediately yields the conclusion.
∎

We remark that on the right-hand side of (
6.3
), the first factor
g_{\mathsf{e},\mathbf{z}}(x)
is the structured part, and the sum over
M
is the pseudorandom part.

6.3
Interlude: Controlling Fourier Growth

As mentioned earlier, the key idea of
[
22
]
is to maintain suitable Fourier
\ell^{1}
-type control under taking products of functions. To this end, they introduce the notion of
(C,s^{*})
-boundedness
, which imposes a carefully calibrated upper bound on the Fourier
\ell^{1}
-norm of the degree-
d
component of a function, separately for each
d
.

For fixed parameters
C
and
s^{*}
, the bound at level
d
takes the following form:

Definition 6.12

(
[
22
, Definition 4.3]
)
.

Let
n,d
be integers such that
1\leqslant d\leqslant n
, and let
C,s^{*}
be positive real numbers. We define a value
F_{C}(n,d,s^{*})
by

F_{C}(n,d,s^{*})=\left(\frac{C\sqrt{n\cdot\max\{s^{*},d\}}}{d}\right)^{d/2}=\begin{cases}\left(C\sqrt{s^{*}n}/d\right)^{d/2},&\text{if }d\leqslant s^{*},\\
\left(C^{2}n/d\right)^{d/4},&\text{if }d>s^{*}.\end{cases}

We record the following two simple observations about the Fourier growth bound
F_{C}(n,\ell,s^{*})
.

Proposition 6.13
.

The function
F_{C}(n,d,s^{*})
is non-decreasing in the first and third variables.

Proposition 6.14
.

Let
n,d
be integers such that
1\leqslant d\leqslant n
, and let
C,s^{*}
be positive real numbers. We always have

F_{C}(n,d,s^{*})\leqslant 2^{s^{*}/4}\left(\frac{C^{2}n}{d}\right)^{d/4}.

Proof.

In the case
d>s^{*}
, this directly follows from Definition
6.12
. In the case
d\leqslant s^{*}
, the conclusion follows by simply using the inequality
s^{*}/d\leqslant 2^{s^{*}/d}
.
∎

We now introduced a slightly generalized version of the
(C,s^{*})
-boundedness notion in
[
22
, Definition 4.3]
. Recall from Section
2.1
that the notation
\|\cdot\|_{\mathsf{W}}
stands for the Fourier
\ell^{1}
-norm of a function.

Definition 6.15
.

Fix parameters
n,C,s^{*}\geqslant 1
and
\delta\geqslant 0
. Given a finite set
\Lambda
and a function
f:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
, we say that
f
is
(n,C,s^{*},\delta)
-bounded if it satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

The expected value of
f
lies in the range
[1-\delta,1+\delta]
, i.e.
1-\delta\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left[f\right]\leqslant 1+\delta
.

(2)

For any integer
d
such that
1\leqslant d\leqslant C^{-2}n
, we have
\left\|f^{=d}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}}\leqslant F_{C}(n,d,s^{*})
.

(3)

The maximum value of
f
is at most
2^{s^{*}}
, i.e.
\|f\|_{\infty}\leqslant 2^{s^{*}}
.

Although the second condition in Definition
6.15
imposes upper bounds only on the first
\lfloor C^{-2}n\rfloor+1
levels, the following proposition shows that some control over higher levels can be obtained via the third condition.

Proposition 6.16
.

Fix parameters
n,C,s^{*}\geqslant 1
and
\delta\geqslant 0
. For any
f:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
that is
(n,C,s^{*},\delta)
-bounded, we have

\left\|f^{=d}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}}\leqslant 2^{s^{*}/2}\left(\frac{3N|\Lambda|}{d}\right)^{d/2}

for any nonnegative integer
d
.

Proof.

We can simply apply Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to the Fourier 1-norm and calculate as follows:

\displaystyle\left\|f^{=d}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda},\;\|x\|_{\mathsf{H}}=d}\left|\widehat{f}(x)\right|\leqslant\sqrt{\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda},\;\|x\|_{\mathsf{H}}=d}1}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda},\;\|x\|_{\mathsf{H}}=d}\left|\widehat{f}(x)\right|^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{\binom{|\Lambda|}{d}N^{d}}\cdot\left\|f\right\|_{2}\leqslant\sqrt{\binom{|\Lambda|}{d}N^{d}}\cdot\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant 2^{s^{*}/2}\left(\frac{3N|\Lambda|}{d}\right)^{d/2}.\qed

The next proposition shows that in the notion of
(n,C,s^{*},\delta)
-boundedness, small modifications of the first parameter
n
can be offset by adjustments on the second parameter
C
.

Proposition 6.17
.

Fix parameters
n,C,s^{*}\geqslant 1
and
\delta\geqslant 0
. For any integer
n^{\prime}
such that
n/2\leqslant n^{\prime}\leqslant 2n
, any
(n,C,s^{*},\delta)
-bounded function is also
(n^{\prime},2C,s^{*},\delta)
-bounded.

Proof.

This is because
(2C)^{-2}n^{\prime}\leqslant C^{-2}n
and
F_{2C}(n^{\prime},d,s^{*})\geqslant F_{C}(n,d,s)
for any
d
.
∎

6.4
Analyzing the Structured Part

Recall that Lemma
6.5
expresses the ratio
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)/\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)
as the expectation of a product of
K|\mathcal{E}|
functions. By Lemma
6.11
, each of these functions admits a decomposition into a structured part and a pseudorandom part. Consequently, we are led to analyze a product of
2K|\mathcal{E}|
functions.

Our strategy is to first control the Fourier growth of the product of the
K|\mathcal{E}|
structure-part functions. We then show that this control does not deteriorate too rapidly as the
K|\mathcal{E}|
pseudorandom-part functions are incorporated into the product one at a time.

The product of the
K|\mathcal{E}|
structure-part functions, as highlighted in the following definition, is the object of study of this subsection.

Definition 6.18
.

Given a restriction sequence
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
, we define the product function
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\in L^{2}\big(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}\big)
by (recalling Definition
6.6
)

g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(x)=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}g_{\mathsf{e},\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}(x)=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\prod_{e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})}N^{k}\mu_{\mathsf{e}}\big(x_{|e}-\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}(e)\big).

(6.6)

The function
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
defined in Definition
6.18
satisfies the following nice properties.

Lemma 6.19
.

Fix a
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
communication game, where
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
. Let
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
be a restriction sequence that is not cyclic (as per Definition
4.11
), and let
V_{1},\dots,V_{t}\subseteq\mathcal{V}\times[n]
be the nontrivial connected components of the hypergraph
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
(as defined in Definition
4.10
). Then the following holds:

(1)

The function
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
is a probability density function, i.e.
\mathbb{E}\left[g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\right]=1
.

(2)

For any
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
, we have
\left\langle g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\chi_{b}\right\rangle\neq 0
only if
\mathrm{supp}(b)\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in[t]}V_{i}
and
|\mathrm{supp}(b)\cap V_{i}|\neq 1
for any
i\in[t]
.

Proof.

The first statement is identical to
[
14
, Lemma 7.10]
, so we omit its proof. We prove the second statement below.

Suppose
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
is a character index such that
\mathrm{supp}(b)\not\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in[t]}V_{i}
. Then it is easy to see that when
x
is a uniformly random vector in
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
, the restriction of
x
to
\bigcup_{i\in[t]}V_{i}
is independent with
\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}\times[n]}b_{v}x_{v}
(which is uniformly distributed in
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
). Since
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(x)
only depends on the restriction of
x
to
\bigcup_{i\in[t]}V_{i}
and
\chi_{b}(x)
only depends on
\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}\times[n]}b_{v}x_{v}
, it follows that
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(x)
and
\chi_{b}(x)
are independent, and hence we have

\left\langle g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\chi_{b}\right\rangle=\underset{x}{\mathbb{E}}\left[g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(x)\overline{\chi_{b}(x)}\right]=\underset{x}{\mathbb{E}}\left[g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(x)\right]\underset{x}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{\chi_{b}(x)}\right]=0.

In the rest of the proof, we assume that
\mathrm{supp}(b)\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in[t]}V_{i}
and (without loss of generality)
|\mathrm{supp}(b)\cap V_{1}|=1
.

We write
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\big(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\big)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
. Let
E
be the edge set of
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
and let
E_{1}
be the collection of edges in
E
that lie in
V_{1}
. For each edge
e\in E
, we let
\langle e\rangle
be the original hyperedge
\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}
such that
e\in\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}}
. Since the edge sets
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})
are pairwise disjoint as
(\mathsf{e},j)
ranges in
\mathcal{E}\times[K]
, we can define a map
\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}:E\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
by letting
\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}(e)=\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}(e)
for each
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
and each
e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})
. Now we can write

g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(x)=\prod_{e\in E}N^{k}\mu_{\langle e\rangle}\Big(x_{|e}-\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}(e)\Big).

(6.7)

Let
v^{*}
be the single element of
\mathrm{supp}(b)\cap V_{1}
. We run a breadth-first search (BFS) on the hypergraph
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
starting at the vertex
v^{*}
, and rank all edges in
E_{1}
by the time they are discovered in the BFS.
19
19

19

The BFS argument has been used in the proofs of Lemma
5.4
and
[
14
, Lemma 7.10]
.
This yields a total order
\prec
on
E_{1}
such that each hyperedge
e\in E_{1}
is incident to at most one vertex that is covered by some hyperedge preceding
e
in the order. Indeed, if some hyperedge
e
violates this condition, then by the time the BFS discovers
e
, it has also found a cycle of distinct hyperedges
e=e_{0},e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{\ell-1}\in E_{1}
and distinct vertices
v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{\ell-1}\in V_{1}
such that
e_{i}
is incident to both
v_{i}
and
v_{i+1\pmod{\ell}}
, for each
i\in\{0,1,\dots,\ell-1\}
. The
\ell
hyperedges
e_{0},\dots,e_{\ell-1}
thus together cover at most
\ell(k-1)
vertices, violating the assumption that
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
is cycle-free.

For notational convenience, we extend the total order
\prec
on
E_{1}
to
E
, by letting
e^{\prime}\prec e
for all edges
e^{\prime}\in E\setminus E_{1}
and
e\in E_{1}
. Using the total order on
E
, we now analyze the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(x)\overline{\chi_{b}(x)}\right]
. For any
e\in E_{1}
, we have:

\displaystyle\quad\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{\chi_{b}(x)}\prod_{e^{\prime}\preceq e}N^{k}\mu_{\langle e^{\prime}\rangle}\Big(x_{|e^{\prime}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}(e^{\prime})\Big)\right]

(6.8)

\displaystyle=\underset{x}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{\chi_{b}(x)}\prod_{e^{\prime}\prec e}N^{k}\mu_{\langle e^{\prime}\rangle}\Big(x_{|e^{\prime}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}(e^{\prime})\Big)\cdot\underset{\widetilde{x}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[N^{k}\mu_{\langle e\rangle}\Big(x_{|e}-\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}(e)\Big)\,\middle|\,x_{v^{*}},\left(x_{|e^{\prime}}\right)_{e^{\prime}\prec e}\right]\right].

Note that by our choice of the acyclic ordering
\prec
, we know that conditioning on
x_{v^{*}}
and
(\widetilde{x}_{\mid e^{\prime}})_{e^{\prime}\prec e}
only fixes at most one coordinate of the coordinates in
e
. If no coordinate is fixed, it is easy to see that the inner conditional expectation evaluates to
1
. Otherwise, suppose the
i
-th vertex
v
of
e
is fixed to
x_{v}=c\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}
by the conditioning. In this case, the inner conditional expectation equals

\displaystyle\underset{x}{\mathbb{E}}\left[N^{k}\mu_{\langle e\rangle}\Big(x_{|e}-\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}(e)\Big)\,\middle|\,x_{v^{*}},\left(x_{|e^{\prime}}\right)_{e^{\prime}\prec e}\right]=N\cdot\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k},\,z_{i}=c}\mu_{\langle e\rangle}\left(z-\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}(e)\right)=N\cdot\frac{1}{N}=1.

due to the one-wise independence of
\mu_{\langle e\rangle}
. Therefore, the expectation in (
6.8
) remains unchanged when the range of the product is changed from
e^{\prime}\preceq e
to
e^{\prime}\prec e
.

By applying this argument recursively — removing the maximal element under
\prec
at each step, we can derive that

\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{\chi_{b}(x)}\prod_{e\in E}N^{k}\mu_{\langle e\rangle}\Big(x_{|e}-\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}(e)\Big)\right]=\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{\chi_{b}(x)}\prod_{e\in E\setminus E_{1}}N^{k}\mu_{\langle e\rangle}\Big(x_{|e}-\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}(e)\Big)\right].

(6.9)

Since
v^{*}\in\mathrm{supp}(b)
, when
x
is a uniformly random vector in
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
, the restriction of
x
to
\bigcup_{i=2}^{t}V_{i}
is independent with
\chi_{b}(x)
. Similarly to the argument in the second paragraph of this proof, we conclude that the right-hand side of (
6.9
) equals 0. Since the left-hand side of (
6.9
) equals
\langle g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\chi_{b}\rangle
, it follows that
\langle g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\chi_{b}\rangle=0
.
∎

To analyze the Fourier growth of
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
, we are interested in knowing how many level-
\ell
Fourier coefficients of
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
can be nonzero, for a given integer
\ell
. This task is reduced by the second statement of Lemma
6.19
to the following purely set-theoretic question: how many
\ell
-element subsets
S\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in[t]}V_{i}
satisfy
|S\cap V_{i}|\neq 1
for all
i
, where
V_{1},\dots,V_{t}
are pairwise disjoint finite sets? The next lemma provides an answer to this question.

Lemma 6.20
.

Suppose
V_{1},\dots,V_{t}
are pairwise disjoint finite sets such that
|V_{i}|\geqslant 2
for any
i\in[t]
. Then for any integer
\ell\geqslant 2
, the number of subsets
S\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in[t]}V_{i}
such that
|S|=\ell
and
|S\cap V_{i}|\neq 1
for any
i
is at most

\left(\frac{20}{\ell}\big(|V_{1}|^{2}+\dots+|V_{t}|^{2}\big)\right)^{\ell/2}.

Proof.

In the polynomial ring
\mathbb{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{t}]
, a monomial is said to be singleton-free if the degree of any variable is not 1. For a polynomial
f\in\mathbb{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{t}]
, let
\mathsf{SF}(f)
denote the sum of the coefficients of all singleton-free monomials in the expansion of
f
. We will show that

\mathsf{SF}\big((a_{1}x_{1}+\dots+a_{t}x_{t})^{\ell}\big)\leqslant\big(2\ell\cdot(a_{1}^{2}+\dots+a_{t}^{2})\big)^{\ell/2}\qquad\text{for any }a_{1},\dots,a_{t}\in\mathbb{R}.

(6.10)

Once we have (
6.10
), it is easy to see that the number of subsets
S\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in[t]}V_{i}
such that
|S|=\ell
and
|S\cap V_{i}|\neq 1
for any
i
is at most

\frac{1}{\ell!}\cdot\mathsf{SF}\Big(\big(|V_{1}|x_{1}+\dots+|V_{t}|x_{t}\big)^{\ell}\Big)\leqslant\frac{\left(2\ell\cdot\big(|V_{1}|^{2}+\dots+|V_{t}|^{2}\big)\right)^{\ell/2}}{(\ell/3)^{\ell}}\leqslant\left(\frac{20}{\ell}\big(|V_{1}|^{2}+\dots+|V_{t}|^{2}\big)\right)^{\ell/2}.

We prove (
6.10
) by induction on
t
. For
t=1
the inequality clearly holds, and we next assume
t\geqslant 2
and that (
6.10
) holds for smaller values of
t
. Without loss of generality assume
a_{1},\dots,a_{t}\geqslant 0
. If
a_{i}=0
for all
i\geqslant 2
then the desired inequality trivially holds, so we can assume the normalization
a_{2}^{2}+\dots+a_{t}^{2}=1
(noting that (
6.10
) is homogeneous in the
a_{i}
’s). We have

\displaystyle\mathsf{SF}\big((a_{1}x_{1}+\dots+a_{t}x_{t})^{\ell}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{SF}\big((a_{2}x_{2}+\dots+a_{t}x_{t})^{\ell}\big)+\sum_{r=2}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{r}{}a_{1}^{r}\cdot\mathsf{SF}\big((a_{2}x_{2}+\dots+a_{t}x_{t})^{\ell-r}\big)

\displaystyle\leqslant(2\ell)^{\ell/2}+\sum_{r=2}^{\ell}\binom{\ell}{r}{}a_{1}^{r}\cdot(2\ell)^{(\ell-r)/2}

(by the induction hypothesis)

\displaystyle=(2\ell)^{\ell/2}\left(\left(1+\frac{a_{1}}{\sqrt{2\ell}}\right)^{\ell}-\ell\cdot\frac{a_{1}}{\sqrt{2\ell}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\big(2\ell\cdot(1+a_{1}^{2})\big)^{\ell/2},

(using Proposition
A.1
)

as desired.
∎

We are now ready to complete our analysis of the function
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
using the framework of Section
6.3
.

Lemma 6.21
.

Fix a
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
communication game, where
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
. Let
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
be a restriction sequence that is not cyclic. If
\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|\leqslant\gamma n
for some constant
\gamma\in(0,1)
, then the function
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
(defined in Definition
6.18
) is
\big(n,20N^{2},\gamma n\log_{2}N,0\big)
-bounded.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
satisfies the three conditions of
\big(n,20N^{2},\gamma n\log_{2}N,0\big)
-boundedness required by Definition
6.15
.

Expectation equals
1
.

It follows directly from Lemma
6.19
(1) that
\mathbb{E}\left[g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\right]=1
.

Infinity-norm bound.

Let
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
, and let
V_{1},\dots,V_{t}\subseteq\mathcal{V}\times[n]
be the list of nontrivial connected components of the hypergraph
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
. For each
i\in[t]
, let
E_{i}
be the set of edges of
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
within the component
V_{i}
. Since
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
does not contain cycles, for each
i\in[t]
we have
(k-1)|E_{i}|<|V_{i}|
and consequently
k|E_{i}|\leqslant|V_{i}|^{2}
. Therefore, we have

\sum_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}k\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\right|=\sum_{i=1}^{t}k|E_{i}|\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{t}|V_{i}|^{2}=\lVert\boldsymbol{\zeta}\rVert,

(6.11)

where the first equality is due to the condition that the edge sets
\left\{\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\right\}_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
are pairwise disjoint. Combining (
6.6
), (
6.11
) and the fact that
\lVert\mu_{\mathsf{e}}\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant 1
for each
\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}
, we conclude that
\|g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant N^{\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|}\leqslant N^{\gamma n}
.

Fourier growth bound.

For any
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
, there are two possibilities:

(1)

If
\mathrm{supp}(b)\not\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in[t]}V_{i}
or
|\mathrm{supp}(b)\cap V_{i}|=1
for some
i\in[t]
, then by Lemma
6.19
(2) we have
\langle g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\chi_{b}\rangle=0
,

(2)

If
\mathrm{supp}(b)\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in[t]}V_{i}
and
|\mathrm{supp}(b)\cap V_{i}|\neq 1
for all
i\in[t]
, then we have
\left|\langle g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\chi_{b}\rangle\right|\leqslant\|g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\|_{1}\cdot\|\chi_{b}\|_{\infty}\leqslant 1
, using Lemma
6.19
(1).

Therefore, for any integer
\ell\geqslant 1
, the Wiener norm
\left\|g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{=\ell}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}}
is upper bounded by the number of
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
such that
\mathrm{supp}(b)
is an
\ell
-element subset of
\bigcup_{i\in[t]}V_{i}
whose intersection with each
V_{i}
has size not equal to 1. It then follows from Lemma
6.20
that

\left\|g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{=\ell}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}}\leqslant N^{\ell}\left(\frac{20}{\ell}\big(|V_{1}|^{2}+\dots+|V_{t}|^{2}\big)\right)^{\ell/2}\leqslant\left(\frac{20N^{2}\gamma n}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\leqslant F_{20N^{2}}(n,\ell,\gamma^{2}n).

Using Proposition
6.13
, we conclude that
\left\|g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{=\ell}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}}\leqslant F_{20N^{2}}\big(n,\ell,\gamma n\log_{2}N\big)
, as desired.
∎

6.5
Analyzing the Pseudorandom Part by Induction

As mentioned earlier, Lemmas
6.5
and
6.11
together express the ratio
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)/\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)
as the expectation of a product of
2K|\mathcal{E}|
functions. The product of the
K|\mathcal{E}|
structured-part functions has already been analyzed in Section
6.4
, and in this subsection we will incorporate the remaining
K|\mathcal{E}|
pseudorandom-part functions into the product.

We will incorporate the
K|\mathcal{E}|
pseudorandom-part functions into the product
one at a time
, using the following “induction lemma” (inspired by
[
22
, Lemma 6.1]
).

Lemma 6.22
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
k
-universe embedded in a finite set
\Lambda
. Fix constants
C\in[1,+\infty)
and
\delta_{0},\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
. Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
be parameters satisfying

0<m\leqslant\frac{|\mathcal{U}|^{3}}{2^{18}N^{10k}|\Lambda|^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\log_{2}(4/\delta)\leqslant s^{*}\leqslant(10C)^{-2}\delta^{4}m.

Suppose
f:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
is a
(|\mathcal{U}|,C,s^{*},\delta_{0})
-bounded function and
\mathcal{D}
is a pseudo-uniform distribution over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
. For any parameter
\eta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, a random sample
M\sim\mathcal{D}
satisfies the following with probability at least
1-3\eta
: for any
A\subseteq\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
of size
|A|\geqslant 2^{-s^{*}}N^{km}
and any one-wise independent distribution
\mu
over
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
,

\text{the function }f\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]\text{ is }\Big(|\mathcal{U}|,\,2^{22}\eta^{-2}N^{8k}C,\,2s^{*},\,\delta_{0}+\eta^{-1}\delta\Big)\text{-bounded.}

Lemma
6.22
is proved in Section
7
. In the rest of this section, we use the induction lemma to complete the proof of Lemma
4.15
(restated below).

See
4.15

Proof of Lemma
4.15
assuming Lemma
6.22
.

We begin by setting some notation for the proof. We write
R=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
. We fix an arbitrary total order on
\mathcal{E}\times[K]
, and for each index
i\in\{1,2,\dots,K|\mathcal{E}|\}
, if
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
is the
i
-th-ranked player, we use the following notations:

(1)

Denote
n_{i}=n-|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})|
and
m_{i}=\alpha n-|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})|
. Note that
m_{i}\leqslant\alpha n_{i}
. As long as
\gamma\leqslant\alpha/2
, we have
n_{i}\geqslant n-\|\boldsymbol{\zeta}\|\geqslant n-\gamma n\geqslant n/2
and
m_{i}\geqslant\alpha n-\gamma n\geqslant\alpha n/2
.

(2)

We use
\mathcal{M}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
as a shorthand for the matching space
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\,\alpha n,\,\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}
.

(3)

Let
\mathcal{D}_{i}
be the distribution of
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})
where
\mathbf{y}
is a uniformly random labeled matching in
A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
. Note that
\mathcal{D}_{i}
is a probability distribution over
\mathcal{M}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
, and it is pseudo-uniform due to Proposition
4.6
.

(4)

For any matching
M\in\mathcal{M}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
, let (see Definition
6.10
)

A_{i,M}=A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}_{M}:=\mathfrak{i}^{-1}_{M,\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\big(A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\big)\subseteq\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right).

Note that the ratio
\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}_{M}\right|/\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right|
is the probability of the matching
M
under the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{i}
. We also record the fact that

\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\frac{1}{|A_{i,M}|}\right]=\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\frac{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}_{M}\right|}{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right|}\cdot\frac{1}{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}_{M}\right|}=\frac{N^{-km_{i}}\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\right|}{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right|}\leqslant 2^{\gamma n}N^{-km_{i}},

(6.12)

where we used the definition of
(\gamma n,\gamma n)
-goodness in the last transition.

(5)

For any matching
M\in\mathcal{M}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
, let

h_{i,M}=h^{(\mathsf{e},j)}_{M}:=\boldsymbol{R}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n],\,M}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}\left[\phi_{A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}_{M}}\right]\in L^{2}\big(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}\big).

By Lemmas
6.5
and
6.11
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)}{\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)}

\displaystyle=\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(x)\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\left(\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\frac{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}_{M}\right|}{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right|}h^{(\mathsf{e},j)}_{M}(x)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(x)\prod_{i=1}^{K|\mathcal{E}|}\left(\underset{{M\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{i,M}(x)\right]\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{{(M_{i})_{i=1}^{K|\mathcal{E}|}\sim\prod_{i=1}^{K|\mathcal{E}|}\mathcal{D}_{i}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(x)\prod_{i=1}^{K|\mathcal{E}|}{h_{i,M_{i}}(x)}\right]\right].

(6.13)

Induction setup.

In the rest of the proof, we fix the following constants:

B=10^{16}N^{8k}K^{2}|\mathcal{E}|^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\eta=10^{-4}K^{-1}|\mathcal{E}|^{-1}.

(6.14)

We will prove that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)/\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\geqslant 1-10^{-3}
if
\gamma
is sufficiently small and
n
is sufficiently large (after
\gamma
is fixed). In other words, we taken
n\gg\gamma^{-1}\gg 1
.

For any tuple of sampled matchings
(M_{i})_{i=1}^{K|\mathcal{E}|}
, where each
M_{i}
is a possible sample from
\mathcal{D}_{i}
, we say
(M_{i})_{i=1}^{K|\mathcal{E}|}

passes the
r
-th test
if

\text{the function}\qquad g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}h_{i,M_{i}}\qquad\text{is}\qquad\Big(n,\,20N^{2}B^{r},\,2^{r+1}\gamma n\log_{2}N,\,r\eta\Big)\text{-bounded,}

(6.15)

for any given index
r\in\{0,1,2,\dots,K|\mathcal{E}|\}
. By Lemma
6.21
we know that any tuple
(M_{i})_{i=1}^{K|\mathcal{E}|}
passes the 0-th test. If
(M_{i})_{i=1}^{K|\mathcal{E}|}
passes the
K|\mathcal{E}|
-th test, then by definition we have

\mathbb{E}\left[g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\prod_{i=1}^{K|\mathcal{E}|}h_{i,M_{i}}\right]\geqslant 1-K|\mathcal{E}|\eta.

We claim that for each
r\in\{1,2,\dots,K|\mathcal{E}|\}
, when each
M_{i}
is sampled independently from
\mathcal{D}_{i}
, the probability that
(M_{i})_{i=1}^{K|\mathcal{E}|}
passes the
r
-th test conditioned on it passes the
(r-1)
-th test is at least
1-4\eta
. Once we have that, it follows from union bound that
(M_{i})_{i=1}^{K|\mathcal{E}|}
passes the
K|\mathcal{E}|
-th test with probability
1-4K|\mathcal{E}|\eta
, and thus the expectation on the right-hand side of (
6.13
) is least
(1-4K|\mathcal{E}|\eta)(1-K|\mathcal{E}|\eta)\geqslant 1-5K|\mathcal{E}|\eta\geqslant 1-10^{-3}
, as desired.

Induction step.

We next prove the induction-step claim using Lemma
6.22
. We fix an index
r\in\{1,2,\dots,K|\mathcal{E}|\}
and assume that
(M_{i})_{i=1}^{K|\mathcal{E}|}
passes the
(r-1)
-th test. By the definition (
6.15
) and Proposition
6.17
, we have

\text{the function }g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}h_{i,M_{i}}\text{ is }\Big(n_{r},\,40N^{2}B^{r-1},\,2^{r}\gamma n\log_{2}N,\,(r-1)\eta\Big)\text{-bounded.}

(6.16)

We apply Lemma
6.22
to the function
f=g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}h_{i,M_{i}}
with the parameters

C=40N^{2}B^{r-1},\qquad\delta_{0}=(r-1)\eta,\qquad\delta=\eta^{2},\qquad s^{*}=2^{r}\gamma n\log_{2}N,

(6.17)

and

m=m_{r}\leqslant\alpha n_{r}\leqslant\frac{n_{r}}{2^{20}N^{10k}|\mathcal{V}|^{2}}\leqslant\frac{n_{r}^{3}}{2^{18}N^{10k}|\mathcal{V}|^{2}n^{2}}.

The condition
\log_{2}(4/\delta)\leqslant s^{*}\leqslant(10C)^{-2}\delta^{4}m
stated in Lemma
6.22
is satisfied as long as we take
n\gg\gamma^{-1}\gg 1
.

Recalling that the probability of any matching
M
under
\mathcal{D}_{r}
is proportional to
|A_{r,M}|
, we have

\displaystyle\underset{M_{r}\sim\mathcal{D}_{r}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\left|A_{r,M_{r}}\right|\leqslant 2^{-s^{*}}N^{km_{r}}\right]\leqslant\frac{\mathbb{E}_{M_{r}\sim\mathcal{D}_{r}}\left[|A_{r,M_{r}}|^{-1}\right]}{2^{s^{*}}N^{-km_{r}}}\leqslant 2^{-s^{*}+\gamma n}\leqslant 2^{-\gamma n}\leqslant\eta,

where we used (
6.12
) in the second transition and
n\gg\gamma^{-1}
in the last transition. Therefore, when
M_{r}
is sampled from
\mathcal{D}_{r}
, with probability at least
1-4\eta
both of the following statements hold:

(1)

|A_{r,M_{r}}|\geqslant 2^{-s^{*}}N^{km_{r}}
.

(2)

The conclusion of Lemma
6.22
holds for
M_{r}
, i.e. for any
A\subseteq\mathrm{Map}\left(M_{r},\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
of size
|A|\geqslant 2^{-s^{*}}N^{km_{r}}
and any one-wise independent distribution
\mu
, the function
f\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n],\,M_{r}}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]
is

\Big(n_{r},\,(2^{22}\eta^{-2}N^{8k}C)\cdot 40N^{2}B^{r-1},\,2s^{*},(r-1)\eta+\eta^{-1}\delta\Big)\text{-bounded.}

By (
6.14
), (
6.17
) and Proposition
6.17
, it is also

\Big(n,\,20N^{2}B^{r},\,2^{r+1}\gamma n\log_{2}N,\,r\eta\Big)\text{-bounded}.

Combining the two statements above, we conclude that with probability at least
1-4\eta
over the sampled matching
M_{r}\sim\mathcal{D}_{r}
, we have

f\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n],\,M_{r}}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]=g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}h_{i,M_{i}}\qquad\text{is}\qquad\Big(n,\,20N^{2}B^{r},\,2^{r+1}\gamma n\log_{2}N,\,r\eta\Big)\text{-bounded}.

In other words,
(M_{i})_{i=1}^{K|\mathcal{E}|}
passes the
r
-th test with conditional probability at least
1-4\eta
.
∎

7
Proof of the Induction Lemma

The goal of this section is to prove Lemma
6.22
. The lemma features several combinatorial/analytic objects of study, each requiring separate treatment. We begin in Section
7.1
by studying the Fourier-analytic properties of the operator
\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}
. In Section
7.2
, we examine the pseudo-uniform distribution of matchings and establish its relevant combinatorial properties. We then address the technical core of the lemma in Sections
7.3
and
7.4
, where we analyze the Fourier growth of the product
f\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]
via the convolution theorem.

7.1
Singular Value Decomposition

A key property of the operator is that it admits a clean singular value decomposition: it maps Fourier characters on the space
\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
to scalar multiples of character functions on
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
. We first define the natural character functions on
\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
.

Definition 7.1
.

For a finite set
M
and a map
\mathbf{a}:M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
, we define a character function
\psi_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
by

\psi_{\mathbf{a}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}):=\prod_{e\in M}\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(e)).

For any
f\in L^{2}\big(\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)\big)
, let
\widehat{f}(\mathbf{a}):=\langle f,\psi_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle
.

Due to the one-wise independence of the distribution
\mu
, some characters on the space
\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
are “killed” by the operator
\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}
(and we must use that to our advantage in order to prove Lemma
6.22
). The following definition collects the characters that are
not
killed.

Definition 7.2
.

For a finite set
M
, we let
\mathcal{X}(M)
be the collection of character indices
\mathbf{a}:M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
such that
\|\mathbf{a}(e)\|_{\mathsf{H}}\neq 1
for any
e\in M
.

Remark 7.3
.

Note that the Hamming weight of
\mathbf{a}(e)
, denoted by
\|\mathbf{a}(e)\|_{\mathsf{H}}
, is the number of
i\in[k]
such that
\mathbf{a}(e)_{i}\neq 0
. This is to be distinguished from the Hamming weight of
\mathbf{a}
itself, denoted by
\|\mathbf{a}\|_{\mathsf{H}}
, which stands for the number of
e\in M
such that
\mathbf{a}(e)\neq 0
. See Section
2.1
for the general notational convention of Hamming weights.

The following notations will be used many times throughout Section
7
.

Notation 7.4
.

Suppose
S
and
T
are disjoint finite sets. For two maps
\mathbf{a}_{1}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
and
\mathbf{a}_{2}:T\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
, we define their concatenation
\mathbf{a}_{1}\uplus\mathbf{a}_{2}:S\sqcup T\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
by setting
(\mathbf{a}_{1}\uplus\mathbf{a}_{2})(e):=\mathbf{a}_{1}(e)
for
e\in S
and
(\mathbf{a}_{1}\uplus\mathbf{a}_{2})(e):=\mathbf{a}_{2}(e)
for
e\in T
.

Notation 7.5
.

Suppose
S
and
M
are finite sets such that
S\subseteq M
. For a map
\mathbf{a}:M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
, we define
\mathbf{a}_{|S}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
to be the restriction of
\mathbf{a}
to
S
, and define
\mathbf{a}_{\setminus S}:M\setminus S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
to be the restriction of
\mathbf{a}
to
M\setminus S
.

Definition 7.6
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
k
-universe embedded in a finite set
\Lambda
, and let
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
be a matching of size
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
. For any character index
\mathbf{a}:M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
, we use
[\mathbf{a}]
to denote the corresponding character index in
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
defined by

1.

[\mathbf{a}]_{v_{i}}=\mathbf{a}(e)_{i}
for any edge
e=(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})\in M
,

2.

[\mathbf{a}]_{v}=0
for any vertex
v\in\Lambda
that is not contained in any edge of
M
.

We are now ready to present the singular value decomposition lemma.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
k
-universe d in a finite set
\Lambda
, and let
\mu
be a one-wise independent distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
. Fix a matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
of size
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
. For any
f\in L^{2}\big(\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)\big)
and any
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
, we have

\begin{cases}\left|\left\langle\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[f],\chi_{b}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda})}\right|\leqslant\left|\widehat{f}(\mathbf{a})\right|,&\text{if }b=[\mathbf{a}]\text{ for some }\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}(M),\\
\left\langle\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[f],\chi_{b}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda})}=0,&\text{if }b\neq[\mathbf{a}]\text{ for any }\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}(M).\end{cases}

Proof.

Recall from Section
2.1
that
\mu(\cdot)
denotes the probability mass function of
\mu
. For each character index
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
, define

r(t):=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}\mu(z)\,\overline{\chi_{t}(z)}=N^{k}\cdot\widehat{\mu}(t).

Since
\mu
is assumed to be one-wise independent, we know that
\widehat{\mu}(t)=0
for any
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
with exactly one nonzero coordinate. Thus,
r(t)=0
for such
t
. Additionally, we have
r(0)=1
and
|r(t)|\leqslant 1
for all
t
since
|r(t)|\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mu}\,\left|\overline{\chi_{t}(z)}\right|=1
.

By Definition
6.7
we have

\left\langle\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[f],\chi_{b}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda})}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\xi}:M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}f(\boldsymbol{\xi})\left\langle\mathbf{R}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\xi}),\chi_{b}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda})}.

(7.1)

If
b\neq[\mathbf{a}]
for any
\mathbf{a}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
, from Definition
6.7
it is easy to see that

\left\langle\mathbf{R}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\xi}),\chi_{b}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda})}=0\text{ for any }\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right),

in which case (
7.1
) evaluates to 0, as desired. We next assume
b=[\mathbf{a}]
for some
\mathbf{a}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
. We can then calculate from (
7.1
) and Definition
6.7
as follows:

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[f],\chi_{[\mathbf{a}]}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\xi}:M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}f(\boldsymbol{\xi})\cdot\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\overline{\chi_{[\mathbf{a}]}(x)}\cdot\prod_{e\in M}\mu\big(x_{|e}-\boldsymbol{\xi}(e)\big)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\xi}:M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}\left(f(\boldsymbol{\xi})\prod_{e\in M}\underset{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mu\big(z-\boldsymbol{\xi}(e)\big)\,\overline{\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(z)}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{\xi}:M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}\left(f(\boldsymbol{\xi})\prod_{e\in M}\left(\widehat{\mu}(\mathbf{a}(e))\,\overline{\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(e))}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=N^{-k|M|}\prod_{e\in M}r(\mathbf{a}(e))\cdot\sum_{\boldsymbol{\xi}:M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}\left(f(\boldsymbol{\xi})\prod_{e\in M}\overline{\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(e))}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{e\in M}r(\mathbf{a}(e))\cdot\langle f,\psi_{\mathbf{a}}\rangle=\widehat{f}(\mathbf{a})\prod_{e\in M}r(\mathbf{a}(e)).

Recall that for a fixed
e\in M
, the complex number
r(\mathbf{a}(e))
always has absolute value at most 1, and equals 0 if
\mathbf{a}(e)
has exactly one nonzero coordinate. Therefore,
\prod_{e\in M}r(\mathbf{a}(e))
has absolute value at most 1, and vanishes if
\mathbf{a}\not\in\mathcal{X}(M)
. The desired conclusion thus follows from the calculation above.
∎

7.2
Random Matchings

Throughout this subsection, we fix a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
embedded in a finite set
\Lambda
, and a nonnegative integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
. Our goal is to study the behavior of a pseudo-uniformly random matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
with respect to a fixed vector
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
, in order to prepare for the convolution analysis in Section
7.3
.

As will become clear in Section
7.3
, given an edge
e\in M
that intersects with the vertex set
\mathrm{supp}(z)
, it is important to distinguish between the case where
e
has exactly one common vertex with
\mathrm{supp}(z)
and the case where they intersect in more than one vertex. We will classify all vertices in
\mathrm{supp}(z)
that are touched by the matching
M
into the following two types.

Definition 7.8
.

Let
e=(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})\in\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U}
be an edge, and let

z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
be a vector. For any
i\in[k]
with
z_{v_{i}}\neq 0
, the vertex
v_{i}
is called a
boundary vertex of
z
to
e
if
z_{v_{j}}=0
for all
j\in[k]\setminus\{i\}
; it is called
an internal vertex of
z
to
e
if
z_{v_{j}}\neq 0
for some
j\in[k]\setminus\{i\}
.

Definition 7.9
.
