For any matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
and any vector
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
, we define two vertex sets as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{bd}_{M,z}

\displaystyle:=\{v\in\mathrm{supp}(z)\mid v\text{ is boundary to some }e\in M\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{in}_{M,z}

\displaystyle:=\{v\in\mathrm{supp}(z)\mid v\text{ is internal to some }e\in M\}.

For fixed
M,z
, the two sets
\mathsf{bd}_{M,z}
and
\mathsf{in}_{M,z}
are always disjoint since
M
is a matching.
Intuitively, when
m
is a small fraction of
|\mathcal{U}|
and
M
is a pseudo-uniformly random matching in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
, the subsets
\mathsf{bd}_{M,z}
and
\mathsf{in}_{M,z}
are typically not too large relative to
\mathrm{supp}(z)
. To formalize this idea, we make the following definition.

Definition 7.10
.

For any nonnegative integers
t,i,b
such that
i+b\leqslant t
, we define a quantity
q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(t,i,b)\in[0,1]
by

q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(t,i,b):=\max_{z,\mathcal{D}}\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|=i\text{ and }|\mathsf{bd}_{M,z}|=b\big.\right],

where the maximum is taken over all vectors
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
of Hamming weight
\|z\|_{\mathsf{H}}=t
and all pseudo-uniform distributions
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
.

We then prove an upper bound on the quantity
q_{\Lambda}^{\mathcal{U},m}(t,i,b)
.

Lemma 7.11
.

For any nonnegative integers
t,i,b
such that
i+b\leqslant t
, we have

q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(t,i,b)\leqslant(24k^{2})^{t}\left(\frac{im}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{i/2}\left(\frac{m}{|\mathcal{U}|}\right)^{b}.

Proof.

The definition of
q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(t,i,b)
features a pseudo-uniform distribution
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
. In the first part of the proof, we reduce the problem to the case where
\mathcal{D}
is the uniform distribution over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
; the uniform case will then be handled in the second part of the proof.

Reducing to the uniform case.

For a fixed vector
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
such that
\|z\|_{\mathsf{H}}=t
, we let
\mathcal{S}_{z,i,b}
be the collection of partial matchings
S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant t}
such that
|\mathsf{in}_{S,z}|=i
,
|\mathsf{bd}_{S,z}|=b
and every edge of
S
contains at least one vertex in
\mathrm{supp}(z)
. We clearly have

\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|=i\text{ and }|\mathsf{bd}_{M,z}|=b\big.\right]\leqslant\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{z,i,b}}\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[S\subseteq M\right].

(7.2)

For each
S\in\mathcal{S}_{z,i,b}
, by Definition
4.5
we have

\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[S\subseteq M\right]\leqslant 2^{|S|}\underset{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[S\subseteq M\right]\leqslant 2^{t}\underset{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[S\subseteq M\right].

(7.3)

For any
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
, let
\mathsf{cnt}(M,z,i,b)
be the number of partial matchings
S\in\mathcal{S}_{z,i,b}
that are contained in
M
. Combining (
7.2
) and (
7.3
), we have

\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|=i\text{ and }|\mathsf{bd}_{M,z}|=b\big.\right]\leqslant 2^{t}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{z,i,b}}\underset{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[S\subseteq M\right]=2^{t}\underset{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathsf{cnt}(M,z,i,b)\right].

(7.4)

It then suffices to give an upper bound for the expectation of
\mathsf{cnt}(M,z,i,b)
over a uniformly random
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
.

Analysis in the uniform case.

We fix a vector
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
with
|\mathrm{supp}(z)\cap\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U}|=r
, and proceed to estimate the expected number of partial matchings in
\mathcal{S}_{z,i,b}
that are contained in a uniformly random matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
. Note that this number
\mathsf{cnt}(M,z,i,b)
only depends on
\mathrm{supp}(z)\cap\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U}
, rather than on the full vector
z
.

Suppose
|\mathrm{supp}(z)\cap U_{j}|=r_{j}
, for
j\in[k]
. We may then equivalently think of
M
as a fixed matching in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
, and
R_{z}:=\mathrm{supp}(z)\cap\bigcup\mathcal{U}
as a random subset of
\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U}
conditioned on
|R_{z}\cap U_{j}|=r_{j}
for all
j\in[k]
. The number of such subsets
R_{z}
is

\prod_{j=1}^{k}\binom{|\mathcal{U}|}{r_{j}}\geqslant\prod_{j=1}^{k}\left(\frac{|\mathcal{U}|}{r_{j}}\right)^{r_{j}}\geqslant\left(\frac{|\mathcal{U}|}{r}\right)^{r}.

A valid choice of a pair
(R_{z},S)
such that
S\subseteq M
and
S\in\mathcal{S}_{z,i,b}
must comply with the following three steps:

(1)

First choose a set
\mathsf{bd}_{S,z}
with size
b
. The number of such choices is
\binom{m}{b}k^{b}
.

(2)

Next choose a set
\mathsf{in}_{S,z}
with size
i
. Note that since every edge of
M
that intersects with
\mathsf{in}_{S,z}
must contain at least 2 vertices from
\mathsf{in}_{S,z}
, the number of such edges is at most
i/2
. Therefore, (after the first step) the number of choices for
\mathsf{in}_{S,z}
is at most
\binom{m-b}{d}\binom{kd}{i}
, where
d:=\min\{\lfloor i/2\rfloor,m-b\}
.

(3)

The first two steps completely determine the partial matching
S
. The third step is to choose the set
R_{z}\setminus(\mathsf{bd}_{S,z}\cup\mathsf{in}_{S,z})
. The number of such choices is at most
\binom{k|\mathcal{U}|}{r-i-b}
.

We can thus conclude that for a fixed
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
and a random
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
with
|\mathrm{supp}(z)\cap U_{j}|=r_{j}
for each
j\in[k]
, the expected number of
S\in\mathcal{S}_{z,i,b}
such that
S\subseteq M
is at most

\binom{m}{b}k^{b}\binom{m-b}{d}\binom{kd}{i}\binom{k|\mathcal{U}|}{r-i-b}\cdot\left(\frac{|\mathcal{U}|}{r}\right)^{-r},

(7.5)

where
r:=\sum_{j=1}^{k}r_{j}\geqslant i+b
(the probability is 0 if
r<i+b
).

To simplify this bound, we first note that if
i/2\leqslant m-b
, then
d=\lfloor i/2\rfloor
and

\binom{m-b}{d}\binom{kd}{i}\leqslant\left(\frac{3(m-b)}{i/2}\right)^{i/2}\left(\frac{3ki/2}{i}\right)^{i}\leqslant\left(\frac{15k^{2}(m-b)}{i}\right)^{i/2}.

If
i/2>m-b
, then
d=m-b
and

\binom{m-b}{d}\binom{kd}{i}\leqslant\left(\frac{3k(m-b)}{i}\right)^{i}\leqslant\left(\frac{3k}{2}\right)^{i}.

Since it is always true that
i\leqslant k(m-b)
,
20
20

20

Otherwise
q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(t,i,b)=0
.
in both cases we have

\binom{m-b}{d}\binom{kd}{i}\leqslant\left(\frac{15k^{3}(m-b)}{i}\right)^{i/2}.

Plugging this into (
7.5
) and simplifying the other terms in (
7.5
), we get

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}\\
\|z\|_{\mathsf{H}}=t\end{subarray}}\underset{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathsf{cnt}(M,z,i,b)\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\max_{i+b\leqslant r\leqslant t}\left(\frac{3m}{b}\right)^{b}k^{b}\left(\frac{15k^{3}(m-b)}{i}\right)^{i/2}\left(\frac{3k|\mathcal{U}|}{r-i-b}\right)^{r-i-b}\left(\frac{r}{|\mathcal{U}|}\right)^{r}

\displaystyle\leqslant\max_{i+b\leqslant r\leqslant t}(12k^{2})^{r}\left(\frac{im}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{i/2}\left(\frac{m}{|\mathcal{U}|}\right)^{b}=(12k^{2})^{t}\left(\frac{im}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{i/2}\left(\frac{m}{|\mathcal{U}|}\right)^{b},

where in the second transition we used the convexity inequality
b^{b}i^{i}(r-i-b)^{r-i-b}\geqslant(r/3)^{r}
.

Combining the above with (
7.4
), for any pseudo-uniform distribution
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
we have

\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda},\;\|z\|_{\mathsf{H}}=t}\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|=i\text{ and }|\mathsf{bd}_{M,z}|=b\big.\right]\leqslant(24k^{2})^{t}\left(\frac{im}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{i/2}\left(\frac{m}{|\mathcal{U}|}\right)^{b},

as desired.
∎

7.3
Transfer of Fourier Mass

Throughout this subsection, we fix a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
embedded in a finite set
\Lambda
, and a nonnegative integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
. The goal of this subsection is to analyze the following quantity.

Definition 7.12
.

Suppose
s^{*}
is a real number with
0\leqslant s^{*}\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
. For any nonnegative integers
t
and
\ell
, we define a quantity
Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,\ell)
by

Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,\ell):=\max_{z,\mathcal{D}}\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\max_{A}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}(M),\;\|z+[\mathbf{a}]\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell}\left|\widehat{\phi_{A}}(\mathbf{a})\right|\right],

(7.6)

where the first maximum is taken over all vectors
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
of Hamming weight
\|z\|_{\mathsf{H}}=t
and all pseudo-uniform distributions
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
, while the second maximum taken is over all sets
A\subseteq\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
of size
|A|\geqslant 2^{-s^{*}}N^{km}
.

Intuitively, the quantity
Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,\ell)
measures the “rate of transfer” of Fourier mass from level
t
to level
\ell
when a function is multiplied by
\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]
. In Sections
7.3.1
,
7.3.3
, and
7.3.4
, we derive upper bounds on
Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,\ell)
for different ranges of
\ell
.

The reason for treating these ranges separately is that, in the “low-level” regime where
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant s^{*}
, we can invoke the level-
d
inequality (Proposition
2.2
) to obtain sharper bounds; see Section
7.3.2
.

Before starting the concrete calculations, we record the following simple observation that handles the case
t>|\mathcal{U}|
.

Proposition 7.13
.

Suppose
s^{*}
is a real number with
0\leqslant s^{*}\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
. For any nonnegative integers
t
and
\ell
, we have

Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,\ell)\leqslant\max_{r}Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},r,\ell-t+r),

where the maximum is taken over all nonnegative integers
r
such that
(t-\ell)^{+}\leqslant r\leqslant\min\{t,k|\mathcal{U}|\}
.

Proof.

Notice that for any
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
, if we define
z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
by

z^{\prime}_{v}=\begin{cases}z_{v},&\text{if }v\in\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U},\\
0,&\text{if }v\not\in\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U}\end{cases}\qquad\text{for any }v\in\Lambda,

then for any
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}(M)
we have
\|z+[\mathbf{a}]\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell
if and only if
\|z^{\prime}+[\mathbf{a}]\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell-(\|z\|_{\mathsf{H}}-\|z^{\prime}\|_{\mathsf{H}})
. Therefore, in the defining equation (
7.6
) of
Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,\ell)
, we may add the requirement
\mathrm{supp}(z)\subseteq\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U}
to the first maximum and replace the condition
\|z+[\mathbf{a}]\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell
in the sum with the condition
\|z+[\mathbf{a}]\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell-t+\|z\|_{\mathsf{H}}
, without changing the value of the right-hand side. The desired conclusion then immediately follows.
∎

7.3.1
Mass Transfer to the Zero-Weight Coefficient

The case where
\ell=0
can be handled relatively easily, as the following lemma shows.

Lemma 7.14
.

Suppose
s^{*}
is a real number with
0\leqslant s^{*}\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
. For any nonnegative integer
t
, we have

Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,0)\leqslant q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(t,t,0).

Proof.

Note that for any vector
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
and any matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
, there is at most one character index
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}(M)
such that
z+[\mathbf{a}]=0
. Furthermore, by the definition of
\mathcal{X}(M)
, there exists such an
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}(M)
only if every vertex in
\mathrm{supp}(z)
is internal to some edge of
M
(as defined in Definition
7.8
). Equivalently, this happens only if
|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|=t
and
|\mathsf{bd}_{M,z}|=0
(as defined in Definition
7.9
). Therefore, by Definition
7.12
we have

Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,0)\leqslant\max_{z,\mathcal{D}}\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|=t\text{ and }|\mathsf{bd}_{M,z}|=0\big.\right\}\right],

where the maximum is taken over all vectors
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
of Hamming weight
\|z\|_{\mathsf{H}}=t
and all pseudo-uniform distributions
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
. It then follows from Definition
7.10
that
Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,0)\leqslant q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(t,t,0)
.
∎

7.3.2
Applying Hypercontractivity

To handle the cases where
\ell\geqslant 1
, we prepare the following lemma using the level-
d
inequality on the product space
\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
.

Lemma 7.15
.

Fix a matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
, a vector
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
of Hamming weight
\|z\|_{\mathsf{H}}=t
, and a set
A\subseteq\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
of size
|A|\geqslant 2^{-s^{*}}N^{km}
. For any nonnegative integer
\ell
, we have

\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}(M),\;\|z+[\mathbf{a}]\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell}\left|\widehat{\phi_{A}}(\mathbf{a})\right|\leqslant 2^{s^{*}/2}N^{2kt}\cdot\left(\frac{6m}{\ell-t+|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|}\right)^{(\ell-t+|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|)/4}.

(7.7)

If
\ell\leqslant s^{*}
, then we also have

\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}(M),\;\|z+[\mathbf{a}]\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell}\left|\widehat{\phi_{A}}(\mathbf{a})\right|\leqslant N^{2kt}\cdot\left(\frac{8\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{\ell-t+|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|}\right)^{(\ell-t+|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|)/2}.

(7.8)

Proof.

Let
M^{\prime}\subseteq M
be the set of edges of
M
that contains at least one vertex in
\mathrm{supp}(z)
. For any
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}(M^{\prime})
, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}(M),\;\|z+[\mathbf{a}]\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell,\\
\mathbf{a}_{|M^{\prime}}=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\left|\widehat{\phi_{A}}(\mathbf{a})\right|\leqslant\sqrt{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}(M),\;\|z+[\mathbf{a}]\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell,\\
\mathbf{a}_{|M^{\prime}}=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}1}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}(M),\;\|z+[\mathbf{a}]\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell,\\
\mathbf{a}_{|M^{\prime}}=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\left|\widehat{\phi_{A}}(\mathbf{a})\right|^{2}}.

(7.9)

Note that for any
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}(M)
, we have

\|z+[\mathbf{a}]\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\left\|z+[\mathbf{a}_{|M^{\prime}}]\right\|_{\mathsf{H}}+\left\|[\mathbf{a}_{\setminus M^{\prime}}]\right\|_{\mathsf{H}}\geqslant t-\left|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}\right|+2\left\|\mathbf{a}_{\setminus M^{\prime}}\right\|_{\mathsf{H}}.

If
\|z+[\mathbf{a}]\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell
, it follows that

\left\|\mathbf{a}_{\setminus M^{\prime}}\right\|_{\mathsf{H}}\leqslant(\ell-t+|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|)/2.

(7.10)

Therefore,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}(M),\;\|z+[\mathbf{a}]\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell,\\
\mathbf{a}_{|M^{\prime}}=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}1\leqslant\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor(\ell-t+|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|)/2\rfloor}\binom{m}{j}N^{kj}\leqslant\left(\frac{6mN^{k}}{\ell-t+|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|}\right)^{(\ell-t+|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|)/2}.

(7.11)

Similarly, we also have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}(M),\;\|z+[\mathbf{a}]\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell,\\
\mathbf{a}_{|M^{\prime}}=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\left|\widehat{\phi_{A}}(\mathbf{a})\right|^{2}\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{X}(M\setminus M^{\prime})\\
\|\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}\|_{\mathsf{H}}\leqslant(\ell-t+|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|)/2\end{subarray}}\left|\widehat{\phi_{A}}(\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\uplus\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime})\right|^{2}\leqslant\left\|\big(\phi_{A}\cdot\overline{\psi_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\uplus\mathbf{0}}}\big)^{\leqslant(\ell-t+|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|)/2}\right\|_{2}^{2},

(7.12)

Note that

\frac{\left\|\phi_{A}\cdot\overline{\psi_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\uplus\mathbf{0}}}\right\|_{2}}{\left\|\phi_{A}\cdot\overline{\psi_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\uplus\mathbf{0}}}\right\|_{1}}=\frac{\|\phi_{A}\|_{2}}{\|\phi_{A}\|_{1}}=\sqrt{\frac{2^{m}}{|A|}}\leqslant 2^{s^{*}/2}.

If
\ell\leqslant s^{*}
, we have
(\ell-t+|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|)/2\leqslant\ell/2\leqslant\log_{2}(\|\phi_{A}\|_{2}/\|\phi_{A}\|_{1})
, so we may apply Proposition
2.2
to (
7.12
) and get

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}(M),\;\|z+[\mathbf{a}]\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell,\\
\mathbf{a}_{|M^{\prime}}=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\left|\widehat{\phi_{A}}(\mathbf{a})\right|^{2}\leqslant\left(\frac{8N^{k}s^{*}}{\ell-t+|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|}\right)^{(\ell-t+|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|)/2}.

(7.13)

We are now ready to finish the proof. We first establish (
7.7
). Apply (
7.11
) to the first square-root term on the right-hand side of (
7.9
), and bound the second term by
\|\phi_{A}\|_{2}\leqslant 2^{s^{*}/2}
.
Summing the resulting inequality over all
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}(M^{\prime})
immediately yields (
7.7
), since
|\mathcal{X}(M^{\prime})|\leqslant N^{k|M^{\prime}|}\leqslant N^{kt}
.

We next prove (
7.8
) for
\ell\leqslant s^{*}
. As before, we apply (
7.11
) to the first square-root term in (
7.9
). This time, however, we bound the second square-root term using (
7.12
) together with (
7.13
). Summing over all
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}(M^{\prime})
then immediately gives (
7.8
).
∎

7.3.3
Mass Transfer to Low-Weight Coefficients

We can now use (
7.8
) to calculate an upper bound on
Q_{\Lambda}^{\mathcal{U},m}(s^{*},t,\ell)
in the case
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant s^{*}
.

Lemma 7.16
.

Suppose
s^{*}
is a real number with
0\leqslant s^{*}\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
. For nonnegative integers
t
and
\ell
such that
\ell\leqslant s^{*}
, we have

Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,\ell)\leqslant 4\left(\frac{72N^{8k}t}{\sqrt{ms^{*}}}\right)^{t/2}\left(\frac{16\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\left(\frac{16m\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{(t-\ell)^{+}/2}.

Proof.

We first expand the definition (
7.6
) as follows:

Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,\ell)=\max_{z,\mathcal{D}}\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{i,b\geqslant 0}\mathbbm{1}\left\{|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|=i\text{ and }|\mathsf{bd}_{M,z}|=b\right\}\cdot\max_{A}\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}(M),\;\|z+[\mathbf{a}]\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell}\left|\widehat{\phi_{A}}(\mathbf{a})\right|\right],

(7.14)

where the first maximum is taken over all vectors
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
of Hamming weight
\|z\|_{\mathsf{H}}=t
and all pseudo-uniform distributions
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
, while the second maximum taken is over all sets
A\subseteq\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
of size
|A|\geqslant 2^{-s^{*}}N^{km}
. Note that only when
|\mathsf{in}_{M,z}|\geqslant t-\ell
can there exist
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}(M)
such that
\|z+[\mathbf{a}]\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell
(see (
7.10
)). Therefore, applying Definition
7.10
and Lemma
7.15
to (
7.14
) yields

\displaystyle Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,\ell)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=(t-\ell)^{+}}^{+\infty}\sum_{b=0}^{+\infty}q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(t,i,b)\cdot N^{2kt}\left(\frac{8\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{\ell-t+i}\right)^{(\ell-t+i)/2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=(t-\ell)^{+}}^{+\infty}\sum_{b=0}^{+\infty}(24k^{2}N^{2k})^{t}\left(\frac{im}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{i/2}\left(\frac{m}{|\mathcal{U}|}\right)^{b}\left(\frac{8\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{\ell-t+i}\right)^{(\ell-t+i)/2}

(using Proposition
7.11
)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2(24N^{4k})^{t}\sum_{i=(t-\ell)^{+}}^{+\infty}\left(\frac{im}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{i/2}\left(\frac{8\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{\ell-t+i}\right)^{(\ell-t+i)/2}

(using
m/|\mathcal{U}|\leqslant 1/2
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2\left(24N^{4k}\sqrt{t}\right)^{t}}{(\ell/2)^{\ell/2}}\sum_{i=(t-\ell)^{+}}^{+\infty}\left(\frac{2m}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{i/2}\left(8\sqrt{ms^{*}}\right)^{(\ell-t+i)/2}

(using
i^{i}\ell^{\ell}t^{-t}(\ell-t+i)^{-(\ell-t+i)}\leqslant 2^{i+\ell}
, which is due to Proposition
A.2
)

\displaystyle=2\left(\frac{72N^{8k}t}{\sqrt{ms^{*}}}\right)^{t/2}\left(\frac{16\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\sum_{i=(t-\ell)^{+}}^{+\infty}\left(\frac{16m\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{i/2}

(rearranging)

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\left(\frac{72N^{8k}t}{\sqrt{ms^{*}}}\right)^{t/2}\left(\frac{16\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\left(\frac{16m\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{(t-\ell)^{+}/2},

(using
16m\sqrt{ms^{*}}/|\mathcal{U}|^{2}\leqslant 1/4
)

as desired.
∎

7.3.4
Mass Transfer to Intermediate-Weight Coefficients

To handle the case where
\ell>s^{*}
, we will use (
7.7
) instead of (
7.8
).

Lemma 7.17
.

Suppose
m\leqslant(6k)^{-1}|\mathcal{U}|
and
s^{*}
is a real number with
0\leqslant s^{*}\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
. For nonnegative integers
t
and
\ell
such that
\ell>s^{*}
, we have

Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,\ell)\leqslant 4\left(\frac{2^{16}N^{16k}t}{m}\right)^{t/4}\left(\frac{96m}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/4}\left(\frac{12m^{3}t}{|\mathcal{U}|^{3}}\right)^{(t-\ell)^{+}/4}.

Proof.

We first calculate an upper bound for
Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},r,\ell)
, for any nonnegative integers
r
and
\ell
such that
r\leqslant k|\mathcal{U}|
(we do not assume
\ell>s^{*}
for now).

As in the proof of Lemma
7.16
, we apply Definition
7.10
and Lemma
7.15
to (
7.14
). The only difference is that rather than applying the bound (
7.8
) in Lemma
7.15
that works for
\ell\leqslant s^{*}
, this time we apply the bound (
7.7
) which works for all nonnegative integers
\ell
. We get

\displaystyle Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},r,\ell)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=(r-\ell)^{+}}^{r}\sum_{b=0}^{+\infty}q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(r,i,b)\cdot 2^{s^{*}/2}N^{2kr}\left(\frac{6m}{\ell-r+i}\right)^{(\ell-r+i)/4}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{s^{*}/2}\sum_{i=(r-\ell)^{+}}^{r}\sum_{b=0}^{+\infty}(24k^{2}N^{2k})^{r}\left(\frac{im}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{i/2}\left(\frac{m}{|\mathcal{U}|}\right)^{b}\left(\frac{6m}{\ell-r+i}\right)^{(\ell-r+i)/4}

(using Proposition
7.11
)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{s^{*}/2+1}(24N^{4k})^{r}\sum_{i=(r-\ell)^{+}}^{r}\left(\frac{im}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{i/2}\left(\frac{6m}{\ell-r+i}\right)^{(\ell-r+i)/4}

(using
m/|\mathcal{U}|\leqslant 1/2
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2^{s^{*}/2+1}\left(24N^{4k}r^{1/4}\right)^{r}}{(\ell/2)^{\ell/4}}\sum_{i=(r-\ell)^{+}}^{r}\left(\frac{m\sqrt{2i}}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{i/2}(6m)^{(\ell-r+i)/4}

(using
i^{i}\ell^{\ell}r^{-r}(\ell-r+i)^{-(\ell-r+i)}\leqslant 2^{i+\ell}
, which is due to Proposition
A.2
)

\displaystyle=2^{s^{*}/2+1}\left(\frac{2^{11}3^{3}N^{16k}r}{m}\right)^{r/4}\left(\frac{12m}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/4}\sum_{i=(r-\ell)^{+}}^{r}\left(\frac{12m^{3}i}{|\mathcal{U}|^{4}}\right)^{i/4}

(rearranging)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{s^{*}/2+2}\left(\frac{2^{16}N^{16k}r}{m}\right)^{r/4}\left(\frac{12m}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/4}\left(\frac{12m^{3}r}{|\mathcal{U}|^{4}}\right)^{(r-\ell)^{+}/4}.

(using
12m^{3}r/|\mathcal{U}|^{4}\leqslant 1/16
)

Now we give an upper bound for
Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,\ell)
for nonnegative integers
t
and
\ell
such that
\ell>s^{*}
. By Proposition
7.13
and the calculation above, we have

\displaystyle Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,\ell)

\displaystyle\leqslant\max_{r}Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},r,\ell-t+r)

(where
r
ranges over all integers such that
(t-\ell)^{+}\leqslant r\leqslant\min\{k|\mathcal{U}|,t\}
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\max_{r}\left(2^{s^{*}/2+2}\left(\frac{2^{16}N^{16k}r}{m}\right)^{r/4}\left(\frac{12m}{\ell-t+r}\right)^{(\ell-t+r)/4}\left(\frac{12m^{3}r}{|\mathcal{U}|^{4}}\right)^{(t-\ell)^{+}/4}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{s^{*}/2+2}\left(\frac{2^{16}N^{16k}t}{m}\right)^{t/4}\left(\frac{24m}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/4}\left(\frac{12m^{3}t}{|\mathcal{U}|^{4}}\right)^{(t-\ell)^{+}/4}

(using
r^{r}\ell^{\ell}t^{-t}(\ell-t+r)^{-(\ell-t+r)}\leqslant 2^{\ell}
, which is due to Proposition
A.2
)

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\left(\frac{2^{16}N^{16k}t}{m}\right)^{t/4}\left(\frac{96m}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/4}\left(\frac{12m^{3}t}{|\mathcal{U}|^{4}}\right)^{(t-\ell)^{+}/4},

(using
s^{*}\leqslant\ell
)

as desired.
∎

7.4
The Induction Step

In this subsection, we use the upper bounds on “Fourier mass transfer” calculated in Section
7.3
to control the Fourier growth of the product function
f\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]
featured in Lemma
6.22
. In Sections
7.4.1
,
7.4.2
and
7.4.3
, we apply the upper bounds from Sections
7.3.1
,
7.3.3
and
7.3.4
, respectively, to control the Fourier growth of
f\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]
on different levels.

7.4.1
Inductive Bound for the Zero-Weight Coefficient

Lemma 7.18
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
k
-universe embedded in a finite set
\Lambda
. Fix constants
C\in[1,+\infty)
and
\delta_{0},\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
. Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
be parameters satisfying

0<m\leqslant\frac{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}{2^{14}k^{4}N|\Lambda|}\quad\text{and}\quad\log_{2}(4/\delta)\leqslant s^{*}\leqslant(\delta/2)^{4}C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|.

Suppose
f:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
is a
(|\mathcal{U}|,C,s^{*},\delta_{0})
-bounded function. Then we have

\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}\setminus\{0\}}\left|\widehat{f}(z)\right|Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}\big(s^{*},\|z\|_{\mathsf{H}},0\big)\leqslant\delta.

(7.15)

Proof.

The left-hand side of (
7.15
) is at most
S_{1}+S_{2}
, where

S_{1}:=\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor s^{*}\rfloor}Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,0)\cdot\left\|f^{=t}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}},\quad\text{and}\quad S_{2}:=\sum_{t=\lceil s^{*}\rceil}^{|\Lambda|}Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,0)\cdot\left\|f^{=t}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}}.

Applying Lemma
7.14
and Definition
6.15
to
S_{1}
, we can calculate

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t=1}^{+\infty}q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(t,t,0)\cdot F_{C}(|\mathcal{U}|,t,s^{*})

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t=1}^{+\infty}(24k^{2})^{t}\left(\frac{tm}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{t/2}\cdot\left(\frac{C\sqrt{|\mathcal{U}|s^{*}}}{t}\right)^{t/2}

(using Proposition
7.11
and Definition
6.12
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{+\infty}\left(\frac{576k^{4}Cm\sqrt{s^{*}}}{\left|\mathcal{U}\right|^{3/2}}\right)^{t/2}

(rearranging)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\delta^{2}}{16}\right)^{t/2}\leqslant\frac{\delta}{2}.

(using the upper bounds on
m
and
s^{*}
)

Applying Lemma
7.14
and Proposition
6.16
to
S_{2}
, we can calculate

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t=\lceil s^{*}\rceil}^{+\infty}(24k^{2})^{t}\left(\frac{tm}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{t/2}\cdot 2^{s^{*}/2}\left(\frac{3N|\Lambda|}{t}\right)^{t/2}

(using Proposition
7.11
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t=\lceil s^{*}\rceil}^{+\infty}\left(\frac{2^{12}k^{4}Nm|\Lambda|}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{t/2}

(replacing
s^{*}
by
t
)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t=\lceil s^{*}\rceil}^{+\infty}4^{-t/2}\leqslant 2^{-s^{*}+1}\leqslant\frac{\delta}{2}.

(using the upper bound on
m
and the lower bound on
s^{*}
)

Therefore, we have
S_{1}+S_{2}\leqslant\frac{\delta}{2}+\frac{\delta}{2}=\delta
, as desired.
∎

7.4.2
Inductive Bound for Low-Weight Coefficients

Lemma 7.19
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
k
-universe embedded in a finite set
\Lambda
. Fix constants
C\in[1,+\infty)
and
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
. Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
be parameters satisfying

0<m\leqslant\frac{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}{2^{15}N^{8k+1}|\Lambda|}\quad\text{and}\quad 1\leqslant s^{*}\leqslant(2C)^{-2}m.

Suppose
f:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
is a
(|\mathcal{U}|,C,s^{*},\delta)
-bounded function. Then for each integer
\ell
such that
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant s^{*}
, we have

\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}}\left|\widehat{f}(z)\right|Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}\big(s^{*},\|z\|_{\mathsf{H}},\ell\big)\leqslant\left(\frac{2^{22}N^{8k}C\sqrt{|\mathcal{U}|s^{*}}}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}.

(7.16)

Proof.

The left-hand side of (
7.16
) is at most
S_{0}+S_{1}+S_{2}+S_{3}
, where

\displaystyle S_{0}

\displaystyle:=Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},0,\ell)\cdot\left\|f^{=0}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}},\qquad

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor s^{*}\rfloor}Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,\ell)\cdot\left\|f^{=t}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}},

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{t=\lceil s^{*}\rceil}^{\lfloor C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|\rfloor}Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,\ell)\cdot\left\|f^{=t}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}},\qquad

\displaystyle S_{3}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{t=\lceil C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|\rceil}^{|\Lambda|}Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,\ell)\cdot\left\|f^{=t}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}}.

It suffices to show that
S_{0},S_{1},S_{2},S_{3}
are all at most
S:=\left(2^{18}N^{8k}C\sqrt{|\mathcal{U}|s^{*}}/\ell\right)^{\ell/2}
.

Applying Lemma
7.16
and Definition
6.15
to
S_{0}
, we can calculate

S_{0}\leqslant 4\left(\frac{16\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\cdot(1+\delta)\leqslant S.

Applying Lemma
7.16
and Definition
6.15
to
S_{1}
, we can calculate

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t=1}^{+\infty}4\left(\frac{72N^{8k}t}{\sqrt{ms^{*}}}\right)^{t/2}\left(\frac{16\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\left(\frac{16m\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{(t-\ell)^{+}/2}\cdot\left(\frac{C\sqrt{|\mathcal{U}|s^{*}}}{t}\right)^{t/2}

\displaystyle=4\left(\frac{16\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\cdot\sum_{t=1}^{+\infty}\left(72N^{8k}C\sqrt{\frac{|\mathcal{U}|}{m}}\right)^{t/2}\left(\frac{16m\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{(t-\ell)^{+}/2}

(rearranging)

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\left(\frac{2^{11}N^{8k}C\sqrt{|\mathcal{U}|s^{*}}}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\cdot\sum_{t=1}^{+\infty}\left(\frac{2^{11}N^{8k}Cm\sqrt{s^{*}}}{|\mathcal{U}|^{3/2}}\right)^{(t-\ell)^{+}/2}

(using
t\leqslant\ell+(t-\ell)^{+}
)

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\left(\frac{2^{11}N^{8k}C\sqrt{|\mathcal{U}|s^{*}}}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\cdot(\ell+1)\leqslant S.

(using the upper bounds on
m
and
s^{*}
)

Applying Lemma
7.16
and Definition
6.15
to
S_{2}
, we can calculate

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t=\lceil s^{*}\rceil}^{\lfloor C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|\rfloor}4\left(\frac{72N^{8k}t}{\sqrt{ms^{*}}}\right)^{t/2}\left(\frac{16\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\left(\frac{16m\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{(t-\ell)/2}\cdot\left(\frac{C^{2}|\mathcal{U}|}{t}\right)^{t/4}

\displaystyle=4\left(\frac{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}{m\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\cdot\sum_{t=\lceil s^{*}\rceil}^{\lfloor C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|\rfloor}\left(\frac{1152N^{8k}Cm\sqrt{t}}{|\mathcal{U}|^{3/2}}\right)^{t/2}

(rearranging)

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\left(\frac{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}{m\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\left(\frac{2^{11}N^{8k}Cm\sqrt{s^{*}}}{|\mathcal{U}|^{3/2}}\right)^{s^{*}/2}\cdot\sum_{t=\lceil s^{*}\rceil}^{+\infty}\left(\frac{2^{12}N^{8k}m}{|\mathcal{U}|}\right)^{(t-s^{*})/2}

(using
t^{t}\leqslant(s^{*})^{s^{*}}(3t)^{t-s^{*}}
)

\displaystyle\leqslant 8\left(\frac{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}{m\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\left(\frac{2^{11}N^{8k}Cm\sqrt{s^{*}}}{|\mathcal{U}|^{3/2}}\right)^{\ell/2}\leqslant S.

(using
\ell\leqslant s^{*}
and the upper bound on
m
)

Applying Lemma
7.16
and Proposition
6.16
to
S_{3}
, we can calculate

\displaystyle S_{3}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t=\lceil C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|\rceil}^{|\Lambda|}4\left(\frac{72N^{8k}t}{\sqrt{ms^{*}}}\right)^{t/2}\left(\frac{16\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\left(\frac{16m\sqrt{ms^{*}}}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{(t-\ell)/2}\cdot 2^{s^{*}/2}\left(\frac{3N|\Lambda|}{t}\right)^{t/2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\left(\frac{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}{m\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\cdot\sum_{t=\lceil C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|\rceil}^{|\Lambda|}\left(\frac{2^{13}N^{8k+1}m|\Lambda|}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{t/2}

(rearranging and using
s^{*}\leqslant C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|
)

\displaystyle\leqslant 8\left(\frac{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}{m\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\cdot 4^{-C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|/2}

(using the upper bound on
m
)

\displaystyle\leqslant 8\left(\frac{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}{m\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\left(\frac{C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|}{\ell}\right)^{-\ell}

(using the inequality
2^{-a}\leqslant a^{-1}
for any
a>0
)

\displaystyle=8\left(\frac{C^{2}\ell}{m}\right)^{\ell/2}\leqslant 8\leqslant S.

(using
\ell\leqslant s^{*}\leqslant(2C)^{-2}m
)

In conclusion, we have shown
S_{0},S_{1},S_{2},S_{3}\leqslant S
and thus
S_{0}+S_{1}+S_{2}+S_{3}\leqslant 4S
, as desired.
∎

7.4.3
Inductive Bound for Intermediate-Weight Coefficients

Lemma 7.20
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a
k
-universe embedded in a finite set
\Lambda
. Fix constants
C\in[1,+\infty)
and
\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
. Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
s^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
be parameters satisfying

0<m\leqslant\frac{|\mathcal{U}|^{3}}{2^{18}kN^{8k+2}|\Lambda|^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad 2\leqslant s^{*}\leqslant C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|.

Suppose
f:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
is a
(|\mathcal{U}|,C,s^{*},\delta)
-bounded function. Then for each integer
\ell
such that
s^{*}\leqslant\ell\leqslant(2C)^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|
, we have

\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}}\left|\widehat{f}(z)\right|Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}\big(s^{*},\|z\|_{\mathsf{H}},\ell\big)\leqslant\left(\frac{(2^{22}N^{8k}C)^{2}|\mathcal{U}|}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/4}.

(7.17)

Proof.

The left-hand side of (
7.16
) is at most
S_{0}+S_{1}+S_{2}
, where

\displaystyle S_{0}:=Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},0,\ell)\cdot\left\|f^{=0}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}},

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|\rfloor}Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,\ell)\cdot\left\|f^{=t}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}},

and

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{t=\lceil C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|\rceil}^{|\Lambda|}Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},t,\ell)\cdot\left\|f^{=t}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}}.

It suffices to show that
S_{0},S_{1},S_{2}
are all at most
S:=\left((2^{20}N^{8k}C)^{2}|\mathcal{U}|/\ell\right)^{\ell/4}
.

Applying Lemma
7.17
and Definition
6.15
to
S_{0}
, we can calculate

S_{0}\leqslant 4\left(\frac{96m}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/4}\cdot(1+\delta)\leqslant S.

Applying Lemma
7.17
and Proposition
6.14
to
S_{1}
, we can calculate

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|\rfloor}4\left(\frac{2^{16}N^{16k}t}{m}\right)^{t/4}\left(\frac{96m}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/4}\left(\frac{12m^{3}t}{|\mathcal{U}|^{4}}\right)^{(t-\ell)^{+}/4}\cdot 2^{s^{*}/4}\left(\frac{C^{2}|\mathcal{U}|}{t}\right)^{t/4}

\displaystyle=2^{s^{*}/4+2}\left(\frac{96m}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/4}\cdot\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|\rfloor}\left(2^{8}N^{8k}C\sqrt{\frac{|\mathcal{U}|}{m}}\right)^{t/2}\left(\frac{12m^{3}t}{|\mathcal{U}|^{4}}\right)^{(t-\ell)^{+}/4}

(rearranging)

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\left(\frac{(2^{12}N^{8k}C)^{2}|\mathcal{U}|}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/4}\cdot\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|\rfloor}\left(\frac{(2^{10}N^{8k}C)^{2}m^{2}t}{|\mathcal{U}|^{3}}\right)^{(t-\ell)^{+}/4}

(using
s^{*}\leqslant\ell
and
t\leqslant\ell+(t-\ell)^{+}
)

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\left(\frac{(2^{12}N^{8k}C)^{2}|\mathcal{U}|}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/4}\cdot(\ell+1)\leqslant S.

(using the upper bound on
m
)

Applying Lemma
7.17
and Proposition
6.16
to
S_{2}
, we can calculate

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t=\lceil C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|\rceil}^{|\Lambda|}4\left(\frac{2^{16}N^{16k}t}{m}\right)^{t/4}\left(\frac{96m}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/4}\left(\frac{12m^{3}t}{|\mathcal{U}|^{4}}\right)^{(t-\ell)/4}\cdot 2^{s^{*}/2}\left(\frac{3N|\Lambda|}{t}\right)^{t/2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\left(\frac{8|\mathcal{U}|^{4}}{m^{2}\ell}\right)^{\ell/4}\cdot\sum_{t=\lceil C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|\rceil}^{|\Lambda|}t^{-\ell/4}\left(\frac{2^{16}N^{8k+1}m|\Lambda|}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{t/2}

(using
s^{*}\leqslant C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|
and rearranging)

\displaystyle\leqslant 4\left(\frac{8C^{2}|\mathcal{U}|^{3}}{m^{2}\ell}\right)^{\ell/4}\cdot\sum_{t=\lceil C^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|\rceil}^{|\Lambda|}\left(\frac{2^{16}N^{8k+1}m|\Lambda|}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{t/2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 8\left(\frac{8C^{2}|\mathcal{U}|^{3}}{m^{2}\ell}\right)^{\ell/4}\left(\frac{2^{16}N^{8k+1}m|\Lambda|}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{\ell}

(using the upper bound on
m
and
\ell\leqslant(2C)^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|
)

\displaystyle=8\left(\frac{8C^{2}|\mathcal{U}|}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/4}\left(\frac{2^{16}N^{8k+1}\sqrt{m}|\Lambda|}{|\mathcal{U}|^{3/2}}\right)^{\ell}

(rearranging)

\displaystyle\leqslant 8\left(\frac{(2^{16}N^{8k}C)^{2}|\mathcal{U}|}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/4}\leqslant S.

(using the upper bound on
m
and rearranging)

In conclusion, we have shown
S_{0},S_{1},S_{2}\leqslant S
and thus
S_{0}+S_{1}+S_{2}\leqslant 3S
, as desired.
∎

7.4.4
Finishing the Proof

We are now ready to finish the proof of the induction lemma, restated below.

See
6.22

Proof.

For any fixed matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
, subset
A\subseteq\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
, one-wise independent distribution
\mu
over
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
, function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda})
and nonnegative integer
\ell
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\left(f\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]\right)^{=\ell}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}}\left(\left|\widehat{f}(z)\right|\sum_{b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda},\;\|z+b\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell}\left|\left\langle\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}],\chi_{b}\right\rangle\right|\right)

(by the convolution theorem)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}}\left(\left|\widehat{f}(z)\right|\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}(M),\;\|z+[\mathbf{a}]\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell}\left|\widehat{\phi_{A}}(\mathbf{a})\right|\right).

(using Lemma
7.7
)

Taking maximum over all
A
with
|A|\geqslant 2^{-s^{*}}N^{km}
, maximum over all one-wise independent
\mu
, and expectation over
M\sim\mathcal{D}
, we get (using Definition
7.12
)

\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\max_{A,\,\mu}\left\|\left(f\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]\right)^{=\ell}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}}\right]\leqslant\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}}\left|\widehat{f}(z)\right|Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},\|z\|_{\mathsf{H}},\ell).

(7.18)

Now suppose
f:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
is a
(|\mathcal{U}|,C,s^{*},\delta_{0})
-bounded function. Taking
\ell=0
and substituting
(f-1)
for
f
in (
7.18
) yields

\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\max_{A,\,\mu}\Big|\mathbb{E}\left[(f-\mathbb{E}\left[f\right])\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]\right]\Big|\right]\leqslant\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}\setminus\{0\}}\left|\widehat{f}(z)\right|Q^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\Lambda}(s^{*},\|z\|_{\mathsf{H}},0)\leqslant\delta,

where we used Lemma
7.18
in the last transition. Since the function
\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]
has expected value 1 by Proposition
6.9
, this means

\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\max_{A,\,\mu}\Big|\mathbb{E}\left[f\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[f\right]\Big|\right]\leqslant\delta.

Noting that
|\mathbb{E}\left[f\right]-1|\leqslant\delta_{0}
(due to the
(|\mathcal{U}|,C,s^{*},\delta_{0})
-boundedness assumption on
f
) and using Markov’s inequality, we obtain

\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\max_{A,\,\mu}\Big|\mathbb{E}\left[f\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]\right]-1\Big|\geqslant\delta_{0}+\eta^{-1}\delta\right]\leqslant\eta.

(7.19)

For any integer
\ell
such that
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant s^{*}
, combining (
7.18
) with Lemma
7.19
yields

\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\max_{A,\,\mu}\left\|\left(f\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]\right)^{=\ell}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}}\right]\leqslant\left(\frac{2^{22}N^{8k}C\sqrt{|\mathcal{U}|s^{*}}}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}=F_{2^{22}N^{8k}C}\big(|\mathcal{U}|,\ell,s^{*}\big),

so by Markov’s inequality and Proposition
6.13
we have

\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\max_{A,\,\mu}\left\|\left(f\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]\right)^{=\ell}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}}\geqslant F_{2^{22}\eta^{-2}N^{8k}C}\big(|\mathcal{U}|,\ell,2s^{*}\big)\right]\leqslant(\eta^{2})^{\ell/2}=\eta^{\ell}.

(7.20)

For any integer
\ell
such that
s^{*}\leqslant\ell\leqslant(2C)^{-2}|\mathcal{U}|
, combining (
7.18
) and Lemma
7.20
yields

\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\max_{A,\,\mu}\left\|\left(f\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]\right)^{=\ell}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}}\right]\leqslant\left(\frac{(2^{22}N^{8k}C)^{2}|\mathcal{U}|}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/4}=F_{2^{22}N^{8k}C}\big(|\mathcal{U}|,\ell,s^{*}\big),

so by Markov’s inequality and Proposition
6.13
we have

\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\max_{A,\,\mu}\left\|\left(f\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]\right)^{=\ell}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}}\geqslant F_{2^{22}\eta^{-2}N^{8k}C}\big(|\mathcal{U}|,\ell,2s^{*}\big)\right]\leqslant(\eta^{4})^{\ell/4}=\eta^{\ell}.

(7.21)

Finally, noting that
\|f\|_{\infty}\leqslant 2^{s^{*}}
(due to the
(|\mathcal{U}|,C,s^{*},\delta_{0})
-boundedness assumption on
f
) and using Proposition
6.8
, we have
21
21

21

Here we need to recall that the maximum is taken over all
A\subseteq\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
such that
|A|\geqslant 2^{-s^{*}}N^{km}
, for which
\|\phi_{A}\|_{\infty}=|A|^{-1}N^{km}\leqslant 2^{s^{*}}
.

\underset{M\sim\mathcal{D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\max_{A,\,\mu}\left\|f\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant 2^{2s^{*}}\right]=1.

(7.22)

Combining (
7.19
), (
7.20
), (
7.21
) and (
7.22
) by union bound, we conclude that with probability at least

1-\eta-\sum_{\ell=1}^{+\infty}\eta^{\ell}\geqslant 1-3\eta

over
M\sim\mathcal{D}
, any one-wise independent distribution
\mu
and any
A\subseteq\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
with
|A|\geqslant 2^{-s^{*}}N^{km}
satisfy:

\text{the function }f\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\Lambda,M}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]\text{ is }\Big(|\mathcal{U}|,\,2^{22}\eta^{-2}N^{8k}C,\,2s^{*},\,\delta_{0}+\eta^{-1}\delta\Big)\text{-bounded.}\qed

8
The Two-Wise Independent Case

In Sections
4
to
7
, we have shown that given a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
and a sufficiently small constant
\alpha
, the communication game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
has communication complexity at least
\Omega(\sqrt{n})
. The assumption that the distributions
\mu_{\mathsf{e}}
(for
\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}
) are one-wise independent has played a crucial role in the proof (see e.g. the proofs of Lemmas
6.19
and
7.7
). In this section, we show that if the distributions
\mu_{\mathsf{e}}
are further assumed to be
two-wise independent
, then the communication lower bound for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
can be improved from
\Omega(\sqrt{n})
to
\Omega(n)
.

Theorem 8.1
.

Fix a independent distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
, an integer
K>0
and a parameter
\alpha\in\big(0,2^{-20}N^{-10k}|\mathcal{V}|^{-2}\big]
. If
\mu_{\mathsf{e}}
is two-wise independent for any
\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}
, then there exists a constant
\gamma=\gamma(G,\alpha,K)>0
such that
\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)\geqslant\gamma n
.

The proof of Theorem
8.1
follows the same approach as the proof of Theorem
3.14
in Sections
4
to
7
. Given a low-cost communication protocol for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
, the plan is to first decompose the joint input space into structured rectangles satisfying certain conditions, and then prove discrepancy bounds for these rectangles. For Theorem
8.1
, the decomposition step is carried out in Section
8.1
, followed by the analysis of the discrepancy in Section
8.2
. In Section
8.3
, we prove Thoerems
1.11
and
1.12
using Theorem
8.1
.

8.1
Decomposition into “Fair” Rectangles

Throughout this subsection, we fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}]})
and a communication game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
.

Lemma
4.13
, we showed that the rectangle decomposition induced by any protocol
\Pi
with
|\Pi|=o(\sqrt{n})
can be refined into “good” structured rectangles.
For a structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
to be deemed “good,” the restriction sequence
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
must be acyclic (see Definition
4.12
).
Informally, this reflects the fact that a communication protocol with
o(\sqrt{n})
communication cost is incapable of detecting cycles in the hypergraph formed by the joint input.

This phenomenon no longer holds if the communication bound is relaxed from
o(\sqrt{n})
to
o(n)
(see e.g. Theorem
1.10
).
Consequently, to obtain an analogous decomposition result for protocols with
o(n)
communication cost, we must correspondingly weaken the notion of goodness imposed on the structured rectangles.

To this end, we seek a relaxation of acyclicity (cf. Definition
4.11
) for restriction sequences
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
and their associated hypergraphs
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
. Recall from Section
2.1
that a
k
-uniform hypergraph is said to be acyclic if any
\ell
edges together cover more than
\ell(k-1)
vertices. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 8.2
.

A
k
-uniform hypergraph
H
is said to be
C
-locally-almost-acyclic
, where
C
is a positive integer, if for any nonnegative integer
\ell\leqslant C
, any
\ell
distinct edges in
H
together cover at least
\ell(k-1.1)
vertices. If
H
is
C
-locally-almost-acyclic for any
C\geqslant 1
, we simply say that
H
is
almost-acyclic
.

In Definition
4.10
, we defined a hypergraph
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
for any restriction sequence
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
. For notational convenience in this subsection, we will use the same notation for a general sequence of labeled matchings.

Notation 8.3
.

Given a sequence
\mathbf{Z}=(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
of labeled matchings, where
\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\leqslant\alpha n}
for any
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
, we let
H_{\mathbf{Z}}
denote the hypergraph with vertex set
\mathcal{V}\times[n]
and edge set

\bigcup_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}).

Definition 8.4
.

A sequence of labeled matchings
\mathbf{Z}=(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
is said to be
C
-locally-almost-acyclic (respectively, almost-acyclic) if the edge sets
\big(\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\big)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
are pairwise disjoint, and the hypergraph
H_{\mathbf{Z}}
is
C
-locally-almost-acyclic (respectively, almost-acyclic).

We can now state the relaxed version of the goodness notion in Definition
4.12
, by replacing acyclicity with almost-acyclicity.

Definition 8.5
.

Let
W
be a positive real number. We say a structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
, where
R=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
, is
W
-fair
if the following conditions hold:

(1)

The restriction sequence
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is almost-acyclic.

(2)

\sum_{(\mathsf{e},j)}\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\right|\leqslant W
.

(3)

\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right|/\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\right|\geqslant 2^{-W}
for all
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
.

The remaining goal of this subsection is to prove that the rectangle decomposition induced by any protocol
\Pi
with
|\Pi|=o(n)
can be refined into “fair” structured rectangles. The proof of this decomposition lemma turns out to be much simpler than its counterpart Lemma
4.13
(which is proved in
[
14
, Appendix A]
). The main reason this decomposition lemma is simpler is the fact that, roughly speaking, a “random regular hypergraph” is with high probability locally-almost-acyclic, as we formlize below.

Recall that in the
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
game, each player
(\mathsf{e},j)
receives a labeled matching
\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
. In both the yes case and the no case, the support of
\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
is a uniformly random matching
M^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
(independent from the other players). For any matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
and any vertex set
V\subseteq\mathcal{V}\times[n]
, we let
M[V]
denote the set of hyperedges
(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})\in M
such that
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\in V
. The following lemma is a slightly extended version of
[
5
, Lemma 6]
:

Lemma 8.6
.

Fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
, an integer
K>0
and a parameter
\alpha\in(0,1]
.
22
22

22

In this lemma, one can without loss of generality assume
\alpha=1
.
For any constant
p>\frac{1}{k-1}
, there exists a constant
\delta\in(0,1)
such that the following holds: if
M^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
is sampled independently and uniformly from
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
for each player
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
, then the condition

\sum_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\left|M^{(\mathsf{e},j)}[V]\right|\leqslant p|V|\qquad\text{for all }V\subseteq\mathcal{V}\times[n]\text{ with }|V|\leqslant\delta n

(8.1)

is satisfied with probability
1-o(1)
, where
o(1)
denotes a term tending to 0 as
n\rightarrow+\infty
.

Proof.

For a fixed
V\subseteq\mathcal{V}\times[n]
such that
|V|\leqslant\delta n
, let
S_{V}
be the set of tuples
(v,\mathsf{e},j)\in V\times\mathcal{E}\times[K]
such that some edge
(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})\in M^{(\mathsf{e},j)}[V]
has
v
as its first vertex, i.e.
v_{1}=v
. We therefore have

|S_{V}|=\sum_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\left|M^{(\mathsf{e},j)}[V]\right|.

For any fixed subset
S\subseteq V\times\mathcal{E}\times[K]
, it is not hard to see that when the random matchings
M^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
are independently sampled, we have

\mathbb{P}\left[S\subseteq S_{V}\right]\leqslant\left(\frac{|V|^{k-1}}{n^{k-1}}\right)^{|S|}.

Therefore we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[|S_{V}|\geqslant p|V|\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq V\times\mathcal{E}\times[K]\\
|S|=\lceil p|V|\rceil\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\left[S\subseteq S_{V}\right]\leqslant\binom{K|\mathcal{E}|\cdot|V|}{\lceil p|V|\rceil}\left(\frac{|V|}{n}\right)^{(k-1)\lceil p|V|\rceil}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{3K|\mathcal{E}|}{p}\right)^{p|V|}\left(\frac{|V|}{n}\right)^{(k-1)p|V|}=\left(C_{1}\cdot\frac{|V|^{(k-1)p}}{n^{(k-1)p}}\right)^{|V|},

(8.2)

where we used
|V|\leqslant n
in the third transition and denoted
C_{1}=(3K|\mathcal{E}|/p)^{p}
.

Let
C_{2}=3C_{1}|\mathcal{V}|
and pick
\delta\in(0,1)
such that
C_{2}\cdot\delta^{(k-1)p-1}\leqslant 1/2
. Applying union bound over all
V\subseteq\mathcal{V}\times[n]
with
|V|\leqslant\delta n
to (
8.2
), we get

\mathbb{P}\left[\text{condition \eqref{eq:locally_sparse} fails}\right]\leqslant\sum_{r=1}^{\lfloor\delta n\rfloor}\binom{n|\mathcal{V}|}{r}\left(C_{1}\cdot\frac{r^{(k-1)p}}{n^{(k-1)p}}\right)^{r}\leqslant\sum_{r=1}^{\lfloor\delta n\rfloor}\left(C_{2}\cdot\frac{r^{(k-1)p-1}}{n^{(k-1)p-1}}\right)^{r}\leqslant o(1).\qed

Lemma
8.6
immediately implies the following corollary.

Corollary 8.7
.

Fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
, an integer
K>0
and a parameter
\alpha\in(0,1]
. There exists a constant
\delta\in(0,1)
such that

\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\mathbf{Y}\text{ is }\delta n\text{-locally-almost-acyclic}\right]=1-o(1).

Proof.

It suffices to note that if two of the matchings
M^{(\mathsf{e},j)}=\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})
, where
(\mathsf{e},j)
ranges in
\mathcal{E}\times[K]
, have a common edge
(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})
, then condition (
8.1
) is violated for
p=1/(k-1.1)
and
V=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
.
∎

We are now ready to state and prove the decomposition lemma needed for Theorem
8.1
.

Lemma 8.8
.

Fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
, an integer
K>0
and a parameter
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Assume
k\geqslant 3
. Then there exists a constant
\eta\in(0,1)
such that given any communication protocol
\Pi
for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
with
|\Pi|\leqslant\eta\cdot n
, there exists a collection
\mathcal{R}
of pairwise-disjoint structured rectangles
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
in the space
\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
such that the following conditions hold:

(1)

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}\left(\bigcup_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}}R\right)\geqslant 0.99
.

(2)

Each
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}
is
(10^{3}|\Pi|)
-fair.

(3)

For each
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}
, there exists
a_{R}\in\{0,1\}
such that
\Pi(\mathbf{Y})=a_{R}
for every
\mathbf{Y}\in R
.

Proof.

By transforming the protocol
\Pi
into a global protocol
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
and taking the leaf rectangles of
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
, it is shown (implicitly) in
[
14
, Proof of Lemma A.8]
that the space
\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
can be partitioned into a collection
\mathcal{R}^{\textup{leaf}}
of structured rectangles such that:

•

\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{\textup{leaf}}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\cdot\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\leqslant 3|\Pi|
(the potential function
\phi
is defined in Definition
5.1
).

•

For each
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{\textup{leaf}}
, there exists
a_{R}\in\{0,1\}
such that
\Pi(\mathbf{Y})=a_{R}
for every
\mathbf{Y}\in R
.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{\textup{leaf}}
be the sub-collection of structured rectangles
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
such that
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is not almost-acyclic, and let
\mathcal{R}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{\textup{leaf}}
be the collection of those violating the second or third conditions of
(10^{3}|\Pi|)
-fairness (as per Definition
8.5
). Using the definition of
\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
, we know that
\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\geqslant 10^{3}|\Pi|
for any
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}_{2}
. Therefore, by Markov’s inequality we have

\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}_{2}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\leqslant\frac{1}{10^{3}|\Pi|}\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{\textup{leaf}}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\cdot\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\leqslant 3\cdot 10^{-3}.

It suffices to show that if
\eta\in(0,1)
is chosen to be small enough, we have

\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}_{1}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{2}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\leqslant 10^{-3}.

(8.3)

Once we have that, the collection
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}^{\textup{leaf}}\setminus(\mathcal{R}_{1}\cup\mathcal{R}_{2})
would satisfy the three conditions in this lemma.

For any
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{\textup{leaf}}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{2}
, since the second condition of
(10^{3}|\Pi|)
-fairness is satisfied, we know that
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
has at most
10^{3}|\Pi|
edges. Furthermore, for any
\mathbf{Y}\in R
, we know that
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
is a subgraph of
H_{\mathbf{Y}}
. Therefore, if
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is not almost-acyclic, then any
\mathbf{Y}\in R
is not
(10^{3}|\Pi|)
-locally-almost-acyclic. From Corollary
8.7
we know that if the constant
\eta
is chosen to be small enough, then

\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\mathbf{Y}\text{ is not }(10^{3}|\Pi|)\text{-locally-almost-acyclic}\right]=o(1),

and hence

\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{\textup{leaf}}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{2}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\boldsymbol{\zeta}\text{ is not locally-acyclic}\right\}\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\leqslant o(1).

This clearly implies the desired inequality (
8.3
).
∎

8.2
Discrepancy of “Fair” Rectangles

The reason we were content with decomposing into “fair” structured rectangles (instead of requiring “good” ones) in Section
8.1
is that when the distributions
\mu_{\mathsf{e}}
in
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
are two-wise independent, we can prove a discrepancy bound even for fair rectangles.

Lemma 8.9
.

Fix a two-wise independent distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
, an integer
K>0
and a parameter
\alpha\in\left(0,2^{-20}N^{-10k}|\mathcal{V}|^{-2}\right]
. There exists a constant
\gamma\in(0,1)
such that for any
\gamma n
-fair structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)\geqslant(1-10^{-3})\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R).

As is the case with Lemma
4.15
, the proof of Lemma
8.9
proceeds by handling the “structured information” and “pseudorandom noise” in
R
separately. It turns out that the psueodorandom part in Lemma
8.9
can be handled in essentially the same way as in Section
6.5
, while the structured part requires a different treatment from its counterpart in Section
6.4
. In Section
8.2.1
, we prove a lemma (resembling Lemma
6.21
) that handles the structured part of Lemma
8.9
; the proofs of Lemma
8.9
and Theorem
8.1
can then be finished rather quickly in Section
8.2.2
.

8.2.1
Fourier Growth of The Structured Part

Recall that in Section
6.4
, in order to analyze the “structured-part function”
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
(defined in Definition
6.18
), a key step is to answer a purely combinatorial question: in a hypergraph, how many vertex subsets (of a given size) intersect each connected component in at least 2 vertices? It turns out that under the new set of assumptions stated in Lemma
8.9
, the analysis of the same function
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
hinges on a different set of combinatorial questions about hypergraphs.

In a large hypergraph
H=(V,E)
, if a subset
E^{\prime}\subseteq E
of
r
edges is chosen uniformly at random, most likely they will not be incident to each other. Our first question is, for how many size-
r
subset
E^{\prime}\subseteq E
does the subgraph
(V,E^{\prime})
have only a small number of connected components? We answer this question in Lemma
8.11
by assuming a degree bound on the original hypergraph
H
.

Definition 8.10
.

Let
t,r
be positive integers such that
r\geqslant t
, and let
H=(V,E)
be a
k
-uniform hypergraph. We use
\mathfrak{E}(t,r,H)
to denote the collection of subsets
E^{\prime}\subseteq E
such that
|E^{\prime}|=r
and the number of nontrivial connected components of the subgraph
(V,E^{\prime})
is
t
.

Lemma 8.11
.

Let
d,t,r
be positive integers such that
r\geqslant t
. Let
H
be a
k
-uniform hypergraph with
m
edges, and suppose that every edge in
H
is incident to at most
d
other edges. Then we have
|\mathfrak{E}(t,r,H)|\leqslant(6d)^{r}(3m/t)^{t}
.

Proof.

A sequence of
t
connected subgraphs of
H
with
r
edges in total can be chosen by the following process:

1.

First choose positive integers
c_{1},\dots,c_{t}
such that
c_{1}+\dots+c_{t}=r
. The number of such tuples
(c_{1},\dots,c_{t})
is
\binom{r-1}{t-1}\leqslant 2^{r}
.

2.

Then for each
i\in[t]
, choose a connected subgraph of
H
with
c_{i}
edges. The number of such connected subgraphs is at most
m(3d)^{c_{i}}
, by standard results in enumerative combinatorics.
23
23

23

For example, one can first transit to the line graph of
H
and then use
[
6
, Lemma 2.1(c)]
.

The total number of choices in this step is thus at most
m^{t}(3d)^{c_{1}+\dots+c_{t}}=m^{t}(3d)^{r}
.

Since each member of the collection
\mathfrak{E}(t,r,H)
is counted
t!
times by this method, we have

|\mathfrak{E}(t,r,H)|\leqslant\frac{2^{r}\cdot m^{t}(3d)^{r}}{t!}\leqslant(6d)^{r}\left(\frac{3m}{t}\right)^{t}.\qed

While in Section
6.4
, the number of nonzero Fourier coefficients of the function
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
was controlled by the number of ways to choose at least 2 vertices from each connected component of a hypergraph, the latter quantity is unaffordable as an upper bound in the current context. In order to utilize the two-wise independence, we will associate the nonzero Fourier coefficients of
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
with a slightly more complicated combinatorial object: the collection
\mathcal{B}(E)
defined in the next definition.

Definition 8.12
.

Suppose
(V,E)
is a
k
-uniform hypergraph. We define
\mathcal{B}(E)
to be the collection of maps
B:E\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{V}
such that for each
e=(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})\in E
, we have

B(e)\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{V},\qquad\mathrm{supp}(B(e))\subseteq\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\left|\mathrm{supp}(B(e))\right|=\|B(e)\|_{\mathsf{H}}\geqslant 3.

For a map
B\in\mathcal{B}(E)
, we denote
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}B:=\sum_{e\in E}B(e)
. For any nonnegative integer
\ell
, we additionally define

\mathcal{B}_{\ell}(E)=\big\{B\in\mathcal{B}(E)\,\big|\,\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}B\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell\big\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{V}.

Before explaining how
Definition

8.12
is related to the nonzero Fourier coefficients of
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
, we first prove some basic facts about the collection
\mathcal{B}(E)
.

Lemma 8.13
.

Suppose
(V,E)
is an almost-acyclic
k
-uniform hypergraph. Let
t
be the number of nontrivial connected components of the hypergraph
(V,E)
, and let
r=|E|
. Then for any
B\in\mathcal{B}(E)
, we have

\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}B\|_{\mathsf{H}}\geqslant\max\left\{2t,\frac{4}{5}r\right\}.

Proof.

Let
V_{1},\dots,V_{t}
be the vertex sets of the nontrivial connected components of
(V,E)
, and let
E_{1},\dots,E_{t}
be the edge sets they induce. For each
i\in[t]
, we write
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}B:=\sum\nolimits_{e\in E_{i}}B(e).
Since
V_{1},\dots,V_{t}
are pairwise disjoint and
\mathrm{supp}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}B)\subseteq V_{i}
, we have

\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}B\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}B\right\rVert_{\mathsf{H}}.

Therefore, it suffices to show for each
i\in[t]
that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}B\right\rVert_{\mathsf{H}}\geqslant\max\left\{2,\frac{4}{5}|E_{i}|\right\}.

(8.4)

If
E_{i}
consists of a single edge
e
, then by definition we have
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}B\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\|B(e)\|_{\mathsf{H}}\geqslant 3
. In the rest of the proof, we fix an
i\in[t]
and assume
|E_{i}|\geqslant 2
. Our goal is to show that
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}B\|_{\mathsf{H}}\geqslant\frac{4}{5}|E_{i}|
. Once we have that, it also follows that
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}B\|_{\mathsf{H}}\geqslant\frac{4}{5}\cdot 2=1.6
and hence
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}B\|_{\mathsf{H}}\geqslant 2
.

We define
V_{i}^{=1}\subseteq V_{i}
to be the set of vertices with degree-1 in the graph
(V_{i},E_{i})
. For
v\in V_{i}
, we use
\mathrm{deg}_{i}(v)
to denote the degree of vertex
v
in the graph
(V_{i},E_{i})
. Since every vertex
v\in V_{i}\setminus V_{i}^{=1}
must satisfy
\mathrm{deg}_{i}(v)\geqslant 2
,

\displaystyle k|E_{i}|=\sum_{v\in V_{i}}\deg_{i}(v)\geqslant\left|V_{i}^{=1}\right|+2\left|V_{i}\setminus V_{i}^{=1}\right|=2|V_{i}|-\left|V_{i}^{=1}\right|.

Using the condition
|V_{i}|\geqslant(k-1.1)|E_{i}|
(which follows from the definition of almost-cyclicity) and rearranging, we get

\displaystyle\left|V_{i}^{=1}\right|\geqslant(k-2.2)|E_{i}|.

(8.5)

For any
v\in V_{i}^{=1}
, there is a unique edge
e\in E_{i}
that contains
v
, so we have
B(e)_{v}=(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}B)_{v}
. This means

V_{i}^{=1}\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}B)\subseteq\bigcup_{e=(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})\in E_{i}}\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}\setminus\mathrm{supp}(B(e)).

Using the condition that
\|B(e)\|_{\mathsf{H}}\geqslant 3
, we get

\left|V^{=1}_{i}\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}B)\right|\leqslant(k-3)|E_{i}|.

Combining this with (
8.5
) yields

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}B\|_{\mathsf{H}}=|\mathrm{supp}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}B)|=\left|V_{i}^{=1}\right|-\left|V^{=1}_{i}\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}B)\right|\geqslant(k-2.2)|E_{i}|-(k-3)|E_{i}|=\frac{4}{5}|E_{i}|,

as desired.
∎

Lemma 8.14
.

Let
d,\ell,m
be positive integers such that
m\geqslant\ell
. Let
H
be an almost-acyclic
k
-uniform hypergraph with
m
edges, and suppose that every edge in
H
is incident to at most
d
other edges. Then we have

\sum_{E^{\prime}\subseteq E}\left|\mathcal{B}_{\ell}(E^{\prime})\right|\leqslant\left(\frac{(15dN^{k})^{4}m}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.13
, we know that for any
E^{\prime}\subseteq E
such that
\mathcal{B}_{\ell}(E^{\prime})
is nonempty, there exist positive integers
t\leqslant\ell/2
and
r\leqslant 5\ell/4
such that
E^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{E}(t,r,H)
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{E^{\prime}\subseteq E}\left|\mathcal{B}_{\ell}(E^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor}\sum_{r=t}^{\lfloor 5\ell/4\rfloor}|\mathfrak{E}(t,r,H)|\cdot\max_{E^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{E}(t,r,H)}\left|\mathcal{B}(E^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor}\sum_{r=t}^{2\ell}(6d)^{r}\left(\frac{3m}{t}\right)^{t}N^{kr}

(using Lemma
8.11
)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\cdot(6dN^{k})^{2\ell}\cdot\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor}\left(\frac{3m}{t}\right)^{t}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\ell\cdot(6dN^{k})^{2\ell}\left(\frac{3m}{\ell/2}\right)^{\ell/2}\leqslant\left(\frac{(15dN^{k})^{4}m}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/2},

(using
m\geqslant\ell
)

as desired.
∎

We can now state and prove the following analogue of Lemma
6.21
.

Lemma 8.15
.

Fix a
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
game, where
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
. Suppose
\mu_{\mathsf{e}}
is two-wise independent for any
\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}
. If
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is an almost-acyclic restriction sequence (as per Definition
8.4
) such that
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
has at most
\gamma n
edges, then the function
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
(defined in Definition
6.18
) is
\big(n,(15kN^{k}K|\mathcal{E}|)^{4},\gamma kn\log_{2}N,0\big)
-bounded.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
satisfies the three conditions of
\big(n,(15kN^{k}K|\mathcal{E}|)^{4},\gamma kn\log_{2}N,0\big)
-boundedness required by Definition
6.15
.

Infinity-norm bound.

Let
E
be the edge set of the hypergraph
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
. For every
e\in E
, define a function
g_{e}\in L^{2}\big(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}\big)
by letting

\displaystyle g_{e}(x)=N^{k}\mu_{\langle e\rangle}\big(x_{|e}-\widetilde{\mathbf{z}}(e)\big),\qquad\text{for all }x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}.

By (
6.6
) we have
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}=\prod_{e\in E}g_{e}
, so

\|g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\prod_{e\in E}\|g_{e}\|_{\infty}\leqslant N^{k|E|}\leqslant N^{\gamma kn}.

Expectation equals
1
.

For each edge
e=(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})\in E
, since
\mu_{\langle e\rangle}
is two-wise independent, we can write

g_{e}(x)=1+\sum_{b^{(e)}}\widehat{g_{e}}(b^{(e)})\cdot\chi_{b^{(e)}}(x),

where the sum is over all
b^{(e)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
such that
\mathrm{supp}(b^{(e)})\subseteq\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
and
\left|\mathrm{supp}(b^{(e)})\right|\geqslant 3
.
We can then expand the product in the identity
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}=\prod_{e\in E}g_{e}
and obtain

g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(x)=\sum_{E^{\prime}\subseteq E}\sum_{B\in\mathcal{B}(E^{\prime})}\left(\chi_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}B}(x)\prod_{e\in E^{\prime}}\widehat{g_{e}}(B(e))\right).

Equivalently, for any
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
we have

\widehat{g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(b)=\sum_{E^{\prime}\subseteq E}\sum_{B\in\mathcal{B}(E^{\prime})}\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}B=b\right\}\cdot\prod_{e\in E^{\prime}}\widehat{g_{e}}(B(e))\right).

(8.6)

For any
E^{\prime}\subseteq E
, the hypergraph
(\mathcal{V}\times[n],E^{\prime})
is almost-acyclic since it is a subgraph of
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
. Therefore, by applying Lemma
8.13
, we know that whenever
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}B=\vec{0}
for some
B\in\mathcal{B}(E^{\prime})
, we must have
E^{\prime}=\emptyset
. It then follows from (
8.6
) that
\widehat{g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(\vec{0})=1
, or equivalently
\mathbb{E}\left[g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\right]=1
.

Fourier growth bound.

For each edge
e\in E
, since
\|g_{e}\|_{1}=1
, we know that
\left|\widehat{g_{e}}(b)\right|\leqslant 1
for any
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
. Therefore, it follows from (
8.6
) that for any positive integer
\ell
, we have

\left\|g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{=\ell}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}}\leqslant\sum_{E^{\prime}\subseteq E}\sum_{B\in\mathcal{B}(E^{\prime})}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}B\|_{\mathsf{H}}=\ell\big.\right\}=\sum_{E^{\prime}\subseteq E}\left|\mathcal{B}_{\ell}(E^{\prime})\right|

Since
E
is the union of
K|\mathcal{E}|
partial matchings, in the hypergraph
H_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}=(\mathcal{V}\times[n],E)
, every vertex is incident to at most
K|\mathcal{E}|
edges. Consequently, every edge is incident to at most
kK|\mathcal{E}|
other edges. We may now apply Lemma
8.14
and obtain

\left\|g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{=\ell}\right\|_{\mathsf{W}}\leqslant\left(\frac{(15kN^{k}K|\mathcal{E}|)^{4}\cdot\gamma n}{\ell}\right)^{\ell/2}\leqslant F_{(15kN^{k}K|\mathcal{E}|)^{4}}\bigl(n,\ell,\gamma^{2}n\bigr)\leqslant F_{(15kN^{k}K|\mathcal{E}|)^{4}}\bigl(n,\ell,\gamma n\log_{2}N\bigr),

where we used Proposition
6.13
in the last transition.
∎

8.2.2
Finishing up the Proofs

As noted earlier, Lemma
8.9
and Theorem
8.1
can now be proved using essentially the same arguments as their counterparts, Lemma
4.15
and Theorem
3.14
.
To avoid unnecessary repetition, we provide only brief proof sketches.

Proof Sketch of Lemma
8.9
.

By the first and second condition of
\gamma n
-fairness, the restriction sequence
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
8.15
. The conclusion of Lemma
8.15
can then be used as the base case for an induction argument similar to the one in the proof of Lemma
4.15
.

More specifically, one can use Lemmas
6.5
and
6.11
to express the ratio
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)/\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)
as an expectation of the form (see (
6.13
))

\underset{(M_{i})_{i=1}^{K|\mathcal{E}|}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{x}{\mathbb{E}}\left[g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(x)\prod_{i=1}^{K|\mathcal{E}|}h_{i,M_{i}}(x)\right]\right],

(8.7)

where each
h_{i,M_{i}}
represents the “pseudorandom part” of the
i
-th player’s messages. Next use Lemma
6.22
to inductively prove the following statement: if
n\gg\gamma^{-1}\gg 1
, then for any
r\in\{0,1,\dots,K|\mathcal{E}|\}
, the function
g_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}h_{i,M_{i}}
is

\Big(n,\,\bigl(15kN^{k}K|\mathcal{E}|\bigr)^{4}\bigl(10^{16}N^{8k}K^{2}|\mathcal{E}|^{2}\bigr)^{r},\,2^{r+1}\gamma n\log_{2}N,\,10^{-4}K^{-1}|\mathcal{E}|^{-1}r\Big)\text{-bounded}

with probability at least
1-4r/10^{4}
over the randomness in
M_{1},\dots,M_{r}
. The
r=K|\mathcal{E}|
case of this statement then yields a lower bound for (
8.7
).
∎

Proof Sketch of Theorem
8.1
.

We apply Lemmas
8.8
and
8.9
to obtain constants
\eta
and
\gamma
, respectively. We show that any protocol
\Pi
for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
with
|\Pi|\leqslant\min\{\eta,10^{-3}\gamma\}\cdot n
must have
\mathrm{adv}(\Pi)<0.1
.

We apply Lemma
8.8
to
\Pi
, and let
\mathcal{R}
be the collection of structured rectangles obtained. By the upper bound on
|\Pi|
and conclusion (2) of Lemma
8.8
, we know that each structured rectangle in
\mathcal{R}
is
\gamma n
-fair. It then follows from Lemma
4.15
that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)\geqslant(1-10^{-3})\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)
for each
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}
. The rest of the proof of identical to the proof of Theorem
3.14
.
∎

8.3
Implications of Two-Wise Independent DHIP

In this subsection, we sketch the proofs of Theorems
1.11
and
1.12
. For the proof of Theorem
1.11
, we need to open up the proof of Lemma
3.19
(given in
[
14
, Proof of Lemma 5.14]
) as a white box.

Proof Sketch of Theorem
1.11
.

Given a constant
\varepsilon^{\prime}\in(0,1)
and predicate family
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\Sigma^{k}\rightarrow\{0,1\}\}
such that every predicate
f\in\mathcal{F}
supports two-wise independence, the proof proceeds as follows:

1.

We can pick an instance
\mathcal{I}\in\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
with
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}<\rho(\mathcal{F})+\varepsilon^{\prime}
(by the definition (
1.2
)). By Proposition
2.4
we know that there is a solution to
{\textsc{BasicLP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
achieving objective value 1 in which the distribution over
\Sigma^{k}
associated to every constraint is two-wise independent.

2.

Then we use Lemma
3.19
(more precisely, the proof of this lemma given in
[
14
]
) with

\varepsilon=\rho(\mathcal{F})+\varepsilon^{\prime}-\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}

to convert
\mathcal{I}
into a
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
communication game. Because of the two-wise independence of the distributions in the
BasicLP
solution, the distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}}))_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}}
provided by the proof of Lemma
3.19
satisfies
N=|\Sigma|
and, importantly, that
\mu_{\mathsf{e}}
is
two-wise
independent for any
\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}
.

By the conclusion of Lemma
3.19
, any
p
-pass streaming algorithm for
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[1,\rho_{\mathcal{F}}+\varepsilon^{\prime}]
must use
(pK|\mathcal{E}|)^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)
bits of memory on instances with
O(n)
variables and
O(n)
constraints. By the two-wise independence of the distributions
\mu_{\mathsf{e}}
(for
\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}
), it follows from Theorem
8.1
that
\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)\geqslant\Omega(n)
. Therefore, any
p
-pass streaming algorithm for
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[1,\rho(\mathcal{F})+\varepsilon^{\prime}]
must use
\Omega(n/p)
bits of memory.
∎

For any predicate family
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\Sigma^{k}\to\{0,1\}\}
of unbounded width, the
\Omega(n)
query lower bound for
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[1,1-\varepsilon]
established in
[
15
]
relies on a known polynomial-time reduction (due to
[
11
]
) from a problem of the form
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})[1,\rho(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})+\varepsilon]
to
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[1,1-\varepsilon]
, where
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
consists of predicates supporting two-wise independence.

As shown in
[
15
, Section 5]
, this reduction can be adapted to the bounded-degree query model. Moreover, it is straightforward to verify that the same reduction extends to the streaming setting, thereby deriving Theorem
1.12
from Theorem
8.1
.

Due to the technical length of the reduction, we omit the full details and provide only a proof sketch of Theorem
1.12
.

Definition 8.16
.

Let
\mathsf{G}
be an Abelian group. For any element
b\in\mathsf{G}
, we let
S^{\mathsf{G}}_{b}:\mathsf{G}^{3}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
be the predicate defined by

S^{\mathsf{G}}_{b}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=1\quad\text{if and only if}\quad x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}=b.

We use
\textsc{3Sum}_{\mathsf{G}}
to denote the predicate family
\left\{S_{b}^{\mathsf{G}}\,\middle|\,b\in\mathsf{G}\right\}
.

Proof Sketch of Theorem
1.12
.

Given a predicate family
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\Sigma^{k}\rightarrow\{0,1\}\}
of unbounded width, the proof proceeds as follows:

1.

We first use the result of
[
11
]
(see also the exposition in
[
15
]
) to obtain an Abelian group
\mathsf{G}
such that the predicate family
\textsc{3Sum}_{\mathsf{G}}
can be “simulated” by
\mathcal{F}
.
24
24

24

For the precise notion of “simulated,” see
[
15
, Definition 2.1]
. Technically, the relational structure
(\mathsf{G},\textsc{3Sum}_{\mathsf{G}})
should be simulated by “the core of
(\Sigma,\mathcal{F})
appended with constant relations.” We refer to
[
15
]
for the details.

2.

Then pick an instance
\mathcal{I}\in\mathrm{CSP}(\textsc{3Sum}_{\mathsf{G}})
with
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}<2/3
.
25
25

25

It is easy to see that such an instance
\mathcal{I}
exist (see e.g.
[
15
, Lemma 4.5]
).
Since every predicate in the family
\textsc{3Sum}_{\mathsf{G}}
supports two-wise independence, by Proposition
2.4
we know that there is a solution to
{\textsc{BasicLP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
achieving objective value 1 in which the distribution over
\Sigma^{k}
associated to every constraint is two-wise independent.

3.

Use Lemma
3.19
(more precisely, the proof of this lemma given in
[
14
]
) with
\varepsilon=2/3-\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}
to convert
\mathcal{I}
into a
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
communication game. Because of the fact that
f^{-1}(1)
is two-wise independent for any
f\in\textsc{3Sum}_{\mathsf{G}}
, the distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}}))_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}}
provided by the proof of Lemma
3.19
satisfies
N=|\mathsf{G}|
and, importantly, that
\mu_{\mathsf{e}}
is
two-wise
independent for any
\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}
.

4.

The proof of Lemma
3.19
also provides a reduction map from the joint input space of
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
to
\mathrm{CSP(\textsc{3Sum}_{\mathsf{G}})}
:

\varphi_{n}:\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}\longrightarrow\mathrm{CSP(\textsc{3Sum}_{\mathsf{G}})},

such that

\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\mathrm{val}_{\varphi_{n}(\mathbf{Y})}=1\right]=1\qquad\text{and}\qquad\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\mathrm{val}_{\varphi_{n}(\mathbf{Y})}\leqslant\frac{2}{3}\right]\geqslant 1-o(1),

where
o(1)
denotes a term tending to 0 as
n\rightarrow+\infty
.

5.

Use
[
15
, Section 5]
to construct a reduction map
\psi_{n}
from the image of
\varphi_{n}
to
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
, such that

\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\mathrm{val}_{\psi_{n}(\varphi_{n}(\mathbf{Y}))}=1\right]=1\qquad\text{and}\qquad\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\mathrm{val}_{\psi_{n}(\varphi_{n}(\mathbf{Y}))}\leqslant 1-\varepsilon^{\prime}\right]\geqslant 1-o(1),

for some constant
\varepsilon^{\prime}\in(0,1)
that does not depend on
n
. The composed reduction map
\psi_{n}\circ\varphi_{n}
satisfies the following:

•

There is a fixed variable set
V_{n}
with size
O(n)
, and a fixed collection
\mathcal{C}_{n}^{(0)}
of constraints on
V_{n}
.

•

For each player
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
, there is a deterministic map that generates from any input
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
a collection of
O(n)
constraints on
V_{n}
.

•

The constraint sequence of the instance
\psi_{n}(\varphi_{n}(\mathbf{Y}))
is the concatenation of
\mathcal{C}^{(0)}_{n}
and the
K|\mathcal{E}|
constraint sequences generated by individual players.

From the last two steps above, we can conclude that for sufficiently large
n
, any
p
-pass streaming algorithm for
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[1,1-\varepsilon^{\prime}]
must use
(pK|\mathcal{E}|)^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)
bits of memory on input instances with
O(n)
variables and
O(n)
constraints. By the two-wise independence of the distributions
\mu_{\mathsf{e}}
(for
\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}
), it follows from Theorem
8.1
that
\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)\geqslant\Omega(n)
. Therefore, any
p
-pass streaming algorithm for
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[1,1-\varepsilon^{\prime}]
must use
\Omega(n/p)
bits of memory.
∎

Appendix A
Some Simple Inequalities

Proposition A.1
.

For any real numbers
x,\ell
such that
x\geqslant 0
and
\ell\geqslant 2
, we have

(1+x)^{\ell}-\ell x\leqslant(1+2\ell x^{2})^{\ell/2}.

Proof.

If
x\geqslant 2/(2\ell-1)
, then
(1+x)^{2}\leqslant 1+2\ell x^{2}
and hence
(1+x)^{\ell}\leqslant(1+2\ell x^{2})^{\ell/2}
.

If
x\leqslant 2/(2\ell-1)
, then by taking second derivatives one can show that
(1+x)^{\ell}\leqslant 1+\ell x+\ell^{2}x^{2}
, and hence
(1+x)^{\ell}-\ell x\leqslant 1+\ell^{2}x^{2}\leqslant(1+2\ell x^{2})^{\ell/2}
.
∎

Proposition A.2
.

For real numbers
a,b,c,d
such that
0<a\leqslant b\leqslant c\leqslant d
and
a+d=b+c
, we have

1\leqslant\frac{a^{a}d^{d}}{b^{b}c^{c}}\leqslant 2^{d}.

Proof.

Let
r=b-a=d-c
. We have

\ln\frac{a^{a}d^{d}}{b^{b}c^{c}}=\ln\frac{d^{d}}{c^{c}}-\ln\frac{b^{b}}{a^{a}}=\int_{a}^{c}\left(\frac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}x}\ln\frac{(x+r)^{x+r}}{x^{x}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{a}^{c}\ln\frac{x+r}{x}\,\mathrm{d}x\geqslant 0,

(A.1)

and therefore
a^{a}d^{d}b^{-b}c^{-c}\geqslant 1
. To obtain an upper bound on
a^{a}d^{d}b^{-b}c^{-c}
, we extend the interval of integral in (
A.1
) from
[a,c]
to
[0,c]
, and then divide it at the point
r
:

\displaystyle\ln\frac{a^{a}d^{d}}{b^{b}c^{c}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{0}^{r}\ln\frac{x+r}{x}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{r}^{c}\ln\frac{x+r}{x}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{0}^{r}\ln\frac{x+r}{x}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{r}^{c}\ln 2\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=r\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\ln\left(\frac{x+1}{x}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x+(c-r)\ln 2

\displaystyle=r\ln 4+(c-r)\ln 2=(c+r)\ln 2=d\ln 2.

Therefore, we have
a^{a}d^{d}b^{-b}c^{-c}\leqslant 2^{d}
, as desired.
∎
